 vi

 Executive Summary
 In 2022, the Heliostat Consortium (HelioCon) published its roadmap report after consultation
 with industrial and research experts from the solar heat for industrial processes (SHIP) and
 concentrating solar power (CSP) industries. This report enumerated the main topic areas and
 technology gaps within each topic area that required attention to progress solar thermal
 technologies—those using heliostat fields in particular—toward cost-effective
 commercialization. The topic areas identified therein were:

 • Techno-economic analysis (TEA)
 • Metrology and standards
 • Components and controls
 • Advanced manufacturing
 • Resources, training, and education (RTE)
 • Field deployment.

 In addition, two special subtopics were identified: wind load and soiling.

 Since its establishment and the publication of the roadmap report, HelioCon has made substantial
 progress toward closing many, but not all, of these gaps. Numerous techno-economic studies
 have been performed, investigating topics ranging from the trade-off between size and
 temperature for SHIP projects to optimization of the heliostat design itself for various
 applications. Significant improvements have been made in optical metrology techniques, with
 first steps toward in situ measurement of heliostat fields. Several standards have been, and
 continue to be, developed with the coordination of an international group of heliostat experts.
 Training programs have been developed, with universities including SHIP and CSP in their
 engineering curricula, and many public webinars have been held to provide broad access to the
 latest heliostat research. Improved heliostat components such as mirror facets and wireless
 communication systems have been developed, and the solar tower at Sandia National
 Laboratories has been upgraded with a testbed for closed-loop controls research and
 development. Field deployment challenges involving heliostat foundations and sensitive wildlife
 habitats have been explored, with progress made toward methods for streamlining project
 development and permitting. Additional knowledge has been added to the body of work on wind
 behavior of heliostats and arrays of heliostats, with progress made toward a holistic
 understanding of wind design methods. Finally, techniques have been developed and
 demonstrated for assessing soiling conditions at a proposed project site, with predictive models
 for the soiling rate showing good results.

 Taking these results together, HelioCon has contributed greatly to the global solar thermal
 research and development effort over the past several years.

 Commercial CSP Deployments and SHIP Opportunity
 HelioCon and related research efforts have made great strides; however, the commercial CSP
 industry (particularly in the United States) has not found similar success. The most notable
 power tower projects in the United States, Crescent Dunes and Ivanpah, have both had varying
 degrees of technical and financial underperformance, and no new power tower plants are planned
 or under construction. In addition, Heliogen, which was a leading developer of heliostats and

 vii

 heliostat control technologies in the United States, has decommissioned their heliostat factory
 and has not publicly progressed their project development activities.

 A noteworthy exception is China. The CSP market there continues to benefit from a combination
 of regulatory support, industrial participation in cost reduction, and economies of scale that are
 unequaled in any other market. Between July 2021 (when data was aggregated for the HelioCon
 roadmap report) and July 2025 (the most current data available at the time of writing), the total
 operational capacity of CSP projects in the United States decreased slightly from 1,731 MW to
 1,422 MW, with none under construction. During that same time, the total operational and under-
 construction projects in China increased from 881 MW to 4,285 MW, representing 40% of the
 total global CSP market.

 Figure 1. Cumulative CSP deployment over time, by country/region.

 While development of CSP projects has proven to be quite challenging, particularly while
 competing with rapidly declining costs for utility scale photovoltaics (PV) and battery energy
 storage system (BESS) projects, there has been a renewed interest in using concentrating solar
 thermal technologies for SHIP applications instead. This is driven both by the need for stable
 thermal energy delivery, placing value on the thermal energy storage (TES) typically integrated
 in SHIP projects, and by the relatively challenging economics of using PV and BESS for process
 heat. Experts consulted during the writing of this report, including those from industry, research,
 and policy, anticipate an increased emphasis on SHIP over CSP for research and deployment in
 the U.S. over the next 5-10 years or more.

 viii

 The power level and temperature requirements vary widely for SHIP applications, and these
 factors can help map onto the most appropriate solar collector type. Generally speaking, line-
 focusing collectors (parabolic troughs and linear Fresnel) are appropriate for process
 temperatures up to approximately 400 C and capable of power levels from megawatts to
 hundreds of megawatts. Point focusing systems (heliostat fields and parabolic dish
 concentrators), meanwhile, are more appropriate for high temperature processes above 400 C and
 potentially beyond 1000 C. Heliostat fields are capable of power from megawatts to hundreds of
 megawatts, while parabolic dishes are generally sized for tens of kilowatts of power.

 Combined heat and power (CHP), in which both electricity and process heat are delivered to the
 end user, is a notable subcategory of SHIP applications. Such systems are common in industrial
 use (typically fossil fuel fired) due to their attractive economics and high efficiency. For solar
 thermal collectors and TES capable of relatively high temperatures, such as those utilizing
 heliostat fields and molten salt storage, CHP is a good technological fit and may be a sound
 economic choice for industrial plants requiring both electricity and process heat.

 HelioCon Goal and Updated Priorities
 In light of the research progress made and the bifurcation in the commercial CSP market
 described above, a revised goal for HelioCon has been formulated:

 HelioCon should focus on eliminating the commercial barriers that prevent heliostat field
 deployments and reducing the uncertainty around the cost, performance, and reliability of
 heliostat fields.

 We believe this will be the most effective way to align HelioCon’s work with the current needs
 of the nascent solar thermal industry in the United States. By focusing on the immediate
 commercial challenges facing SHIP and CSP technologies and project developers, HelioCon can
 improve the success rate of project development efforts securing financing and reaching
 commercial operation. With successful projects being deployed, SHIP and CSP projects will
 attract more industrial interest, creating a virtuous cycle of development, optimization, and scale,
 as we have seen develop in China over the past 5–10 years.

 The following subsections provide summaries of key insights and updated priorities within each
 topic area.

 Techno-Economic Analysis

 1. Accessibility of the tools and metrics developed in the TEA task should be a priority
 moving forward.
 2. There is a general concern that techno-economic models for SHIP and CSP like the
 System Advisor Model™ (SAM) currently overestimate performance when using default
 input assumptions.
 3. Interactions with the rest of the system are important to consider in future analysis.

 ix

 4. The benefits of reducing the real and perceived risk of SHIP and CSP plants may
 outweigh the benefits of adding performance improvements or cost reductions.

 1. Explore further enhancements to performance models and validate the performance
 forecasting framework, including integration of aiming strategy.
 2. Publish the scripts and software libraries used to develop the analyses in this task into a
 publicly available repository, with a guide and documentation that allows others to use
 the tools and metrics therein.
 3. Building on prior work, develop and publish baseline parameters for heliostats size and
 field layouts for electricity and process heat projects of varying sizes.

 Metrology and Standards

 1. Although substantial progress has been made around indoor (lab or manufacturing
 facility) optical metrology, unsolved problems remain for outdoor, in situ measurements,
 including the ability to measure wind effects.
 2. Long-term monitoring of heliostat performance and durability is of interest to industry.

 1. Prepare outdoor facilities (under a separately funded project), including mobile
 metrology tools and long-term testing capabilities.
 2. Continue maturing outdoor measurement capabilities such as in situ tracking and
 calibration control (including lookback).
 3. Increase efforts and resources for standardization work.
 4. Focus on the usability of measurement capabilities and make them available to the public.
 5. Use test results to inform baseline heliostat design.
 6. Conduct testing to support the investigation of the optical effects of wind and temperature
 changes, and integrate test results into digital twins (time and resources permitting; this
 may need to happen outside of HelioCon).

 Components and Controls

 1. The previous gap analysis remains relevant and appropriate, and good progress has been
 made toward closing the Tier 1 gaps.
 2. Standards work should consider safety and wireless communication cybersecurity issues.

 x

 1. Work on composite structural components should cease, but work on composite facets
 should continue.
 2. Via request for proposal (RFP) projects, continue to evaluate scale-up issues for wireless
 control.
 3. Working with the TEA task, continue to improve tools to easily assess field performance,
 including the impacts of facet focal length variation due to wind, gravity, and thermal
 variability; controls optimization; and performance gains from automatic calibration.
 4. Add battery and photovoltaic power supply to the existing generic heliostat controller
 design.
 5. Via RFP projects, continue to support open-source plant controls, maximizing automation
 within the available budget.
 6. Continue to support International Electrotechnical Commission (IEC) standards
 development, including recommendations for cycles, loads, safety, and cybersecurity.
 7. Forego any work on coatings.

 Advanced Manufacturing

 1. The priority should be to minimize field work; avoid optimizing for ex-factory cost at the
 expense of installation.
 2. Align design, manufacturing, and installation processes with relevant industries to benefit
 from their experience.

 1. Support field deployment and heliostat reference design efforts, focusing on creating a
 heliostat design appropriate for the U.S. market that minimizes field work.
 2. Align heliostat manufacturing processes with those developed for and used within
 mature, high-volume industries.
 3. Explore a wider range of composite material constructions for mirror facets.

 Resources, Training, and Education

 1. Key challenges include faculty expertise limitations, curriculum content and delivery
 gaps, student employment perceptions, and critical disconnects with industry needs.

 1. Faculty education: Initiate professional development workshops and expand collaborative
 research opportunities.
 2. Industry connections: Create connections between universities, industry, and national
 labs; establish mentorship programs.

 xi

 3. Curriculum development: Begin with the fundamental principles of SHIP, CSP, and
 related fields, and expand to include more advanced topics, such as performance
 modeling and control systems.
 4. Consortium integration: Leverage resources from HelioCon and partners (e.g., databases,
 short courses).

 Field Deployment

 1. Although large plants are calculated to have the lowest levelized cost of electricity
 (LCOE), they also present risks that may be insurmountable.
 2. The likelihood of project success can be increased through early consideration of issues
 like environmental impacts, land use permitting, visual impact, and capitalization.

 1. Assess small SHIP projects (<10 MWth) on previously disturbed land, using a standard
 heliostat design and avoiding new technology risks and transmission line buildouts.
 2. Develop a glare analysis tool for a stationary heliostat case in an urban environment.
 3. Evaluate integration benefits of beam-down systems in the context of small industrial
 SHIP systems (i.e. reduced visual impact, process integration with equipment near grade,
 etc.).
 4. Develop a techno-economic assessment for small SHIP systems (1–10 MW).

 Wind Load

 1. Significant progress has been made in collecting static and dynamic wind load data, both
 in outdoor, real-world conditions and in operational CSP plants.
 2. Although a complete, detailed understanding of all wind interactions with heliostats is not
 yet attainable, there is a need to provide guidelines to heliostat designers regarding design
 assumptions related to wind loading.

 1. Work toward a guideline for wind measurement and procedures relevant to the design
 and establishment of a new SHIP or CSP plant.
 2. Work toward a guideline for measurement, characterization, and analysis procedures to
 inform the testing requirements of a heliostat design for ultimate and operating load
 considerations.
 3. Extend research on static and dynamic wind loads to include their effect on pointing and
 optical accuracy.

 xii

 Soiling

 1. Soiling continues to be an important issue for SHIP and CSP plant developers and
 operators, and efforts aimed at informing site selection and plant operations remain
 valuable to those groups.
 2. There’s a trend of skepticism toward automation (for cleaning) and toward anti-soiling
 coatings.

 1. Continue work on supporting site selection and plant operations.
 2. De-prioritize work on cleaning via automated vehicle and work on anti-soiling coatings.
 3. Provide operators with advice on soiling-related measurements and mitigation measures.
 4. Develop a report or standard describing the state-of-the-art characterization methodology
 for use by industry to assess soiling at a potential project site. This should be done in
 coordination with relevant Solar Power and Chemical Energy Systems (SolarPACES)
 projects/tasks.
 5. Prioritize modeling improvements and uncertainty analysis for site characterization.

 Heliostat Reference Design
 Based on feedback from SHIP and CSP industry stakeholders, HelioCon recommends the
 inclusion of a conceptual design exercise for a reference heliostat in its scope for the next fiscal
 year. This reference conceptual design would be jointly developed by research institutes and
 industry, with the goal of providing a well-documented, transparent reference for the early stages
 of a heliostat design process. This would serve as a starting point for future entrants to the SHIP
 and CSP industry, allowing them to accelerate project deployment by reducing the risk and cost
 associated with the development of a new heliostat.

 HelioCon’s priorities in this effort will include:

 1. Developing a foundational document defining the objectives, use cases, intended
 environment, and other important considerations and constraints that will guide further
 heliostat development.
 2. Identifying and enumerating requirements from all stakeholders, such as customers,
 operators, regulators, manufacturers, installers, and others.
 3. Synthesizing product requirements that achieve the objectives while satisfying the
 stakeholder requirements.
 4. Analyzing the functions the heliostat must perform, and allocating those functions to
 specific components or subassemblies (hardware or software).
 5. Creating multiple conceptual designs for the heliostat, evaluating each against the
 requirements, and selecting a preferred conceptual design from the set.

 xiii

 At each step in this process, HelioCon will aim to publish, or otherwise provide to the public, the
 work underlying each output. For example, rather than simply reporting a table of wind loads the
 heliostat must withstand, HelioCon will document the method and calculations that led to those
 loads. In this way, the conceptual design exercise will provide a template that future heliostat
 developers can follow, should their heliostat requirements differ from the reference case studied
 here.

 HelioCon’s Role
 The updated goal and priorities enumerated above provide a detailed, specific description of the
 work HelioCon aims to complete in the next fiscal year. Zooming out, and thinking about
 HelioCon’s role in the SHIP and CSP community, we identified five areas where HelioCon is in
 a unique position to contribute:

 1. Nurture the consortium. HelioCon should bring the right people together from different
 segments of the SHIP and CSP community via research project solicitations, workshops,
 webinars, and other means of highlighting and communicating progress.
 2. Synthesize and share data and information. HelioCon should make information
 available in a way that’s accessible and useful to the recipients, with consideration of all
 the SHIP and CSP industry segments described above.
 3. Engage stakeholders. HelioCon should develop and maintain long-term relationships
 that contribute to advancement of SHIP and CSP technologies.
 4. Support the SHIP and CSP industries. HelioCon should develop a center of excellence
 for testing and qualification of heliostats, acting as a trusted third party when new
 products are launched.
 5. Push the boundaries. HelioCon should continue lab work to move the state of the art
 forward, with priorities being set in collaboration with diverse stakeholders.
 By maintaining its focus in these five areas, we are confident that HelioCon can and will
 continue to make meaningful, measurable impacts to advancing SHIP and CSP technologies and
 supporting their commercial deployment.

 xiv

 xxi

 1 Introduction
 The Heliostat Consortium (HelioCon) was established in 2021 with funding from the U.S.
 Department of Energy’s Solar Energy Technologies Office, with the goal of advancing U.S.
 heliostat technologies, leading to commercially competitive SHIP and CSP industries. HelioCon
 engages subject matter experts and stakeholders from academia, the research community, and
 industry through research projects, workshops, and external consulting. HelioCon’s core
 objectives are to:

 • Develop strategic core capabilities and infrastructure to support high-performance
 heliostat manufacturing, validation, and optimization and facilitate industry’s ability to
 design, manufacture, install, and operate central receiver heliostat fields with higher
 technical and economic performance.
 • Ensure that these capabilities are readily available to industry, meeting its needs.
 • Fund research on new technologies with significant potential to improve heliostat field
 economic performance.
 • Form U.S. centers of excellence focused on heliostat technology to restore U.S.
 leadership in heliostat research, development, and validation.
 • Promote workforce development by encouraging student internships and postdoctoral
 positions, forming a HelioCon early career scientist group to promote networking, and
 highlighting existing training and educational programs in heliostat design, production,
 and operation.

 1.1 HelioCon Roadmap Report
 Upon its formation, HelioCon convened a panel of subject matter experts who provided input via
 surveys and workshops regarding the state of the art in heliostat technologies, gaps between that
 state of the art and a competitive U.S. CSP market, and recommended areas for HelioCon’s
 research efforts. This input was synthesized into the Roadmap To Advance Heliostat
 Technologies for Concentrating Solar-Thermal Power (Zhu et al. 2022), hereafter referred to as
 the Roadmap Report, which provided a reference on state-of-the-art heliostat technologies and
 defined the priorities under which HelioCon has been operating for the past three years.

 The Roadmap Report includes gap assessments within six topic areas:

 • Techno-economic analysis
 • Metrology and standards
 • Components and controls
 • Advanced manufacturing
 • Resources, training, and education
 • Field deployment.

 And two special subtopics:

 • Wind load
 • Soiling.

 1

 Within each topic area, gaps between the state of the art and the technical and economic
 conditions necessary for a competitive CSP market are identified and discussed. These gaps were
 assigned a priority tier (Tier 1 = most important, Tier 3 = least important) based on a
 combination of technical analysis and expert opinion. The Tier 1 gaps were further studied to
 describe an envisioned end state where the gap is closed, a justification for why this gap is worth
 addressing, and recommended pathways to accomplish this.

 1.2 Purpose of This Report
 At the time of publishing this report, HelioCon has one year remaining in its five-year funding
 period. Having spent nearly four years and $18 million on approximately 56 separate projects
 addressing the gaps identified in the Roadmap Report, substantial progress has been made in
 advancing heliostat technologies. Meanwhile, new challenges have also presented themselves;
 during these two years, there has been an apparent loss of momentum in commercial CSP
 activity in the United States, leading to renewed concerns about the viability of this technology
 domestically. Heliogen, a leading U.S. manufacturer of heliostats and developer of CSP plants,
 has ceased production of heliostats and closed its manufacturing facility. And more recently,
 there has been great uncertainty over the future of the Ivanpah Solar Electric Generating System
 in California. In 2025, the plant’s owners, including NRG Energy, Google, and BrightSource,
 agreed with Pacific Gas & Electric to end their power purchase agreement and shut down two of
 the three units at the plant (NRG Editorial Voices 2025). In early 2026, however, California
 regulators blocked that closure, citing the substantial and increasing need for power on the
 California electric grid, driven in large part by the demand for new data centers (Smith 2026).

 Given the juxtaposition of technical progress fueled by HelioCon’s efforts and the commercial
 challenges being faced in the CSP industry, HelioCon leadership recommended the creation of
 this 2025 update to the Roadmap Report. The purpose of this report is to update the summary of
 the heliostat state of the art, assess the progress made by HelioCon so far, and reprioritize the
 topics to be addressed by HelioCon during the remainder of its funding period.

 1.3 Report Organization
 Section 1 provides an introduction to the HelioCon Roadmap Report and this 2025 update. In
 Section 2, the state of the art in heliostat technology is discussed, with emphasis on the changes
 since the Roadmap Report. Section 3 describes the approach to drafting this update. Section 4
 discusses the original goals of HelioCon as described in the Roadmap Report, and revisits those
 goals with proposals for refinement, given the current state of the U.S. CSP industry and the
 growing interest in SHIP. Sections 5–12 provide topic-by-topic descriptions of progress made to
 date by the HelioCon research projects and reprioritized assessments of the research activities to
 support the updated goals described in Section 4. Section 13 makes a proposal for the
 development of a heliostat reference design, to be made publicly available to developers of CSP
 systems as a way to reduce risk and accelerate near-term heliostat field deployments in the
 United States. Section 14 discusses system-level considerations and gaps for SHIP and CSP plant
 deployment outside the heliostat field. Finally, Section 15 concludes the report.

 2

 2 Update to Heliostat State of the Art
 Since the publication of the 2022 HelioCon Roadmap Report, heliostat technology has continued
 to evolve, albeit incrementally, rather than through major breakthroughs. The last few years have
 seen progress in cost reduction, refinements in heliostat design, and early-stage advancements in
 automation and wireless control. However, the commercial deployment of new heliostat fields in
 the United States has been limited by economic and market challenges rather than technical
 hurdles.

 This section provides an updated assessment of the state of the art in heliostat technology,
 highlighting both improvements and persistent challenges that continue to affect the industry's
 growth, and including topics ranging from design to supply chain, deployment, and more.

 2.1 CSP Project Deployments and Configuration
 Figure 2, Figure 3, and Figure 4 show worldwide maps of CSP power plant capacity by country
 or region, including both operational plants (in blue) and plants under construction (in red),
 indicating data as of 2021, 2023, and 2025, respectively. While worldwide CSP plant capacity
 has grown substantially over that time, from 7,572 MW to 10,779 MW (operational and under
 construction), a close examination of the regional changes shows significant variation:

 • China has been completing projects and adding new construction to their pipeline
 • South Africa and the Middle East have completed projects, but have not added new
 construction to their pipeline
 • The United States has decommissioned plants, reducing overall CSP plant capacity
 • The rest of the world has remained largely unchanged, neither adding capacity nor
 decommissioning operational plants.

 3

 Figure 2. Map of indicating year 2021 worldwide CSP power plant capacity by country or region,
 including operational plants (blue) and plants under construction (red).

 Figure 3. Map of indicating year 2023 worldwide CSP power plant capacity by country or region,
 including operational plants (blue) and plants under construction (red).

 4

 Figure 4. Map of indicating year 2025 worldwide CSP power plant capacity by country or region,
 including operational plants (blue) and plants under construction (red).
 Upon examination of the pipeline of projects under construction and recently commissioned,
 both in China and elsewhere, a consensus appears to have formed that the most cost-effective
 system includes CSP with thermal energy storage in addition to a photovoltaic plant and, in
 locations where favorable, a wind farm as well. This configuration takes advantage of
 photovoltaics’ (PV’s) (and wind’s) relatively low LCOE, while also benefiting from the low cost
 of storage through CSP with TES. While the PV and/or wind plant is producing power, the CSP
 system charges its thermal storage system, such that it can discharge and produce power when
 the PV and wind plants are at part or no load.

 2.2 Heliostat Design Trends
 Since the publication of the HelioCon Roadmap Report, the evolution in heliostat design has
 been relatively conservative, working toward refinement and incremental improvements rather
 than wholesale changes or radical departures from the norm. Some noteworthy trends include

 • Incremental cost and performance optimization: Particularly in the Chinese market,
 where CSP plants are being deployed regularly leading to higher volume production of
 heliostats, new heliostat designs have incrementally reduced cost while maintaining or
 moderately improving optical performance. According to one expert familiar with that
 market, cost reductions on the order of 10% have been achieved over the last 2-3 years.
 • Shift away from novel heliostat configurations: Two leading American heliostat
 designers, Heliogen and Solar Dynamics, have both recently abandoned their novel
 heliostat configurations. Heliogen reports their intent to procure heliostats from an
 undisclosed third party, while Solar Dynamics has redesigned their heliostat around a
 more traditional pedestal-mounted azimuth slew drive, rather than their carousel
 “SunRing” approach.

 5

 • “Optimal” size still an open question: The debate over the ideal size for a heliostat has
 been active for decades and remains so today. With that said, it’s noteworthy that several
 heliostat developers are now promoting designs ranging from approximately 6 m2 (Vast
 Solar) to 50 m2 (sbp sonne), casting some doubt on the assertion that bigger is always
 better.
 • New materials being evaluated, not yet implemented: The heliostat research and
 design community has continued to explore new materials of construction for heliostats,
 for example structural composite materials (for both structural components and mirror
 facets) and anti-soiling coatings. While there have been some preliminary indications that
 such advanced materials could bring benefits, none have yet been incorporated into a
 commercial heliostat design.

 2.3 Wireless Control and Automation
 “Autonomous” heliostats, meaning heliostats which communicate wirelessly and power
 themselves (typically via onboard PV panels and batteries), remain an active area of
 development. Several heliostat developers have integrated such systems into their designs, most
 notably BrightSource Energy which has deployed autonomous heliostats at several commercial
 CSP plants. At the same time, Cosin Solar reports that in the Chinese market, the added cost of
 self-powering and wireless communication exceeds the cost of field wiring, and therefore they
 continue to commercialize non-autonomous heliostats for their projects in China.

 As wireless control of heliostats has gained preference within industry, the research community
 has increased its focus on the needs for security and robustness of the heliostat communications
 network. Research and testing are ongoing now, aiming to develop secure communication
 protocols with failsafe behaviors to minimize the chance of a cybersecurity incident within a
 CSP plant, and to ensure safe responses to any anomalies.

 Closely related to communications and controls is the heliostat field calibration system, which
 has also seen development in recent years. While all operating central tower CSP plants are (to
 the best of the authors’ knowledge) relying on traditional calibration techniques such as beam
 characterization system targets or beam-on-camera data collection, there has been ongoing
 research and demonstration projects for advanced systems such as Heliogen’s SOHOT (System
 for Observing Heliostat Orientations While Tracking). In 2023, Heliogen announced that they
 had successfully deployed SOHOT to the NSTTF solar tower at Sandia National Laboratories,
 demonstrating its ability to estimate heliostat orientations while the heliostat is tracking the
 receiver, allowing for real-time, continuous heliostat calibration. Whether SOHOT or similar
 systems will be deployed at a large scale remains to be seen.

 2.4 Cleaning and Maintenance
 While much is said about automated cleaning of heliostats, the state of the art still relies on
 manual cleaning or manually-operated cleaning machinery. The reasons for this range from
 economics (labor being relatively inexpensive compared to automation in some CSP plant
 locations) to feasibility (for example in heliostat fields that have not been graded and compacted,
 automated vehicles have difficulty navigating the terrain).

 6

 While cleaning remains relatively untouched by automation, inspection has made more progress.
 Drones have been shown to effectively survey heliostat fields, looking for damaged mirrors as
 well as alignment errors.

 2.5 Supply Chain
 Recent developments in the heliostat supply chain display a stark contrast between China and the
 rest of the world. Within China, as CSP plant construction has accelerated, the supply chain is
 optimizing around cost reductions. For example, suppliers are beginning to relocate their
 production facilities for key components closer to the project sites in the northern and western
 parts of the country in order to reduce the costs associated with logistics. Meanwhile, the U.S.
 supply chain for heliostats has contracted since the HelioCon Roadmap Report was first
 published, with Heliogen deciding to close its California factory and source heliostats from third
 parties instead.

 Two noteworthy challenges remain in developing a U.S. supply chain for heliostats. First, the
 heliostat requirements and the ideal heliostat design differ by application – the best heliostat for a
 Gen 2 (molten salt) CSP plant is likely not the best heliostat for a Gen 3 (solid particle) plant
 operating at higher temperatures and flux levels. In addition, without a pipeline of projects, the
 supply chain lacks the ability to iteratively improve. As long as CSP plants remain one-off
 projects, the vendors for various heliostat components don’t have an incentive to invest in
 improvements after learning from a given production run.

 2.6 Ivanpah’s Uncertain Future
 While not directly a heliostat topic, it’s worth noting that the Ivanpah Solar Electric Generating
 Station announced in January 2025 an intended closure of 2 of its 3 units, with an expectation
 that the final unit will soon follow (NRG Editorial Voices 2025). As of January 2026 the plant is
 still operating, and California regulators have reportedly blocked the shutdown, citing the need
 for power on California’s electrical grid, driven by increasing demand from data centers (Smith
 2026). While this is a potential setback for the CSP industry, it’s also worthwhile to note some of
 the contributing factors, including:

 • Ivanpah includes direct steam generating receivers and no thermal storage. Therefore, it
 generates only while the sun is shining, leading to a lack of differentiation from PV.
 • Ivanpah’s power purchase agreements (PPAs) were signed before the rapid price
 declines in PV that occurred around 2010, and are very expensive by today’s standards.

 While Ivanpah and the direct steam generating technology it’s based on are not economically
 competitive in today’s environment, this does not extend to other CSP system designs that
 include thermal energy storage.

 7

 3 Approach to the 2025 Update
 Mirroring the approach taken by HelioCon during the creation of the original Roadmap Study,
 this 2025 update effort began with input from stakeholders. Initially through a series of informal
 conversations discussing changes to the CSP industry since the HelioCon initiative began, and
 later through a series of topic-focused stakeholder meetings, HelioCon placed great importance
 on gathering information from many perspectives to avoid creating an echo chamber within the
 research community.

 3.1 Informal Discussions
 A series of small-group and one-on-one conversations were held during the fall of 2024 to
 collect high-level input from those involved with HelioCon research as well as those working in
 the CSP industry. Certain themes became apparent during these discussions, which were used to
 formulate several proposals leading into this 2025 update, and which are summarized here.

 First, given the lack of traction in the U.S. CSP industry over the past several years, there is
 broad agreement that HelioCon could be most helpful to industry by focusing on a nearer-term,
 intermediate goal rather than maintaining its focus exclusively on long-term competitiveness.
 This goal and the arguments behind it will be further discussed in Section 4.

 Second, the importance of system-level considerations was made in several discussions. While
 HelioCon’s mission is focused on heliostat fields, it’s impossible to design or assess a heliostat
 field without the context of a SHIP or CSP plant within which it operates. Further, addressing
 commercialization gaps within the heliostat field alone will not guarantee market adoption, as
 there remain similar gaps in other plant systems. Therefore, it was recommended and agreed that
 this report provide a cursory review of system-level considerations, providing improved context
 for the environment in which heliostat technologies exist today. These system-level
 considerations are discussed in Section 14 of this report.

 Finally, out of these informal discussions arose a proposal to initiate the conceptual development
 of a reference heliostat design (see Figure 83 for a summary of discussion feedback). This
 heliostat would be developed under HelioCon’s guidance, meeting a set of requirements
 designed to meet the needs of near-term, small- to medium-scale industrial process heat projects,
 and the design details would be made publicly available. The perceived benefits of this effort
 would include:

 • Reduced development costs and schedule for new entrants to the CSP industry, giving
 them a design to use as-is or as a starting point to improve upon
 • Increased level of confidence in the design, having been designed and qualified by trusted
 third parties
 • Improved bankability and a reduced risk profile for early projects
 • Publication of the design documents will provide an example for others to follow for the
 development of heliostats tailored to a different application

 The proposal for developing this reference heliostat design is discussed in more detail in Section
 13 of this report.

 8

 3.2 Stakeholder Meetings
 During February and March 2025, a series of stakeholder meetings were held to gather input on
 specific topic areas. Each meeting was led by the HelioCon topic area lead (and co-lead where
 applicable), and had similar goals:

 • Present an overview of activities conducted within HelioCon which were aimed at
 closing the commercialization gaps identified in the Roadmap Study which are relevant
 to this topic area
 • Assess the degree to which those gaps have been closed or remain
 • Gather input from the stakeholder group regarding how the previously-identified
 commercialization gaps might be reprioritized to support the intermediate goal of near-
 term commercial heliostat field deployments, or what new gaps might exist which were
 previously omitted.

 More than a dozen meetings and many more one-on-one conversations were held including over
 one hundred participants providing valuable input to this process. The input gathered during
 these meetings serves as a basis for much of what is written in the following sections of this
 report.

 9

 4 A Reimagined Goal for HelioCon
 4.1 Goals, Revisited
 4.1.1 Background
 The HelioCon Roadmap Report published in 2022 envisioned a fully competitive, mature market
 for heliostat-based CSP systems contributing to several sectors of U.S. energy infrastructure:
 • Utility-scale electricity generation systems with dispatchability
 • Heavy-duty industrial processes requiring high temperature (500ºC or above)
 • Solar fuel production with solar heat.

 Further, it identified areas for the consortium to focus on in order to achieve commercial
 competitiveness:
 • Heliostat installation cost
 • Heliostat technical performance
 • Heliostat field operations and maintenance
 • Commercial deployment risks.

 And finally, the Roadmap Report set targets for the levelized cost of electricity (LCOE) and
 levelized cost of heat (LCOH), 5 cents/kWhe and 0.8 cents/kWht, respectively, that would be
 used to assess competitiveness. To that end, the activities undertaken by HelioCon thus far have
 been focused on improving and optimizing heliostat technology, identifying cost drivers and
 opportunities for reduction thereof, and developing standards to drive toward long-term
 competitiveness.

 While progress has been made in all these areas, industry feedback indicates that the primary
 barriers to CSP deployment in the United States are less related to technology optimization, and
 more related to the risk profile associated with deploying nascent technologies without a long
 track record of successful operation. The risks associated with deploying CSP projects, both real
 and perceived, can create innumerable and sizeable challenges, for example:

 • Technology risks must be identified, reviewed, and addressed, typically during a
 diligence process with an independent engineer. This process is costly and can delay
 project deployments by many months.
 • Uncertainties in performance or availability can lead to either (a) underperformance of
 the completed plant relative to projections, or (b) conservative design practices,
 increasing margin at added cost.
 • Uncertainty around the performance, durability, degradation, reliability, and maintenance
 costs often manifest as increased borrowing or hurdle rates for project financing. These
 can have an outsized impact on the LCOE, dwarfing any benefit achievable through
 modest cost reductions or technology improvements.

 10

 4.1.2 Financial Implications of Project Risk
 To illustrate how project risks influence the economic viability of CSP projects, we conducted a
 sensitivity analysis looking at how the PPA price for a CSP power plant varies with key financial
 assumptions, namely the fraction of total capital cost financed by debt and the project equity
 internal rate of return (IRR). Both of these figures are dependent on the level of risk (real or
 perceived) of the project being financed, with more favorable terms (higher debt fraction and
 lower equity IRR) being granted to less risky projects.

 For debt fractions from 50% to 70% and equity IRRs from 10% to 15%, and assuming a case
 study similar to that of the large electric plant from the baseline cases in the roadmap report
 (updated to match default values and assumptions in the most recent release of SAM), we
 calculated the required Year 1 PPA price under flat pricing to achieve a given return on equity in
 Year 20 of the project. Then, separately, by establishing a baseline of 50% debt and a 15% rate
 of return (typical of a higher-risk project) and running a sensitivity analysis on the capital cost of
 the heliostat field, we developed a mapping of the Year 1 PPA price to an equivalent installation
 cost for the heliostat field. The results are shown in Figure 5.
 PPA Equivalent
 (cents/kWh) Installed Cost
 ($/m2)
                                                                                               13.27       127.0

                                                                                               12.99       117.7

                                                                                               12.71       108.5

 11.33 62.2

 11.06 52.9

 10.50 34.4

 Figure 5. Summary of (a) Year 1 PPA Price and (b) equivalent heliostat installation cost to achieve
 target rate of return on equity, as a function of the target rate of return and available debt for the
 project. Assumes a baseline of 15% return on equity and 50% of project capital financed by debt
 for the installation cost calculation.
 Figure 5 shows that a reduction in the cost of equity to 12% and an increase in debt access to
 70% of the capital cost, both reasonable measures for a large project of moderate risk, is
 analogous to a reduction in heliostat installation cost from the current assumption of $123/m2 to
 well below the $50/m2 SunShot goal.

 While the specifics of this sensitivity study depend on the selected plant configuration and many
 underlying assumptions, the key conclusion remains: reducing project risk is a strong lever to
 pull on as we seek to improve the deployment rate of CSP plants.

 11

 4.1.3 Restated HelioCon Goal
 Given the industry feedback described above, the slowdown in commercial CSP activity in the
 United States since the Roadmap Report was published, and the importance of risk reduction as
 illustrated with the sensitivity analysis above, HelioCon should refocus its efforts on eliminating
 the commercial barriers that prevent heliostat field deployments and reducing the
 uncertainty around the cost, performance, and reliability of heliostat fields.

 4.2 Facilitating Market Adoption
 With this newly articulated goal in mind, there are several steps HelioCon can take to support
 near-term commercial deployments.

 1. Promote standardization
 o Standardization can provide assurance to CSP project developers, lenders,
 investors, and end users that the heliostat field was designed and qualified
 according to industry best practices
 o Such standards can also streamline projects by providing a common framework
 under which to evaluate a heliostat field, avoiding potentially protracted
 negotiations about the details of such an evaluation
 o Standardization can also provide a more competitive supply chain, allowing CSP
 project developers to more easily consider multiple sources for heliostat fields,
 ensuring the offers from different vendors can be compared objectively
 2. Narrow the gap between projected and real-world performance
 o Increasing the level of confidence in performance predictions made during project
 development will decrease the risk of a performance shortfall
 o Reduced performance risk should lead to a lower cost of capital
 o In addition, improving confidence in performance predictions can allow plant
 designers to reduce contingency and overcapacity, reducing plant capex
 3. Identify near-term market opportunities
 o Recognizing that cost, performance, and reliability of any technology improve
 through repeated deployments, it’s imperative to identify those market
 opportunities which will be most forgiving to the current imperfections
 o By selecting near-term market opportunities which are best served by the current
 state of the art, a nascent CSP industry can gain momentum and consistency,
 encouraging and attracting wider industrial participation
 4. Streamline technology development and validation
 o The CSP industry in the United States will develop faster if certain foundational
 tools and capabilities are provided by third parties, such as national labs,
 obviating the need for private companies to internally develop the same

 12

 4.3 Putting It Into Action
 In the subsequent sections of this report, each HelioCon topic area will be addressed in the
 context of these revised goals and priorities. The gaps identified in the 2022 Roadmap Report,
 between the then-state of the art and the long-term goal of a fully competitive CSP market, will
 be summarized, as will the work done to make progress toward closing those gaps. Then the
 topic area priorities will be reassessed according to their alignment with these updated goals for
 HelioCon. This will result in recommendations for future work done under HelioCon during the
 remainder of its funding duration.

 13

 5 Techno-Economic Analysis
 The TEA topic uses models and analysis to quantitatively assess the benefits of heliostat design,
 manufacturing, and operation concepts considered by other topics. The TEA topic works to
 provide analysis on a “total system” level to identify the tradeoffs and interactions of proposed
 concepts. The objectives of this topic include:
 • Model economic viability of new heliostat designs and concepts
 • Perform analysis on fundamental problems that would promote heliostat economics in
 general
 • Provide analysis and support to guide the HelioCon R&D directions and portfolio.

 One of the main objectives of the TEA topic is to relate the cost and performance of heliostats
 and heliostat components to the overall system performance. The thermal energy collected from
 heliostat fields can be used for different end uses, such as electricity generation (CSP) or
 industrial process heat (SHIP). Overall plant performance is affected by things such as the power
 cycle chosen, the type of cooling used, or the temperature of the end-use application.

 In the studies that we develop within this task, we limit our scope to the analysis of heliostat
 solar field. An individual heliostat’s performance is affected by its position in the solar field, so
 our scope must include the entire field to capture this. Further, heliostats interact with the tower
 and receiver. The size and type of the receiver (external or cavity) determines how much of the
 sun image reflected by a heliostat intercepts the receiver and is collected by the heat transfer
 medium in the receiver. The receiver also has a limit to the heat flux it can tolerate before being
 damaged. The tower height also impacts how the reflected sunlight from the heliostats intercepts
 the receiver, with the optimum tower height generally increasing with field size.

 Once the heat transfer medium leaves the tower, it does not interact with heliostats anymore,
 regardless of whether it goes into a TES tank, a power cycle, or another end use such as
 industrial process heat. Therefore, we limit the scope of this topic to the heliostat field, tower,
 and receiver as shown in Figure 6. We assess the heliostat field, receiver, and tower performance
 based on the thermal energy collected by the heat transfer medium compared to its installed and
 operating costs, using the levelized cost of heat (LCOH) as the key metric in our analysis. We
 assume that thermal energy can be used for any range of applications so that the analysis can
 focus on the heliostats used to collect solar energy. We recognize that the economic value of
 thermal energy depends on its temperature, with higher temperatures having more electricity
 generation potential and a greater number of potential applications. Therefore, comparisons of
 LCOH should only be made between systems with similar heat transfer medium temperatures.

 14

 Figure 6. Scope of CSP system considered in the HelioCon TEA
 Figure adapted from (Cox et al. 2022)

 5.1 Review of Tier 1 Gaps
 We developed an initial list of gaps for the TEA of heliostats early in the project. Industry
 developers and experts were asked to name gaps in TEA during the HelioCon Roadmap
 Workshop in 2021. Additional gaps were identified through conversations with other topic
 members, literature review, and during the development of the heliostat field base cases
 developed early in this project (Zolan et al. 2024a).

 Table 1 summarizes the solution functionality, justification and benefits, and proposed strategy
 for addressing each TEA Tier 1 gap as established in the roadmap study and separately published
 gap analysis (Augustine et al. 2024). Most of the gaps are related to developing models or data.
 Strictly speaking, none of these gaps are essential for heliostat development, but all would aid in
 the heliostat development process.

 Gap T1, poor understanding of linkage between model inputs and actual components, is listed
 first and is considered the most important gap. The team has been addressing this gap by
 developing models for all heliostat components and subcomponents, which may not be possible
 due to the large number of models that would be needed to cover all potential designs and
 technologies. Instead, the TEA will be used to identify which research could be most impactful
 and then focus on developing models and inputs for those high-impact areas.

 Gap T2, which refers to the lack of a well-adopted model for solar field operations and
 maintenance (O&M) costs, could be considered a subset of Gap T1 but we list it separately

 16

 because this is a gap that spans several field activities, and a concerted effort is needed to better
 understand field O&M and the trade-offs between O&M costs and system performance.

 Gap T3, impact of construction and commissioning labor costs and time frame, is an analysis gap
 and was included due to difficulties in getting projects commissioned on time in recent projects
 that have impacted the perceived bankability of CSP projects.

 Gap T4, the lack of models for SHIP applications, is similar to that of Gap T3 in that we require
 this model to proceed with the proposed HelioCon work.

 5.2 Related Projects and Progress Made Toward Closing Gaps
 This section summarizes the projects undertaken to date to address the Tier 1 gaps identified in
 Section 5.1. In what follows, we describe progress toward three of the four Tier 1 gaps, with the
 exception being gap T3. After some initial investigation and interviews with subject matter
 experts (SMEs), we determined that while construction and commissioning costs, and the impact
 of delays on financing costs are very important considerations to know, they tend to be very site-
 and project-specific and can vary widely from project to project. As a result, we prioritized
 efforts to address the other three gaps via the efforts that we summarize in this section.

 5.2.1 Equivalent Breakeven Installed Cost TEA Metric
 Gap T1 was considered a high priority for the HelioCon project because there are many
 opportunities to improve the cost, reliability and/or performance of a heliostat identified in the
 roadmap report, and without a framework to put these characteristics on equal footing there it is
 difficult to identify what the return on investment on a proposed improvement would be if
 successful. To that end, we developed a tradeoff-informed TEA metric which we termed
 “equivalent breakeven installed cost” (EBIC) as it presents a single, project-specific measure to
 compare potential heliostat improvements.

 This EBIC metric, which we describe in detail in a separately published paper (Zolan et al.
 2024a), recasts relative benefits into an equivalent installed capital cost required to obtain the
 same plant-wide levelized cost. To do so, we calculated the levelized cost of electricity (or heat)
 for a given project that is optimized to the system specifications of the underlying business case,
 which in our case includes either a receiver power delivery at specific design conditions or
 targeted annual energy delivery in a simulation from System Advisor Model (SAM) or
 SolarPILOT. In the example shown in Figure 7, for a potential reduction in (single-axis slope
 equivalent) optical error from 2.0 mrad to 1.0 mrad, an increase in installation cost from $140/m2
 to approximately $185/m2 yields the same levelized cost of heat (LCOH) as the baseline plant
 and heliostat design for an industrial process heat case, while the relative benefit is smaller for a
 larger, electricity-producing plant.

 17

 Figure 7. Equivalent breakeven installation costs summary, as a function of single-axis slope-
 equivalent optical error. The baseline of $140/m2 is located at the center of the horizontal axis, at 2
 mrad.
 Source: (Zolan et al. 2024b)

 We believe that this metric is more instructive for candidate improvements as compared to an
 estimate of LCOH reduction for a single case study, as this more clearly illustrates the value of a
 candidate heliostat improvement and determines the projects in which it would be most cost-
 effective to implement. This has also allowed us to assist other tasks within HelioCon by
 providing a measure of the effectiveness of a candidate improvement to a candidate heliostat
 attribute, whether related to components and controls, metrology, manufacturing, O&M, or
 deployment.

 5.2.2 Impact of Process Temperature on Cost and Efficiency of Solar Industrial
 Process Heat
 To address the lack of models for SHIP applications described in gap T4, we develop a first-
 principles analysis of heliostat field requirements for high-temperature process heat. The primary
 contribution of this work, the full details of which are available in a separate journal paper
 (Zolan et al. 2025), is a collection of cost curves that highlight the change in levelized costs as a

 18

 function of operating temperature due to the changing trade-off between optical and thermal
 losses at the receiver aperture. These costs are normalized to a baseline case of a molten-salt
 power plant to focus the results on the relative cost as a function of temperature rather than the
 estimated value itself. The costs are restricted to those of the receiver, tower, and solar field to
 keep the analysis focused on the cost of generating thermal energy and agnostic to the industrial
 process and the system configuration utilizing the heat. This also removes the impact of storage
 costs, which vary by application and by the required capacity factor for a specific industrial
 process.

 The study makes several simplifying assumptions that provide a lower bound on the relative
 difference in LCOH as the operating temperature of the receiver changes. First, we assume
 blackbody radiation, i.e., we assume that the receiver is perfectly insulated except for emitting
 heat as a black body at the aperture. We adopt a per-kW rated power cost model for the receiver,
 which effectively removes it from consideration when determining a minimum-cost
 concentration ratio (CR) for the designed plant. Finally, we remove heliostat size from
 consideration by assuming a fixed ratio between the heliostat size and width and those of the
 receiver aperture and assume the cost of the heliostat does not change as a function of the project
 size.

 An additional simplifying assumption is the receiver’s thermal resistance and lack of storage or
 piping losses, as we assume radiation losses occur at the process temperature in each application.
 For example, a 100°C difference between the absorbing surface and the work heat transfer
 medium, then in the 1200°C case, the emission losses would increase by approximately 30%
 (i.e., ≈ (1573.15/1473.15)4). As a result, the authors consider the estimates of efficiency losses
 and the related increase in LCOH as a function of temperature in this work to be a lower bound
 on those of a deployed project.

 This effort explores the trade-off between thermal and optical losses for solar heat for industrial
 processes (SHIP) applications using a collection of three case studies at operating temperatures
 that range from 900–1,550°C, comparing levelized costs estimates to a baseline estimate of
 levelized cost of heat (LCOH) for a molten-salt tower system.

 Figure 8 illustrates a parametric study in which the thermal power rating of the receiver varies
 and the same method described earlier in this section finds a minimum-cost solar field and tower
 and receiver specifications for each pairing of system size and receiver temperature.

 19

 Figure 8. Minimum levelized cost multiplier as plant size varies for target receiver temperatures of
                                       900, 1,200, and 1,550 C
 The results have several implications on the development of commercial high-temperature SHIP
 systems. First, the results indicate that the optimum SHIP solar field size to minimize LCOH, as
 shown in Figure 8, is approximately 100–400 MWth over the modeled temperature range. The
 optimum field size decreases and the LCOH increases as the receiver operating temperature
 increases. The local minimum also becomes sharper as receiver temperature increases, but the
 curves are relatively shallow so that a large range of field sizes are available to designers with
 little change in LCOH. The optimum field sizes are smaller than those used for commercial CSP
 generation, which would be in the range of 600–700 MWth for a 100-MWe power plant paired
 with 10–12 h of storage. While the cost-minimizing solar field power ratings of approximately
 160 MWth are in the range of existing clinker (30–200 MWth) and calcination (50–200 MWth)
 plants (Voldsund et al. 2019; ‘Gus’ Nathan et al. 2023), this does not include the required higher
 solar multiple and storage required to maintain high utilization of the process as is currently done
 in the industry with consistent heat sources. Additionally, the recommended receiver power
 ratings in our results are much larger than anything that has been demonstrated for SHIP at
 elevated temperatures. The largest high-temperature (>900°C) receiver design to date is the
 CentRec receiver by DLR, a centrifugal particle receiver designed for an operating temperature
 up to 1000°C and a 2.5-MWth power rating. The receiver has been tested in the lab at design
 conditions but has only been tested under partial load in on-sun experiments (Ebert et al. 2019).
 This indicates a large gap may exist between state of the art and commercial receiver designs.

 The results of this study illustrate the importance of considering the temperature of an industrial
 process when determining levelized cost goals when using SHIP technologies to produce process
 heat at high temperatures. As receiver technologies advance and costs become known for de-
 signs that can accommodate high operating temperatures and the CRs they can accommodate,
 this study can be revisited for more accurate LCOH assumptions and heat transfer properties. To

 20

 continue this work, a separate project includes integration of the solar thermal collection system
 into SHIP applications at high temperatures for a full-system analysis, starting with estimating
 thermal losses and temperature reductions from the receiver to the TES system, and then the TES
 system to the off-taker.

 5.2.3 Heliostat Sizing Study
 This effort develops a methodology from which a project developer can select the best range of
 sizes for the heliostat according to the needs of the project, which include energy delivery
 targets, operating temperature, and additional site considerations (e.g., maximum tower size).
 The most recent and relevant study for this was performed more than a decade ago (Blackmon
 2013), in which the author included several assumptions on the heliostat performance and
 component-specific cost functions that we improve upon in this study.

 Methodology

 We start by establishing the needs of the underlying business case for the project, e.g., a SHIP
 application for an Aluminum Heat Treatment facility, requiring heat delivery at ~500°C,
 targeting annual energy of ~50 GWht. The plant uses shift labor of 16 hours/day and 7
 days/week, with a target of ≥ 75% sourcing from solar resources. Sizing the system and TES
 required to meet this demand can be done using design planning tools such as REopt (Hirwa et
 al. 2022; Mishra et al. 2022). Our approach addresses the key question of what heliostat size will
 provide the most cost-effective option (i.e., lowest LCOH) for the solar thermal heating of this
 project.

 We evaluate the LCOH of a paired heliostat design and project by developing and integrating
 two models of system performance and cost which encompasses installation and O&M costs,
 while a fixed charge ratio (FCR) is assumed to be constant. These two models simplify the
 analysis by focusing on the heliostat field, tower, and receiver dimensions while remaining
 agnostic to process heat integration requirements, as illustrated in Figure 9.

 21

 Figure 9. An overview of the LCOH evaluation incorporating performance and costs.

 In this analysis, the heliostat field size and optical error will change as a function of heliostat
 size, while targeting an annual energy demand (i.e., fix annual energy production for all heliostat
 sizes). The receiver thermal power (field design power) is fixed for all heliostat sizes. While the
 cost model incorporates two developed cost correlations as a function of heliostat size i.e.,
 component installation and O&M costs.

 Performance Model

 We developed an optical performance correlation encompassing four optical error sources, i.e.,
 slope, tracking, canting, and reflected image errors, as depicted in Figure 10.

 22

 Figure 10. Methodology to generate a function that estimates optical performance as a function of
 heliostat size.

 Each source of error is linearly fitted based on 3–4 sources from the literature (Table 2), except
 for the reflected image error which is assumed as constant (0.2 𝑚𝑟𝑎𝑑), and the combined optical
 error calculation is then translated into a single-axis slope error equivalent, 𝜎𝑡𝑜𝑡𝑎𝑙 same as the
 definition used in the System Advisor Model (SAM) and the combined error calculation from
 SolarPILOT, but with canting error source been added.
