 The performance of the receiver is identical to that of the SHIP study, i.e., we assume that the
 receiver aperture radiates thermal power at the designed operating temperature and is otherwise
 perfectly insulated.

 Cost Model

 In contrast to assuming a fixed specific heliostat cost of 127 $/m2 as in prior work, we developed
 a customized cost surrogate model based on Blackmon’s model where the cost of a heliostat is
 based on 3 categories: (1) fixed costs per m2 (irrespective of heliostat size or number of
 heliostats), (2) heliostat size-dependent costs per m2 based on imposed moments, and (3) fixed
 costs per m2 based on the number of heliostats in the field. Blackmon relied on the ‘three-halves
 power law’, which is considered conservative because it yields a scaling aerodynamics exponent

 24

 of 0.5 cost per unit area proportionality; thus, it was modified to 0.65 in their latest publication
   (Blackmon 2021).

 Let 𝑖 ∈ 𝐼 be the set of all heliostat components (i.e., mirror facet, drive, structure, controls, PV
 cell, battery, foundation, infrastructure, labor, and transport), then the cost ($/m2 ) of a heliostat
 component, 𝐶𝑖 as a function of heliostat size, 𝐴, and number of heliostats in project, 𝑁 is:

 𝐴 𝑠𝑖 𝑁
 𝑓
                                    𝐶𝑖 = 𝐶𝑖 + 𝐶𝑖ℎ ( 0 ) + 𝐶𝑖𝑛 ( 0 ) , ∀𝑖 ∈ 𝐼                              (3)
 𝐴 𝑁

 where the superscripts 𝑓, ℎ, and 𝑛 represents the three cost categories of fixed, heliostat size-
 𝑓
 dependent, and number-dependent, respectively. Therefore the coefficients 𝐶𝑖 , 𝐶𝑖ℎ , and 𝐶𝑖𝑛 are
 the fixed cost ($/𝑚2 ), heliostat size-dependent cost ($/𝑚2 ), and number-dependent cost of
 component 𝑖 ($/𝑚2 ), respectively, 𝑠𝑖 is the scaling factor (or aerodynamic exponent), 𝐴𝑜 is the
 reference heliostat area (𝑚2 ), and 𝑁 𝑜 is the reference total heliostat number.
 The heliostat, 𝑁, cost per m2, 𝐶(𝑁) is the sum of the heliostat component, 𝐶𝑖 :

                                                 𝐶(𝑁) = ∑𝑖∈𝐼 𝐶𝑖                                            (4)

 However, our approach does not rely on a single commercial heliostat as a reference heliostat,
 instead we collected the cost breakdown for the heliostat components from 3 different
 commercial heliostats: SunRing by SolarDynamics (27 m2) (Kattke 2023), Stellio by sbp (48.5
 m2) (Yerudkar et al. 2024), and LH-2.3 by BrightSource Energy (19 m2. Our reasoning is that
 SunRing is mostly suitable for category 2 costs, except for the drive systems, and it has the most
 up to date compressive component cost data based on the Design for Manufacturing and
 Assembly (DFMA) analysis (Kurup et al. 2022) and the SolarDynamics technical report (Kattke
 2023). Heliostat infrastructure (assembly and check out) and site labor costs must rely on a
 commercially deployed heliostat (SunRing was not deployed at any plant while Stellio has a
 difficult mirror design for modeling though it was deployed at the Hami plant); thus, the LH-2.3
 has the most relevant data suitable for this analysis. Stellio foundation cost data is more
 appropriate for the study since Stellio has a simple and similar configuration to the conventional
 T-tube design. We assume that the heliostat is fully wireless and autonomous thus the control
 and communication costs follow the SunRing data, with most up-to-date technology and costs.

 This study focuses more on the methodology than the initial results, as the publicly available data
 is limited, and there are multiple outside factors that would influence decision making in a size
 selection, including a procurement process that may differ significantly from the existing cost
 curves, component selection that may change performance, and prior experience with a given
 design that will allow for learning-based cost reduction as additional projects are installed with
 the same design. These factors, particularly prior experience, are likely to cause selection of
 larger designs that may be perceived as suboptimal under the current framework.

 Next Steps

 We are drafting a manuscript detailing the methodology with initial results from this study and
 expect to submit it to a peer-reviewed journal in the next quarter.

 25

 5.2.4 Bottom-up Costing of Next-Generation Heliostat Design
 To support the heliostat sizing and understanding of the market, the TEA milestone in HelioCon
 this year is to conduct a bottom-up costing of a next-generation heliostat. For this effort we chose
 the SunLite design by SolarDynamics as they agreed to share the bill of materials and design
 information with us as part of a related study. We are using Design for Manufacturing and
 Analysis (DFMA) to obtain cost estimates by subcomponent. Prior work by NLR has performed
 DFMA analysis on two other heliostat designs, the SunRing and Stellio (Kurup et al. 2022). This
 work is still in progress and should be completed later this year. The cost analysis will help to
 provide perspective on what a next-generation heliostat field costs for a given project, as the
 analysis includes a baseline assumption on project size that can be adjusted. Once completed, we
 can use the DFMA analysis as another data point in the heliostat sizing study as well as inform
 future cost estimates of heliostat fields for future cost assumptions in publicly available datasets,
 such as NLR’s Annual Technology Baseline (Mirletz et al. 2024).

 5.3 Gap Updates and Priorities Supporting Intermediate Goal
 The projects described in Section 5.2 have made progress toward addressing three of the four
 top-tier gaps outlined in the roadmap report. The tradeoff-informed TEA metric in Section 5.2.1
 provides a measure by which component-specific changes to a heliostat can be compared on
 equal footing under a specific project design, which helps address T1. The SHIP study in Section
 5.2.2 provides a foundation for future solar field research to support industrial heat applications
 at high temperatures, making progress on gap T4. Additionally, the heliostat sizing study
 addresses both T1 and T4 as a SHIP case is included in the study, and separate work in the
 soiling subtask is developing a soiling dataset to help address the O&M modeling gap in T2.
 Commissioning and construction costs were determined to be highly variable and site-specific in
 early discussions with developers and other subject matter experts, and while some work in the
 field deployment task is focused on reducing permitting barriers and determining foundation
 needs as a function of soil composition, the opportunity to address this gap in the TEA task has
 been limited to date.

 A review with a technical advisory committee took place recently to determine the best focus
 moving forward for this task. The feedback in general was positive with respect to the progress
 made in the TEA task to date, and the following three key insights were made by the group
 which will help prioritize TEA efforts in HelioCon moving forward:

 1. Accessibility to the tools and metrics developed in the TEA task should be a priority
 moving forward. While publishing the studies in open-access journals is useful in
 generating awareness of the metrics and demonstrating their usefulness, SAM does
 enable many of these analyses to be performed, and easily accessible and publicly
 available scripts utilizing these capabilities to replicate the analyses would maximize the
 impact of this research to developers. While developing new graphical user interfaces
 that enable easy use of the tools is likely out of scope for HelioCon, a software repository
 that includes copies of these analyses is likely to still provide value to the CSP
 community in the interim.

 2. There is generally a concern that technoeconomic models for CSP like SAM
 currently overestimate performance in default “off-the-shelf” assumptions. The key

 26

 events highlighting this are the discussions of potential closure at the Ivanpah Solar
 Electric Generating System and the general underperformance of the solar field at
 Crescent Dunes Solar Energy Project when compared to the expected output advertised
 when the projects were commissioned. A key goal of the high-fidelity performance
 prediction modeling effort under the Field Deployment task attempts to address this by
 both including more realistic assumptions and adding performance factors not currently
 considered in TEA models under default settings, such as the use of a finite number of
 focal lengths, as well as including uncertainty in the performance parameters in the
 forecast. While SAM does allow for uncertainty in its CSP modules, a framework with
 guidelines on incorporating uncertainty in key performance characteristics is useful, and
 such a framework has added value if available to the public in a digestible or usable
 format, referencing point #1 above.

 3. Interactions with the rest of the system are important to consider in future analysis.
 The most important component interaction with the heliostats is the receiver, which has
 flux limits on parts of the receiver’s absorbing surface as well as the heat shields, the
 latter of which has caused issues at operating plants when heliostat aiming is off target.
 However, system-level losses and temperature differentials from the receiver to the
 storage system, and then from the storage system to the heat delivery system in an
 industrial process, should be considered when determining solar field needs and how the
 heliostat can deliver them for a particular project. While the SHIP study did include the
 thermal losses at the receiver, the underlying assumption was that the receiver
 temperature was equivalent to the target industrial process, i.e., the reactor was in the
 receiver instead of a storage medium or heat transfer medium delivering the heat to a
 process. Additionally, the high concentration ratios were achieved in the study using
 simple aiming, which is likely to damage the receiver’s absorbing surface when the solar
 field is delivering the designed thermal power.

 4. The benefits of reducing perceived risk associated with SHIP or CSP in general may
 outweigh those of adding performance improvements or cost reductions. Higher-risk
 projects are more difficult to finance as the cost of equity is likely to increase, in addition
 to the ratio of equity required to secure debt financing. The cost of this financing may
 outweigh the improvements to a plant’s levelized costs associated with a technology
 improvement that increases accuracy or reduces cost. While technology risk includes the
 reliability of components that are beyond the scope of HelioCon, understanding this
 benefit can demonstrate the potential benefit of a more robust heliostat design when the
 tradeoff of O&M cost or component uptime isn’t sufficient to justify the improvement on
 its own.

 To that end, in addition to closing out the heliostat sizing study and bottom-up costing of the
 SunLite heliostat, the next, near-term priorities will include: (1) exploration of further
 enhancements to performance models and validation of the performance forecasting framework,
 including integration of aiming strategy; (2) publication of the scripts and software libraries used
 to develop the analyses in this task into a publicly available repository, with a guide and
 documentation that allows others to use the tools and metric built in this task; and, (3) utilization
 of insights from the heliostat sizing study to inform standard heliostat and solar field designs that
 serve as baselines for future research and development. We believe that these priorities represent

 27

 the best path to extracting maximal value from the TEA task in the remaining time left in the
 project. Additionally, it may enhance the usability of SAM by project developers who conduct
 project analysis using the outputs of a SAM simulation but require several additional steps
 parsing and plotting both time-series and summary output.

 28

 6 Metrology and Standards
 Optical metrology systems are critical to enabling the construction of heliostat fields that deliver
 high economic performance.

 Metrology, in the context of heliostat, is the application of measurement for performance
 assessment and heliostat quality assurance. It is a fundamental requirement of successful wide-
 scale CSP deployment. The nature of CSP, particularly achieving high temperatures via high
 solar concentration ratios, implicitly requires high optical accuracy. Traditional optical
 metrology techniques fall short due to the very large size of CSP solar field, complicated by their
 outdoor setting. Further, CSP incurs special metrology challenges, such as measuring systems
 distributed over very wide land areas exceeding multiple square kilometers, long optical focal
 lengths of up to 1.6 km or longer, assessment of optical accuracy under varying gravity loads and
 temperatures, high production volumes, and operation in a harsh environment with overhead
 solar flux hazards. Despite these challenges, heliostats must demonstrate optical accuracy of a
 few milliradians in slope error, implying that their metrology systems must deliver sub-
 milliradian accuracy. In addition, the long service life of CSP plants mandates recurring
 inspection of thousands of heliostats to detect defects needing maintenance or repair.

 Standards are equally important for any given product development, and the heliostat is no
 exception. Standards can serve as a fundamental basis for the heliostat community to
 communicate; when it comes to comparing different heliostat technologies, a consistent platform
 can be adopted to ensure fairness and promote healthy unbiased competition. More importantly,
 when the heliostat technology is placed in a market with other available technologies such as PV,
 wind, and fossil fuel, sufficient standards would be a critical reference for investors to examine
 the bankability of a given commercial system. At the same time, the development of standards
 needs to be a collective effort across the entire global heliostat community. It would involve a
 series of rigorous necessary steps such as gathering a dedicated working group, regular technical
 meetings, initial draft guideline, collection of comments/edits, revision, various stages of voting,
 and organization-specific requirements on standards. Thus, development of final standards is
 typically time-consuming, and it is always suggested to start as soon as possible.

 29

 6.1 Review of Tier 1 Gaps
 Below we list the Tier 1 gaps in both metrology and standards, as reported in the first HelioCon
 roadmap (Zhu et al. 2022) Table 8.

 Metrology Tier 1 Gaps

 M1: Opto-mechanical error measurement in laboratory: there are no fully validated
 metrology techniques on opto-mechanical error measurement in the laboratory, which is
 typically performed on an integrated heliostat under loads (structural load and additional
 simulated load). Current available metrology techniques have not been validated against
 each other and their operation often involves a complex, error-prone process.

 M2: Opto-mechanical error measurement in outdoor environments: there are missing
 metrology techniques on opto-mechanical error measurement in outdoor environments
 that can test one or multiple integrated heliostats under realistic impact factors, such as
 • Wind load
 • Temperature
 • Full range of orientation.
 While a measurement in an outdoor environment may be prone to additional unpredicted
 uncertainty, a direct verification using reflected beam assessment would also become
 necessary.

 M3: Opto-mechanical quality assurance tools for mass production: there are missing
 opto-mechanical quality assurance tools for heliostat mass production. The tools need to
 be adapted/optimized for specific heliostat design in order to achieve required accuracy,
 efficiency, and reliability.

 M4: Opto-mechanical quality calibration after installation: there is missing metrology for
 opto-mechanical quality calibration after installation.

 M5: Opto-mechanical error in situ monitoring tools: there are missing in situ monitoring
 tools for the full spectrum of opto-mechanical error including surface slope error, mirror
 facet canting error, and heliostat tracking error. The in situ tools should be applicable to
 commercial-scale heliostat fields.

 M6: Receiver flux quality real-time assurance tools: there are missing validated receiver
 flux quality real-time assurance tools that can accommodate aiming strategies based on
 the knowledge of full field heliostat opto-mechanical performance.

 The first year of HelioCon also pursued another approach to metrology gap analysis, which
 centered around the questions that metrology systems are intended to answer. This question-
 based gap analysis also included stakeholder interviews, and was published as part of HelioCon
 at SolarPACES 2021 (Brost 2022).

 The following subsections will provide a brief review of project work performed, corresponding
 to the project items listed in Table 4 above. We will also include a few non-HelioCon projects
 which are closely related to the HelioCon work we report here, and which influence updates to
 the original gap analysis presented above. Note that we are not attempting a comprehensive
 review of all metrology work in the CSP field; for that, we refer you to the excellent surveys
 Sattler et al. (2020) and Röger et al. (n.d.).

 6.2.1 Metrology: Foundation
 We’ve categorized six metrology projects as “foundational,” meaning they pertain to
 fundamental aspects of CSP optics and/or optical measurement. This section discusses these six
 projects.

 6.2.1.1 Distortion Analysis
 This analysis was performed in a prior project, but it is important to mention, because its findings
 significantly inform the design of nearly all CSP optical metrology systems.

 Reflection is the fundamental mechanism used by concentrating solar optics to achieve high
 solar flux intensity. Thus, effective CSP metrology systems must measure reflection to correctly
 characterize how well a given CSP collector reflects light onto its target.

 Reflections from imperfect mirrors exhibit distortion, and this is an important phenomenon
 affecting the reliability of CSP metrology systems that are based on the principle of identifying
 features in reflected images (ReTNA, NIO, and UFACET are all examples). If distortion is
 excessive, then reflected features cannot be recognized, causing measurement degradation or
 failure.

 Observations in functioning heliostat fields revealed situations where distortion became
 excessive. This led to the questions: Why? What conditions increase distortion, and what can be
 done to reduce it?

 This was investigated by two key experiments performed at the Sandia NSTTF heliostat field. In
 the first experiment, a series of images were captured viewing the same heliostat mirror, with the
 back of an NSTTF facet seen in reflection. The image sequence varied the camera-to-mirror and
 target-to-mirror distances. The results are shown in Figure 14; distortion is seen to increase with
 camera-to-mirror and target-to-mirror distance, and especially both together. The second key
 experiment captured a series of images using a similar parameter exploration, but with longer
 range distances. In this case the NSTTF tower was the reflection target. The results are shown in
 Figure 15. Again, distortion increases with both camera-to-mirror and target-to-mirror distance,
 leading to extreme distortion when both values are large. Note that these distances are realistic
 for heliostat metrology, since mirror-to-tower distances can reach 1.6 km in large solar fields
 such as the Crescent Dunes Solar Energy Project.

 High distortions such as those seen in the upper right of both Figure 14 and Figure 15 will defeat
 feature recognition in CSP metrology systems that require robust identification of reflected

 36

 objects. Thus, to design robust CSP metrology systems, we must either avoid the geometric
 situations leading to high distortion or choose measurement techniques that are fundamentally
 distortion-tolerant, such as fringe deflectometry.

 Understanding distortion effects is necessary to design CSP metrology systems that reliably
 achieve metrology goals despite the presence of distortion. These factors strongly influenced the
 design of Sandia’s SOFAST, UFACET, Lookback, and Ground Truth systems, NLR’s ReTNA
 system, and provided key numerical input to the design of NLR’s NIO data collection. Thus, this
 analysis supports reducing gaps M1, M2, M3, M4, and M5. For additional information, see Brost
   (2023b) and Mitchell and Zhu (2020).

 6.2.1.2 Equinox Data
 Heliostat beams reflected onto a tower beam characterization system (BCS) target exhibit
 significant changes in shape from heliostat to heliostat, and also over time. What is the pattern in
 these changes? How will they impact energy production? What are the ramifications for
 metrology system design?

 To investigate these questions, we conducted a systematic observational study of these effects.
 We directly measured the variation in reflected beam shape and pointing accuracy that occurs
 throughout the day and year for nine key heliostats in the NSTTF heliostat field (Figure 16(a)).
 We used BCS to identify aim point corrections that removed observed pointing errors, and
 collected data to express corrections as a function of time of day (Figure 16(b)). Our
 observations show that a heliostat pointing error must be measured multiple times per day and
 multiple days to identify aim corrections. We also used BCS to record the shape of reflected
 beams throughout the day. Figure 17 shows how the reflected beam for heliostat 5W09 begins
 well focused in the morning but becomes badly defocused by late afternoon due to sun incidence
 angle. (The morning beams are focused but appear elliptical, due to the beam’s steep angle of
 incidence relative to the BCS target wall.) Figure 18 shows the results organized in a manner that
 provides insight into how heliostat position and time of day influence beam quality. These
 effects are inevitable for fixed-shape heliostats.

 By showing the variation in pointing error and reflected beam shape as a function of varying sun
 incidence angle, this work supports the design of accurate in-field metrology systems, addressing
 gaps M2, M4, and M5. It also inspires the design of the University of Arizona’s variable-shape
 heliostat described in the next section. For additional information, see Brost et al. (2024).

 6.2.1.3 University of Arizona Heliostat Assessment
 As seen in Figure 17 and Figure 18, heliostat beam focus degrades with varying sun incidence
 angle. This can cause a significant loss of captured energy. To mitigate this, a team led by Roger
 Angel at the University of Arizona devised a heliostat design which changes shape as it tracks
 the sun, so that the reflected beam remains in focus (Figure 19). Under HelioCon RFP funding,
 they built a prototype of this heliostat and sent it to the Sandia for testing at the NSTTF.

 Data obtained during Sandia testing is shown in Figure 20. Part (a) shows the location of the
 University of Arizona twisting heliostat on the NSTTF field; it is placed very close to heliostat
 5E09, which exhibits beam defocus in the morning as shown in Figure 18 above. Also
 participating in the test is a single NSTTF heliostat facet, placed near the University of Arizona
 twisting heliostat. Thus the test included three heliostats: 5E09, the twisting heliostat, and a
 single facet. The test procedure was to direct these one at a time at the side of the tower, and
 capture a BCS image from a camera at ground level close to the three heliostats. This process
 was repeated throughout the day at approximately 30-minute intervals.

 Figure 20, part (b) shows the images captured near the top of each hour. The top row shows the
 beam spot created by NSTTF heliostat 5E09; this heliostat exhibits the severe defocus in the
 early morning hours seen in the Equinox data shown in Figure 18, with beam focus improving in
 the mid-day and afternoon. Inspection of Figure 18 confirms that this was expected. The second
 row shows the beam spots from the University of Arizona twisting heliostat. The spot remains
 well focused throughout the day, despite the widely changing sun incidence angle. This confirms
 that the University of Arizona heliostat operated successfully. Note that some images are
 saturated, making spots appear larger than they actually are. Quantitative analysis of non-
 saturated images to determine true spot size is pending. The third row shows the result for a
 single NSTTF facet. The spot also remains well-focused throughout the day. We believe this is
 due to the large f/d ratio for this heliostat situation, where f is the facet focal length, and d is the
 facet aperture diameter. We hypothesize that the defocus effect diminishes as f/d increases.

 This experiment is important, because:

 • It confirms significant beam defocus effect for fixed-shape heliostats such as 5E09.
 • It confirms that this defocus can be eliminated by a variable-shape heliostat such as the
 University of Arizona twisting heliostat.
 • It shows that defocus can also be reduced by small heliostats with high f/d ratios.
 • Studying Figure 16(a) and Figure 18, we see another approach to reducing defocus is to
 place heliostats closer to north-south centerline of the field, avoiding heliostats far to the
 east or west of this centerline.

 This provides us with important understanding of the defocus effect, and also multiple options
 for mitigating it.

 This investigation provides valuable heliostat design information, and also informs heliostat
 metrology, since outdoor metrology systems can expect to encounter this defocus effect when
 measuring reflected beams. Therefore in addition to providing key design insights, this work
 supports metrology gaps M2, M4, and M5. For additional information, see Vagher et al. (2024),
   NREL (2024), NREL (2025), and Brost and Hwang (2025).

 40

 Figure 19. University of Arizona twisting heliostat example optical surface as a function of solar
 incidence angle (Vagher et al. 2024)

 6.2.1.4 Canting Angle Prescriptions
 Heliostat metrology systems such as SOFAST, ReTNA, UFACET and NIO, described below,
 are designed to measure heliostat slope maps and canting angles. Their goal is to report canting
 error and identify corrections to improve performance. But calculating canting error requires
 comparing measured canting angles against desired canting angles. This raises the question:
 What are the desired canting angles?

 Design canting angle parameters are needed to complete heliostat metrology systems, so they can
 calculate canting error. But discussion with members of the CSP community quickly revealed
 mixed results on this topic, with many assuming that heliostats should be canted to form a
 paraboloid with focal length equal to the slant range distance to the receiver. This strategy is
 called “on-axis” canting, but it is not correct. One among several alternative canting strategies is
 “off-axis” canting, which sets canting angles to maximize focus for a particular target time of
 day and year. Optimum canting strategy depends on the performance goal of the heliostat field,
 and the kinematic structure of the heliostat (Buck and Teufel 2009).

 For the Sandia NSTTF heliostat field, off-axis canting was chosen, with a maximum
 performance time of solar noon on the spring equinox (Yellowhair 2020). This choice maximizes
 NSTTF solar flux in the vicinity of solar noon throughout the year, enabling high-temperature
 testing. This is why the solar noon time is the best focus time for all of the nine heliostats shown
 in Figure 18.

 This project has developed software to compute the optimum off-axis canting angles for a multi-
 facet heliostat. The software allows specification of heliostat facet configuration, heliostat
 position in the field, target aim point, and maximum-performance time, all of which influence
 optimum canting prescription. We have used this to compute specific canting angles for every
 heliostat and every facet in the NSTTF heliostat field, and we are in the process of releasing the
 resulting code and data to OpenCSP (Sandia National Laboratories n.d.); see below. We have
 also used this software to compute a variety of scenarios of interest, for example, comparing on-
 axis vs. off-axis canting, demonstrating the change in optimum canting with different receiver
 target points, and also variation in optimum canting as a function of time of day. We also
 reversed these scenarios to compute the degradation in focus resulting from using a single fixed
 canting scheme for different receiver target points and different times of day, all of which are
 realistic variations that occur at NSTTF routinely.

 Figure 21 shows an example, For NSTTF heliostat 5E09, the canting angles corresponding to on-
 axis canting and off-axis canting are shown side-by-side. The needles indicate surface normals at
 each facet center, and the on-axis canting prescription (a) shows the expected symmetry.
 However, the corresponding illumination condition is not of practical interest. In contrast, the
 off-axis canting prescription (b) is constructed for an illumination condition corresponding to the
 target reference date and time, and the resulting canting angles exhibit an asymmetry which
 introduces astigmatism in the heliostat. This astigmatism varies with each heliostat, but
 magnifies solar flux at the target point and time.

 This work identifies desired goal canting angles, providing multiple benefits. Related to heliostat
 optical metrology, these canting angles provide the correct design reference to compare when
 computing canting errors. Additional results include identification of the canting variation with

 43

 time required to overcome loss of focus effects seen in the previous section, and also the optical
 ramifications of using fixed-focus heliostats with multiple receiver targets, such as the Sandia
 NSTTF field with multiple targets on the main tower, and a second G3P3 tower target. This work
 supports gaps M2, M4, and M5. All of these results are to be released to OpenCSP (Sandia
 National Laboratories n.d.). For additional information, see Brost and Hwang (2025) and Hwang
 and Brost (n.d.). For information on canting strategies for other heliostat kinematic designs or
 other heliostat field performance goals, see Buck and Teufel (2009) and Landman and Gauche
 (2014).

 (a) (b)

 Figure 21. Comparing on-axis canting (a) vs. off-axis canting (b).

 6.2.1.5 Ground Truth
 To achieve high solar flux intensities for high-temperature applications, CSP collectors must
 have high optical accuracy, which in turn requires accurate metrology. A variety of CSP optical
 metrology systems have been developed, including März et al. (2011), Andraka et al. (2014),
 Montecchi et al. (2022), Kesseli et al. (2022), and Brost, Smith, Brimigion, et al. (2024). These
 sophisticated systems include complex calibrations and calculations to deduce optical
 parameters. How do we know if these methods are accurate?

 One approach to assessing measurement accuracy is to identify a ground truth reference which is
 known to be accurate, and compare metrology system measurements against that reference. We
 seek simple ground truth references to enable CSP metrology developers to cross-check the
 accuracy of their systems. These fall into two categories:

 • Ground truth methods. Simple and inherently accurate.
 • Ground truth physical standards. Physical objects with known metric properties.
 Figure 22 shows an example ground truth method that exploits a basic property of optical self-
 reflection for spherical mirrors (Strachan 1992). The figure includes a cross-check between a
 directly-captured point spread function and a prediction produced from a SOFAST measurement
 (Brost, Smith, Brimigion, et al. 2024). Figure 23 shows two physical ground truth standards. Part
 (a) shows a water pool, which can be used to check metrology systems that measure mirrors
 indoors in a face-up orientation (März et al. 2011; Montecchi et al. 2022). Part (b) shows a high-
 precision mirror fabricated using optical imaging techniques, with a 255 m radius of curvature,
 and absolute optical accuracy better than 0.04 mrad. In a later section we present use of these
 ground truth standards to cross-check the accuracy of SOFAST 2.0.

 Ground truth supports other metrology tools, by enabling verification of absolute accuracy.
 Capabilities span laboratory, outdoor under varying conditions, manufacturing settings, and in
 situ measurements. Thus ground truth work supports gaps M1, M2, M3, M4, and M5. However,
 some tests still require ground truth references. For additional information, see Brost et al.
 (2025).

   6.2.1.6 OpenCSP
 At SolarPACES 2024, we announced the launch of OpenCSP (Sandia National Laboratories
 n.d.), an open-source platform including source code, applications, and data to enable
 collaborative development for the CSP community worldwide. It is designed to support industry,
 research, and education. OpenCSP initially includes these main components:
 • OpenCSP_Code – Both foundation classes for building new programs and ready-to-run
 applications, including Sandia’s SOFAST 2.0 code (Brost, Smith, Brimigion, et al. 2024),
 all in Python. The development environment includes several features to support effective
 team code development, including automatic document generation, automatic testing, and
 issue tracking.
 • OpenCSP_Data – Large data sets for research, optical targets to support metrology
 ground truth tests (Brost et al. 2025), and test data for OpenCSP_Code.
 • OpenCSP_Mechanical – Includes an interactive CAD tool for designing deflectometry
 layouts (Brimigion et al. 2024), plus a gallery of CAD models to support collaborative
 CSP research.

 46

 • OpenCSP_Tools – Non-code tools to aid CSP analysis and understanding.
 • OpenCSP_Documents – Documents supporting OpenCSP and related topics.
 All of these are provided with an open-source license allowing unlimited use, requiring
 acknowledgement (see license files for details).

 The goals of OpenCSP are to:
 • Accelerate transfer of state-of-the-art CSP metrology and analysis tools to industry,
 eliminating barriers to access.
 • Provide a resource for businesses seeking to support CSP development.
 • Enable the code and data to contribute to education.
 • Provide a community collaborative development environment to enable teams to build
 new advanced CSP applications more quickly, and to speed their deployment.
 OpenCSP initially focuses on CSP optics, and currently includes over 50 foundation classes and
 over 82,000 lines of code, and over 290 GB of data. Figure 24 and Figure 25 show examples of
 OpenCSP output, and data. In Table 4 and Table 8, results marked * have been released to
 OpenCSP, and results marked ** are planned to be released to OpenCSP. Some released results,
 such as SOFAST 2.0 and the high-precision calibration mirror, are complete high-TRL results.
 Others, such as the UFACET image processing pipeline, are in a rougher state. We continue to
 add content.

 By disseminating metrology tools and data addressing multiple problems, OpenCSP reduces
 metrology gaps M1, M2, M3, M4, and M5. For additional information, see Sandia National
 Laboratories (n.d.) and Brost, Bean, et al. (2024).

 6.2.2 Metrology: Deflectometry
 Deflectometry is the use of reflection to measure mirror optical properties. The general approach
 is to place an optical target (either fixed or active, such as a display) in front of a mirror, and then
 view the reflection of the target with a camera. After capturing one or a series of images,
 computer software analyzes the image sequence to deduce mirror slope for many points across
 the mirror. Relative positions of target, mirror and camera need to be known for this.

 Deflectometry systems developed at Sandia and DLR have been used to measure CSP mirrors
 for years, along with others (März et al. 2011; Andraka et al. 2014; Montecchi et al. 2022;
 Yellowhair et al. 2011). HelioCon has two active projects developing deflectometry systems with
 advanced capabilities – SOFAST 2.0 and ReTNA. The following sections will review progress
 made with these systems, and also briefly discuss the CSP Services deflectometry system for
 context.

   6.2.2.1 QDec
 CSP Services offers the high-quality QDec deflectometry system for CSP mirror measurement.
 QDec-M (CSP Services, n.d.-a) is designed for production quality control, and the QDec-H
 (Ulmer 2011) system measures heliostats in the field. Both are fringe deflectometry systems.
 QDec-H measures heliostats in a single configuration at night. Figure 26 shows images of QDec
 systems.

 QDec-M is relevant to metrology gaps M1 and M3, while QDec-H is relevant to metrology gaps
 M2 and M5. For additional information on CSP Services deflectometry systems, see CSP
 Services (n.d.-b), CSP Services (n.d.-a), and Ulmer et al. (2011).

 48

                                                             Ulmer, et al. 2011.

 Figure 26. The CSP Services QDec system (CSP Services, n.d.-a; n.d.-b).

   6.2.2.2 SOFAST 2.0
 An example is the original SOFAST deflectometry system, which was developed at Sandia
 National Laboratories by Chuck Andraka and his colleagues (Andraka et al. 2014; Yellowhair et
 al. 2011). This system was successfully used to measure mirrors for dishes, troughs, and
 heliostats.

 Over the course of Heliocon we have created and released SOFAST 2.0, a complete
 reimplementation of the original SOFAST program with many improvements:

 • Python language, maintainable, extensible code.
 • Control interface via Python script API, command line, or GUI all supported.
 • Separate data acquisition and analysis, storing intermediate computation stages.
 • Modern software engineering practice, including automated testing.
 • Flexible for a variety of application scenarios.
 • Allows a broader range of installation and operation conditions (Smith and Brost 2024).
 • Simplified calibration, including 3-d screen characterization (Smith and Brost 2024).
 • Hybrid deflectometry: Both fringe-based and fixed-pattern, enabling measurement in
 ambient light or darkness (Brost, Smith, and Bean 2024).
 • Computation of difference between SOFAST measurements.
 • Multiple analytic outputs.
 • Built-in ray-tracing, based on measured data.
 • Multi-facet capability.
 • Interactive CAD tool for layout (Brimigion et al. 2024).
 • Supporting tools: Mirror vergence angle, largest allowable error.
 • Measurement of optical effects of temperature (Brost, Brimigion, Smith, et al. 2024).
 • Ground truth verification (Brost et al. 2025).
 The paragraphs that follow will explain some of the key advances mentioned above.

 Flexibility. Augmented by its companion CAD layout tool (Brimigion et al. 2024), the new
 SOFAST can be applied in a wide variety of configurations. Figure 27 shows some examples.

 49

 All of these were laid out with the SOFAST CAD Layout tool, and have been successfully
 implemented and tested. Note that the outdoor implementation (e) is still under development.

 (a) (b) (c)

 (d) (e)

 Figure 27. Example SOFAST configurations (Brimigion et al. 2024)
 SOFAST 2.0’s flexibility also enables low-cost implementations using hardware that is often
 already on hand. Figure 28 shows a SOFAST implementation using a laptop computer’s built-in
 camera to provide SOFAST functionality with no additional hardware required; this is well-
 suited to education lab curricula. A similar setup using a webcam and a larger monitor has also
 been tested, enabling low-cost measurement of larger mirrors.

 50

 camera

 Figure 28. SOFAST laptop installation for small, short focal length mirrors and education.
 Robustness. SOFAST 2.0 includes several improvements to relax its requirements to simplify
 installation. These include the ability to perform fringe deflectometry in limited amounts of
 ambient light (as opposed to fixed deflectometry in full ambient light; see below), and screen
 calibration that allows screens to be both less flat and less clean than previously required. To see
 the full list of robustness improvements, see Smith and Brost (2024).

 Hybrid deflectometry. The original SOFAST performed fringe deflectometry only, to produce
 high-resolution measurements using a projector in a darkened room. In some applications this is
 undesirable, so we extended SOFAST to also perform fixed deflectometry using a printed
 pattern. This enables SOFAST to operate under ambient light conditions, without a projector.
 This is useful in factory settings with limited space for creating a darkened test room, or in
 buildings with skylights where turning off the lights does not achieve darkness. Fixed mode has
 faster data collection, but produces only medium spatial resolution. Fringe mode is
 fundamentally distortion tolerant, while fixed mode can be adversely affected by high distortion.
 SOFAST 2.0 also supports hybrid deflectometry, where a single implementation can use a target
 printed in light grey, and then switch between fixed and fringe modes with only a software
 change (and turning off the lights). An example where this would be useful is a factory or
 prototype facility desiring fast measurements in ambient light, but also the ability to capture
 high-resolution measurements for detailed analysis. For further details, see Brost, Smith, and
   Bean (2024).

 Multiple analytics. SOFAST 2.0 produces several analytic outputs. In addition to basic statistics
 such as measurement resolution, root mean square (RMS) error, etc., SOFAST generates high-
 resolution maps of primary mirror properties such as slope X and Y components, slope
 magnitude, and curvature X, Y, and magnitude. With the addition of desired nominal properties,
 SOFAST outputs slope error X, Y, and magnitude. With the addition of usage scenario
 information, SOFAST outputs a ray-tracing prediction of beam shape on the receiver, and
 enclosed energy as a function of receiver size. These are output in both graphical and machine-
 readable numerical format. This ensemble provides information not only about the mirror’s
 optical properties, but also the energy capture implications of those properties. Figure 29 shows
 an example standard layout of these results for an NSTTF facet measurement, presented in Brost
 (2023a). This arrangement was manually generated; automatic generation of this layout is work
 in progress.

 Optical effects of temperature. Heliostats in outdoor environments can experience a wide range
 of operating temperatures. Despite this, many heliostat mirrors combine components with
 different coefficients of thermal expansion. Measurement of the optical effects of temperature is
 a gap identified by HelioCon (M2). To investigate the optical impact of temperature change, we
 installed SOFAST 2.0 with an environmental temperature chamber, and then measured the
 optical temperature response for two different CSP facets. The physical layout of this system is
 shown in Figure 27(d) above. Figure 30(a) shows the effect of temperature change on a common
 CSP mirror design with a stamped steel back plate glued to a glass mirror. This mirror exhibits
 substantial change with temperature, resulting in substantial degradation in beam quality. Figure
 30(b) shows the impact on energy collection for an example heliostat placement. For the
 illustrated example situation, temperature change causes up to 17% loss in energy capture. This
 phenomenon merits attention, because we estimate that there are currently 4.3 × 106 m2 of
 mirrors of this type installed worldwide (NREL n.d.). Figure 30(c) compares the measured
 optical impact of temperature change for the stamped-back mirror vs. a different glass-sandwich
 mirror design, demonstrating that mirror design can reduce this effect. For further detail, see
 Brost, Brimigion, et al. (2024).

 Optical effects of tilt. Heliostats are known to change shape as they tilt through different
 elevation angles (Yuan et al. 2015). However, measurement of the optical effects of tilt angle is a
 gap identified by HelioCon (M2). To assess the optical effects of varying elevation angle, we
 used the SOFAST CAD layout tool (Brimigion et al. 2024) to develop a design approach for
 laying out SOFAST systems capable of measuring mirror slope maps at varying tilt angles. We
 instantiated this design approach to design a SOFAST hybrid deflectometry system to measure
 the new SolarDynamics heliostat at elevation angles of 4°, 25°, 30°, 35°, 40°, 45°, 55°, and 60°.
 Example measurement configurations are shown in Figure 31. This implementation has been
 constructed; testing is in progress (NREL 2025). Preliminary measurement results are shown in

 for the precise mirror, but only after fine adjustment of SOFAST control parameters. Note that
 these are form errors; ground truth indicated that optical power errors are higher. This
 demonstrates the value of ground truth – identifying areas of improvement for a complex
 metrology system.

 form errors; optical power errors are higher.
 Note that the above RMS values should not be interpreted as a general statement of SOFAST
 accuracy. Accuracy can vary with configuration and other conditions. In ongoing work, we are
 pursuing elimination of the abovementioned parameter adjustments, and automatic estimation of
 accuracy for a given SOFAST configuration.

 Impact. At this point, five companies have either used, installed, or indicated a plan to use
 SOFAST, and five more have expressed interest. For example, one company used SOFAST to
 support both protype development and their high-volume manufacturing line which produced
 over 8,000 mirrors.

 To recap, SOFAST 2.0 delivers rapid, high-resolution slope maps under a variety of
 measurement conditions, including high reflection distortion. SOFAST 2.0 is available to the
 public open-source as part of the OpenCSP community development environment (Brost, Bean,
 Brimigion, et al. 2024). It is intended to support all sectors of the CSP community, including
 industry, research, and education.

 Because SOFAST 2.0 supports both indoor prototype environments and manufacturing
 applications, SOFAST 2.0 addresses metrology gaps M1 and M3. Because it enables assessment
 of the optical effects of temperature and tilt angle, it also supports metrology gap M2. Thus
 SOFAST 2.0 supports gaps M1, M2, and M3. For additional information, see Brost, Smith,
 Brimigion, et al. (2024) and the references noted in the above text and figures. For detailed
 technical information, see the technical documents Smith, Brost, and Bean (2024d); Smith,
 Brost, and Bean (2024b); Smith, Brost, and Bean (2024c); Smith, Brost, and Bean (2024a); and
 Brimigion and Brost (2024).

   6.2.2.3 ReTNA
 Since the start of HelioCon, NLR began developing a printed target deflectometry system, to act
 as a secondary system to compare with SOFAST, and to fill a gap between high resolution and
 precision laboratory fringe deflectometry methods like SOFAST, and outdoor methods like NIO
 or UFACET. This tool was initially called NIO-a and later renamed Reflected Target

 56

 Nonintrusive Assessment (ReTNA). The initial version of ReTNA used printed black dots to
 measure 2D surface slope, and was presented at SolarPACES in 2022 (Kesseli et al. 2022). Since
 then, it has undergone many changes and been applied across research and industry. As it has
 expanded, some applications of this tool have been funded under other projects outside of
 HelioCon. When designing ReTNA, the major design objectives were:

 1. Measurement with printed targets, in ambient lighting.

 2. Photogrammetric calibration of the target surface shape, removing the need for a
 perfectly flat target.

 3. Methodology that supports measurement with a smaller target, combining multiple
 images to measure a heliostat surface or any size.

 These were determined by the metrology gaps identified in HelioCon, discussions with
 commercial partners, and laboratory space limitations at NLR. The ReTNA system described in
 Kesseli et al. (2022) met this criteria. Figure 34 shows two actual ReTNA targets, with the z-
 direction (out of the target plane) exaggerated to show distortion. Each deflection target point,
 marked by a white dot in the image, is solved independently in space, so this distortion in the
 target does not impart error in the surface slope measurement.

 Figure 34. 3D photogrammetry reconstructions of actual ReTNA targets, with the z-direction (out
 of the plane) exaggerated to show distortion.
 ReTNA changes and improvements. Since ReTNA’s initial implementation, it has undergone
 significant changes and improvements. The target slowly evolved to an array of coded computer
 vision targets. ReTNA moved in this direction to address some challenges identifying which dot
 is which, both during calibration and in the deflected measurement, and to make target points
 into single points (each corner of each computer vision target) instead of a centroid, but each
 strategy has advantages and disadvantages. Comparison with SOFAST fixed (and fringe) will be
 valuable to both tools.

 57

 Figure 35. ReTNA target evolution over time.
 ReTNA verification

 Significant effort has gone toward verifying ReTNA in different environments and conditions.
 For the original system, ReTNA slope results were compared qualitatively with SOFAST,
 however issues reconciling the two datasets prevented a quantitative comparison. ReTNA facet
 canting results were compared with a high precision digital level and showed agreement within
 the uncertainty of the level.

 More recently, we demonstrated consistent measurement in a wide range of lighting conditions,
 on soiled and smudged mirrors, at varied target heights and camera positions, changed camera
 settings, and after moving and rotating the mirrors. This work was completed at the request of a
 commercial partner to validate the commercial setup, and many results cannot be shared
 publicly. Many of these tests have been repeated on NLR’s ReTNA setup, for an upcoming
 publication.

 As mentioned in the ground truth cross checks section above, it is essential to compare results
 against a known ground truth surface. Now that ReTNA is a more established tool and Sandia
 has developed a ground truth mirror and procedure, comparing ReTNA and SOFAST with this
 ground truth surface is a priority. Though also as discussed above, a measurement system’s
 uncertainty can be highly dependent on the specific setup.

 Adapting ReTNA to new measurement scenarios

 Since its inception, ReTNA has been demonstrated on a commercial heliostat assembly line, in a
 commercial heliostat developers R&D facility, and has supported various heliostat projects in the
 laboratory. Three different layouts are shown in Figure 36.

 58

 Figure 36. Several different ReTNA layouts that have been demonstrated in the last 18 months.
 Image 1 in Figure 36 shows an implementation of ReTNA installed at NLR. This compact
 system was put in right in front of a heating chamber, to support composite sandwich facet
 development work. A mirror is removed from the heating chamber, and single-image ReTNA
 measurements are collected as it cools. These results have helped assess new composite facet
 constructions and will be presented with that work.

 Image 2 in Figure 36 is a diagram of the commercial quality control ReTNA layout demonstrated
 at a commercial heliostat facility. This setup underwent a strict series of tests to show it could
 meet commercial heliostat quality control needs during manufacturing.

 Finally, Image 3 in Figure 36 shows the unique ReTNA system developed for Solar Dynamics
 LLC, to measure their heliostat on the assembly jig, and adjust the jig accordingly. This layout
 was deployed last fall, and after some important code improvements, has been used to
 successfully measure the heliostat. This required new image stitching algorithms to combine
 over 40 images for one heliostat measurement, and uses a new wireless camera control system
 shown in Figure 37.

 Figure 37. New ReTNA layout supporting measurement of the Solar Dynamics SunLite commercial
 heliostat, layout 3 in the previous image.

 59

 Figure 38. Proof-of-concept ReTNA measurement on the NLR HFSF heliostat.
 Figure 38 shows a proof-of-concept ReTNA system used to measure a portion of the NLR high
 flux solar furnace (HFSF) heliostat. A more mobile version of this system able to measure the
 whole heliostat, along with a new truck-mounted system, will be demonstrated this year.

 Impact and Metrology Gaps

 As described above, ReTNA is being actively used by heliostat research and industry. In the last
 year, ReTNA results were delivered to both commercial partners, composite facet design work at
 NLR, to the NLR HFSF team. Just in 2025, we’ve delivered 3 full characterizations of the 27m2
 SunLite heliostat, and 7 sets of surface slope and temperature deformation measurements to the
 composite facet design team. We have plans to measure the full 25m2 HFSF heliostat and
 heliostats at a commercial plant with ReTNA later this quarter. This further impact has only been
 limited by scheduling and NLR staff bandwidth.

 This work was designed to address several of the identified metrology gaps:

 • M1: opto-mechanical error measurement in the laboratory. To provide a
 secondary metrology technique to evaluate against SOFAST and established
 technique, meeting the M1 need of allowing these systems to be validated against
 each other. A versatile printed target system also allows for measurement at varied
 orientation angle and under simulated loads.

 • M2: outdoor opto-mechanical measurement. ReTNA was designed to fill a gap
 between high resolution and precision laboratory fringe deflectometry methods, and
 outdoor methods like NIO, HelioPoint, or UFACET. It uses printed targets so it can
 take measurement in bright outdoor lighting, and significant effort has been made to
 image large heliostats by stitching together images of smaller targets.

 • M3: quality assurance tools for mass production. ReTNA is well suited to fast
 lower resolution measurement on a manufacturing line, given that it works in bright

 60

 ambient lighting and measures 2D slope with a single image. It has been
 demonstrated on one heliostat assembly line, and at one commercial heliostat R&D
 facility.

 For additional information, see Kesseli et al. (2022), Kesseli et al. (2023), and Kesseli et al.
 (2024).

 6.2.3 Metrology: Field
 Optical metrology in the field is a particularly challenging task for the CSP industry, requiring
 high-accuracy optical measurements made in harsh environments, over long distances, and in the
 presence of confounding factors like sunlight/shadows, dust on the mirrors, etc. This section
 discusses progress made on a number of such outdoor optical metrology systems.

 6.2.3.1 Beam Characterization System (BCS)
 The Beam Characterization System (BCS) employs one or more cameras looking at a BCS
 target, which is typically a large white flat area on the tower (Strachan 1992). Heliostat reflected
 beams are aimed one at a time onto the target, and the camera captures images of the resulting
 bright spots, which are then analyzed to determine spot centroid and other properties. The
 resulting observed spot centroids are compared to desired aim points, to calculate an aim point
 error. This process is repeated to provide multiple aim point error observations for each heliostat,
 which are then input to regression fitting calculations to infer kinematic calibration parameters
 for each heliostat. These kinematic parameters are then applied during heliostat tracking to
 reduce tracking error (Khalsa et al. 2011). BCS heliostat calibration is currently employed in
 many CSP plants worldwide, for example the Crescent Dunes Solar Energy Project (Ayres et al.
 2019).

 BCS is a widely used technology, but it is limited by several issues:
 • Information loss – no direct heliostat slope or shape information.
 • Slow – measures one heliostat at a time.
 • Beam shape varies through the day, making error interpretation more complex.
 • Since BCS targets are typically below the receiver, and since heliostats are generally
 placed to achieve tight packing while avoiding blocking from heliostats to the receiver,
 the lower BCS target is subject to occlusion from the lower portion of some heliostats,
 especially for distant heliostats. This can cause BCS calibration methods to only consider
 a portion of the heliostat surface, compromising accuracy.
 In addition to Sandia’s extensive BCS database in OpenCSP, NLR and Sandia are participating
 in a BCS comparison study with international partners at PSA, CENER, and several other
 international plants and research institutions employing BCS systems to compare image
 processing techniques and results on shared images. The objective of this work is to compare
 methodologies and compile best practices for data collection, image processing, and
 measurement conventions for research and industry. Early results of this work show wide
 variation in all of these. Reconciling these differences will make quality BCS measurement more
 accessible to new and existing plants.

 The BCS system takes measurements relevant to metrology gaps M2, M4, and M5, subject to the
 limitations listed above. We describe it here to establish context for the following alternative

 61

 technologies, which seek to improve upon BCS. For additional information on BCS calibration,
   see Strachan (1992), Khalsa et al. (2011), and Ayres et al. (2019).

 (a) (b) (c)
 Figure 39. Example BCS analysis. (a) Sandia NSTTF BCS camera. (b) Spot on NSTTF tower. (c)
 Crescent Dunes calibration (courtesy of Mark Ayres).

   6.2.3.2 SOHOT
 SOHOT was not funded by HelioCon, but it is important to mention because its real-time
 heliostat tracking capabilities are directly relevant to heliostat metrology gaps identified in the
 original HelioCon roadmap.

 SOHOT is a system developed by Heliogen for measuring and correcting heliostat pointing
 angles in real time during operation. It uses a set of four cameras surrounding the receiver aim
 point, and a fifth camera pointed at the sun. The fifth camera measures the instantaneous
 circumsolar radiation distribution, while the four cameras surrounding the receiver measure the
 reflected solar intensity of many heliostats simultaneously. Then for each observed heliostat, the
 intensities observed by the four cameras are compared in a calculation to deduce pointing error.
 This system measures multiple heliostats in parallel, and can operate continuously while the
 heliostat field is operation. Figure 40 shows a few images of the SOHOT system from Sonn et al.
 (2022). In addition to Heliogen’s experience using SOHOT with their heliostats at their
 Lancaster heliostat field, the SOHOT system was also tested at Sandia’s NSTTF, and
 successfully achieved a tracking error of 0.33 mrad for Sandia’s heliostats (Heliogen 2023).
 Since Sandia’s heliostats have multiple facets, SOHOT was able to also measure facet canting
 angles in addition to heliostat pointing.

 62

 (a) (b)
 Figure 40. The Heliogen SOHOT system (Sonn et al. 2022). (a) Cameras surrounding receiver and
 BCS target (red) and looking at the sun (yellow). (b) Example image seen by camera looking back
 at heliostat field.
 The SOHOT system measures heliostat pointing errors outdoors and in situ, and thus addresses
 gaps M2, M4, and M5. For additional information, see Sonn et al. (2022) and Heliogen (2023).

   6.2.3.3 HelioPoint
 HelioPoint was not funded by HelioCon, but it is important to mention because its drone-based
 method of estimating pointing accuracy for heliostats in situ is directly relevant to heliostat
 metrology gaps identified in the original HelioCon roadmap.

 HelioPoint is a system developed by DLR and CSP Services for measuring heliostat pointing
 accuracy, and thus obtaining information to support heliostat calibration to improve pointing. It
 utilizes a drone equipped with a camera and one or more color LED lights, and maneuvers the
 drone so that the camera can see the drone’s lights in reflection (Figure 41(a)) (Jessen et al.
 2022). By accurately estimating the (x, y, z) position of the drone, the system can deduce the
 local mirror surface normal at the point where the reflection is seen. By maneuvering the drone
 to obtain several observations, the surface normal for multiple points on the mirror can be
 measured. These are then used to deduce actual heliostat pointing angle. If the heliostat
 configurations are chosen appropriately, multiple heliostats may be measured simultaneously,
 increasing measurement speed (Figure 41(b) (Krauth et al. 2024). The HelioPoint system has
 achieved reported measurement accuracy of 0.3 mrad.

 63

 (a) (b)

 Figure 41. The DLR HelioPoint system (Jessen et al. 2022; Krauth et al. 2024). (a) Drone carrying
 color LEDs, seeing its own reflection in a heliostat. (b) Measuring multiple heliostats
 simultaneously.
 The HelioPoint system measures heliostat pointing errors outdoors and in situ, and thus
 addresses gaps M2, M4, and M5. For additional information, see Jessen et al. (2022) and Krauth
   et al. (2024).

   6.2.3.4 NIO
 The Non-Intrusive Optical method (NIO) is a tool that measures the optical error of heliostats by
 observing the deflection of the reflected tower edge from videos collected from an unmanned
 aircraft system (UAS) (Mitchell and Zhu 2020). Measured tower edge deflections are broken
 down into slope, canting, and tracking errors. Collecting data by UAS (unmanned aircraft)
 allows for fast, large-scale data collection of heliostats in situ, without taking them out of
 operation. This work began in a separate project before HelioCon, and has been supported by
 HelioCon and another project over the last few years.

 Figure 42. Basic NIO workflow. UAS flights capture the tower edge sweeping across heliostats,
 which are used to measure optical error.
 NIO has been used to collect data at two commercial CSP plants (Crescent Dunes in the United
 States and Cerro Dominador in Chile), and two CSP research facilities (NSTTF and Plataforma
 Solar de Almería (PSA) in Spain). Most recently, data collection at the Cerro Dominador plant

 64

 was used to demonstrate commercial scale data collection. NIO data was collected on 1100
 heliostats, 10% of the field, and reached up to 100 heliostats per flight. This work also used UAS
 contractors to collect data, and a Flight Planner user interface automatically plans flights on in
 situ heliostats based on project objectives and measurability. Applying NIO to new plants and
 streamlining data collection is an important step to addressing metrology gap M5, However there
 are still some significant barriers to processing data at scale, which is necessary to address this
 gap.

 The most recent data collected at PSA is being used for some much-needed NIO repeatability
 and verification testing and software improvements, by verifying repeated NIO measurement
 yields the same results, and comparing NIO tracking error results to BCS. Broadening NIO
 beyond the plants it was initially developed for has led to significant new functionality to deal
 with new tower shapes, image processing with different tower and ground colors, and more
 rigorous software control to make the software broadly applicable to the CSP industry.

 This work supports metrology gaps M2, M4 and M5. NIO can provide measurements on
 installed outdoor heliostats under realistic impact factors, such as temperature and a range of
 orientations on installed heliostats (M2 and M4). Recent work described above has also
 demonstrated NIO’s ability to collect large scale data at commercial scale, though there are still
 some barriers to processing at this scale.

 A second challenge for NIO measurements is that currently, errors can only be measured in the
 direction of movement of the tower reflection across the mirror, The second error dimension
 must be measured at a different orientation of the heliostat (e.g., different time of day). We are
 working on adding capabilities for in situ, two-dimensional error characterization.

 For additional information, see Mitchell and Zhu (2020) and Farrell et al. (2021).

 6.2.3.5 Flatirons Outdoor Testing Site
 With the goal of creating a testing platform that can support outdoor heliostat testing as well as
 improving and verifying our metrology techniques, HelioCon supported preparation of NLR’s
 Flatirons campus site. This included:
 • The determination of 50,000-ft2 land area and infrastructure, including a data shed,
 generic control system access and the installation of a 10x10 foot BCS target.
 • Heliogen generously donated nine heliostats, four of which were installed at the site in
           April 2025.
 • Laying the groundwork for DOE to fund a separate spinoff third-party heliostat
 metrology platform project with the goal to reduce market entry barriers for heliostat
 developers by providing the metrology capabilities and support standardization of
 procedures.
 This site has consistent and predominantly eastward wind, making it a good candidate for
 heliostat wind load testing. The platform has the potential to address gaps M2, M4, M5, S1, S2,
 S4 and S6. It will also support work toward integrating the metrology tools that were developed
 under HelioCon into the larger picture so that results become meaningful in their interpretation.

 65

 Figure 43. An annotated illustration of potential capabilities at Flatirons Campus analyzed in the
 scoping study. These were down selected based measurement objectives.

   6.2.3.6 UFACET
 The UFACET project was initially funded by another project, and then UFACET development
 continued under HelioCon.
 The UFACET system was designed to measure heliostat slope maps and canting of an entire
 heliostat field at high speed. The UFACET flight planner automatically computed a drone
 scanning flight path over the field to obtain video images of the heliostats sweeping under the
 drone’s camera, with geometry selected so that the back side of nearby heliostats would be seen
 in reflection, with their reflections seen passing vertically across the mirror. The design goal was
 to use these reflections to deduce slope error maps, infer facet canting angles, and potentially
 estimate heliostat pointing errors.
 The UFACET flight planner and flight operation/data collection procedures performed well,
 successfully collecting good quality data over many flights at multiple solar fields. Data post-
 processing was successful in tracking heliostats and performing photogrammetric 3-d shape
 analysis to estimate small facet dislocations, rough canting angles, and rough heliostat poses.
 Figure 44 shows some example images of the UFACET system.
 While the UFACET system made good progress toward accomplishing its goals, we decided to
 pause development of the post-processing software and re-assess lessons learned and the
 system’s potential for providing a high-performance solution to the problem of accelerated full-
 field heliostat calibration (metrology gap M4), one of its key design goals. After some
 deliberation, we decided to shelve the project, and pursue an alternative approach that seemed
 better positioned to satisfy all of the constraints required for an effective, high-performance
 solution. (This “lookback” approach is discussed later.) The analysis that led to this decision is
 briefly summarized below.
 UFACET successes:
 • High speed data collection, roughly 3 seconds per heliostat.

 66

 • Flight path avoids high flux, informed by flux hazard analysis.
 • Distortion effects successfully limited.
 • Ubiquitous optical target.
 • Successful automated heliostat tracking (on one data set, given manual seeds).
 • Successful automated 3-d facet pose recovery, heliostat field state estimation.
 • (Processing of optical reflections never accomplished.)
 UFACET issues:
 • Not fast enough, due to enroute time, drone recovery time, often drone cooling interval.
 • The need to capture many heliostat observations implied not necessarily faster than BCS.
 • Weather sensitive, potentially increasing delay, or making afternoon measurements less
 frequent and therefore calibration data basis imbalanced.
 • Transform required from optical target to receiver aim point target.
 • Very large data corpus to post-process (terabytes).
 • Reasonably likely need to modify/write new code for each new site.
 • Safety concerns – potential to break heliostats, personnel safety.
 • Regulatory variation for drone operations in different locales.
 • Specialized training required.
 • Operation complexity.
 • Above factors suggest a potential traveling service organization approach, increasing cost
 and impeding response speed after major weather events, etc.
 • UFACET’s use of nearby heliostats as optical targets will result in reduced angular
 precision as heliostat size decreases.
 This work was designed to support metrology gaps M4 and M5. For additional information, see
 Brost et al. (2023) and Brost, Small, et al. (2024).

 (a) (b) (c)
 Figure 44. The UFACET system for drone-based heliostat field assessment (Brost, Small, Novick,
 et al. 2024). (a) Drone flight path. (b) Heliostat tracking. (c) Heliostat pose estimate.

   6.2.3.7 Lookback
 In response to lessons learned from the foundational studies, SOFAST, UFACET, and the prior
 work of others (Goldberg and Zisken 2015; Collins et al. 2017). Sandia has designed a new
 approach to in situ heliostat field assessment. This approach was selected from a competitive
 phase-gate analysis we performed in FY24, and utilizes a camera looking back at the heliostat
 field, and is designed to measure medium-resolution heliostat slope maps and pointing errors at
 high speed, while avoiding problems encountered by other methods. If successful, it would

 67

 reduce metrology gaps M1, M2, M4, and M5. A conceptual design has been developed with
 initial critical parameter analysis and calculation, and also evaluation of potential solutions for
 critical components. This is still in early stages of development.

 6.2.4 Standards

   6.2.4.1 Overview
 Standards are important in any product development. They are technical rules and guidelines that
 help reduce barriers to trade, ensure quality and risk management and form the basis for testing
 and certification. Since the development of standards is a collective, global effort, they reflect
 global consensus by technical experts from industry and research, who are delegated by their
 countries. The governing entity for global standards on electrical technologies is the International
 Electrotechnical Commission (IEC), which often works closely with the International
 Organization for Standardization (ISO), which covers a wider range of fields. Within this
 framework, Technical Committees work on drafting and publications for specific topics.

 Since CSP technology is competing with other available energy sources, such as fossil fuels,
 photovoltaics (PV), and wind energy, standards are critical references for investors to examine
 bankability of a given commercial system. Recognizing the importance of this, the IEC Spanish
 National Committee established that Technical Committee TC 117 – “Solar Thermal Electric
 Plants” over a decade ago.

 Table 5 gives an overview of the released publications and all currently active drafting projects
 under TC 117. Of the published documents, only one is related to solar tower technology and
 most are designed for line-focusing systems (e.g., parabolic trough and linear Fresnel). Even the
 terms and definitions outlined in TS 62862-1-1 do not include definitions related to heliostats. It
 is our goal to include those in the revised version, which is currently in progress. However, many
 of the currently active projects that are drafting standards to be released within the next few years
 do have relevance for central receiver systems and heliostats specifically. NLR is leading the
 development of PT 62862-4-3, which is nearing completion as a committee draft to enter the IEC
 review process.

 The process is slow, however, since international consensus needs to be reached for every part of
 the document and experts need to put in their time, often without funding. Additionally, there are
 some areas where the need for standards is high, but the body of research and consensus on the
 technical aspects is not at a level where it can be easily translated into a standard. This is the case
 for example, for gap S6, Site characterization. We must recognize at this point the enormous
 groundwork that SolarPACES has provided in this respect, by creating a number of guidelines
 which then form the basis for the IEC standardization efforts. Table 6 shows how many of the
 IEC standardization groups are supported by a SolarPACES guideline (Röger 2022; Schlichting
 et al. 2022; Fernández-García et al. 2020; Wolfertstetter et al. 2022; Sutter et al. 2016; 2024).

 We can see in Table 6 how coming standards for mirror reflectance and accelerated ageing
 testing along with field control systems and a heliostat design and test procedure will be relevant
 for heliostat technology. These standards ensure high quality and durability at the component
 level of heliostat/concentrator materials, designs and controls, to support the success and
 longevity of the whole solar plant. Recently, researchers under DLR (German Aerospace Center)

 68

 leadership created a heliostat field acceptance testing guideline (Schlichting et al. 2022), which
 will be released this summer. However, gaps in heliostat field design and site evaluation remain.

 Table 5. Overview of current IEC publications and active projects.
