 In addition to the gaps identified, key discussions were held to obtain information about how to
 address the identified gaps. The recommended pathway forward for the CSP community was
 summarized as follows:

 • Pursue advanced facet and mirror array designs. Consider extensions of past research
 developing composite mirrors.
 • Advance the (1) self-supporting facet concept, and the (2) simultaneous facet
 shape/canting assembly concept.

 108

 • Support direct collaboration between heliostat developers and manufacturing solution
 partners, starting early in the design phase.
 • Heliostat designs should include factory productivity estimates, input assumptions,
 factory capital cost, and factory operating cost.
 • Compile a catalog of manufacturing processes relevant to heliostat manufacture, with
 ways to find professional solution providers.
 • Characterize all issues affecting stow up/stow down decision.
 • Evaluate variable-focus heliostat designs and their economic trade-offs.
 • Evaluate adhesive and foam material properties for creep and other effects.
 • Develop methods for providing ground condition data for potential CSP sites.
 • Develop accelerated, cost-reducing concrete foundation fabrication methods.
 • Select a representative baseline heliostat and characterize its cost and performance.

 Over the course of two years since these recommendations for pathway forward were
 established, several points have been addressed, either directly or indirectly, through both
 laboratory research and industry collaboration.

 8.2 Related Projects and Progress Made Toward Closing Gaps
 Material relevant to advanced manufacturing overlaps broadly across HelioCon. Task 5:
 Metrology and Standards, Task 6: Components and Controls, and several external projects from
 HelioCon’s Request For Proposals (RFP) have addressed gaps identified within Advanced
 Manufacturing.

 8.2.1 Components and Controls

 A main focus of Task 6: Components and Controls is on composite structures. Particularly, with
 structural components and with composite mirror facets. With composite mirror facets, gap AM1
 is being directly addressed. For successful development of composite mirror facets, it is
 absolutely imperative that adhesive creep and polymer foam core durability be well understood.
 Thus work in Components and Controls should also close gap AM9 should a composite facet
 design be qualified to reach commercial manufacturing scale.

 8.2.2 University of Arizona Twisting Heliostat

 Composite mirror facets are only one approach to innovate in the facet area. An innovative
 project from the RFP subtask supports the University of Arizona heliostat design that can
 passively adjust its optical prescription to mitigate astigmatism effects throughout the day
 (Figure 19) (Vagher et al. 2024). Future work in both of these projects will naturally progress
 toward closing gap AM2. Further, validation work for the economic benefits of optical
 prescription tuning will be carried out if not done already, closing AM6.

 8.2.3 Metrology and Standards

 Major leaps forward in Task 5: Metrology and Standards for optical quality testing tools
 developed specifically for integration with the factory assembly line include SOFAST 2.0

 109

 (Figure 27) and ReTNA (Figure 34). Both of these systems have been implemented into Solar
 Dynamics’ facet assembly line at their production facility. This represents major progress toward
 closing gap AM8. It is recognized that gap AM7 remains unaddressed up to this point, however
 planned future developments for in situ heliostat field assessment are already in early stages to
 close this gap (ref. 6.2.3 – Lookback).

 8.2.4 Baseline Reference Heliostat Design

 As a part of the roadmap update report, a new subtask is proposed for designing, fabricating, and
 evaluating a reference heliostat design for the purpose of broadcasting results to industry to be
 used as a benchmark tool. Within the proposed work for the baseline heliostat task are several
 component features that directly address AM10 and AM11 (see Section 13.2). Notwithstanding,
 the main objective of the baseline heliostat directly addresses AM12.

 8.3 Gap Updates and Priorities Supporting Intermediate Goal
 As mentioned previously the purpose of the Advanced Manufacturing task is to reduce cost in
 components and processes across the entire heliostat field. Typically these cost decreases are a
 result of economies of scale forcing manufacturing to become faster and more efficient. Though,
 at present, demand for heliostats is not sufficient to drive such efficiencies.

 One approach to drive a cost reduction in components would be to modularize heliostats to a
 point where commonality between various manufacturers for a single component drives down
 the cost of that component. Similarly, cost reduction in processes could be achieved by
 modularizing the heliostat field. Thus, a modular approach to design may be the starting point to
 creating a demand pipeline.

 Furthermore, development of a domestic supply chain for certain heavy components may also be
 necessary for reduction in transportation costs.

 With the current state of the market, manufacturing technology and heliostat cost-performance
 (i.e., the product and the process) must both make small incremental advancements in parallel to
 surmount a chicken-or-the-egg scaling paradox and ultimately achieve a thriving CSP industry.

 8.3.1 Achieving Results by Progress Made in Other Tasks

 For tasks that seek to develop new technologies it is imperative that a process is developed in
 parallel. For example, if composite mirrors are to be a success, it will not be without developing
 a process for manufacturing which guarantees optical performance at mass production scales. As
 such, the burden to develop the process ties directly into the success of the project.

 With cooperative efforts based on the below recommendations for support from industry
 professionals experienced in high volume composites manufacturing, it may be possible to
 introduce optical quality control processes to result in mass production of composite mirror
 facets without power losses.

 Similarly, the heliostat baseline design task would follow closely to the recommended pathway
 outlined herein. As a result, the outcome will demonstrate the effectiveness of the
 recommendations in the heliostat baseline database.

 112

 8.3.2 Summing Up and Implications for the Path Forward

 Since many of the Advanced Manufacturing tasks are covered by projects in various other tasks
 the focus should be adjusted to support the outstanding items from the original roadmap plus
 some additional points identified along the way. The following points are based on a review of
 commercially available heliostats.

 • Manufacturing for installation:
 o Spend an extra dollar in the factory to save 3 dollars installing in the field. Labor
 time costs are more expensive than materials costs. Avoid the pitfall of shaving
 every last cent of material out of designs to meet the cost-optimum per square
 meter of reflective surface.
 o With proven design for easier field installation, iterate the design to shave every
 last cent of material out.
 • Pick specific industries to align components with:
 o Align to Automotive Manufacturing:
 ▪ Drives/Actuators
 ▪ Frame/ Support Structures,
 ▪ Mirrors
 ▪ Controller
 o Align to Electronics Manufacturing:
 ▪ Mirror Facet Assemblies (pick and place)
 o Align to Road/Bridge/Building Construction:
 ▪ Foundations/Pads
 ▪ Helical
 ▪ Pile driving
 ▪ Vibro-hammering
 ▪ Soil knowledge for construction

 Furthermore, it is recommended to modify tasks AM3 and AM4 to recruit highly experienced
 professionals with advanced manufacturing background and make them available as a point of
 reference for aspiring heliostat manufacturers. These professionals exist in other industries (e.g.,
 automotive) and bring their expertise to new endeavors frequently.

 Several items should be removed from the previous list as they are covered in other tasks or are
 irrelevant. Gaps related to composite mirror manufacturing should be removed in favor of
 creating collaboration with professionals that can assist in developing the manufacturing process
 as discussed above for modification of AM3 and AM4. The same applies to AM9 for
 characterizing and understanding creep in polymers, as it must be addressed for composite mirror
 facets.

 Also, AM5 is recommended to be removed due to its imposing limitation on heliostat design
 characteristics and since mirrors still get dusty either way.

 113

 Ground condition data for potential future CSP project sites can be found on an interactive
 webapp https://websoilsurvey.nrcs.usda.gov/app/ and therefore does not need to be reported on
 from within HelioCon. Data suggests that new developments in heliostats are trending toward
 driven or helical piles and away from poured concrete foundations, in conjunction with smaller
 facet areas. Therefore, no new work on concrete foundation production should be done. The
 interaction between piles and soil types is well studied in the construction industry, and
 experienced professionals can be leveraged for information in this area.

 The remaining items to be addressed are more important than ever. Heliostat design for mass
 manufacturing, with special attention given to high-performance facets, rounds out the list.
 Facets suffer the most from manufacturing and supply chain deficits, as there are no other
 industries to siphon from, and therefore, must be developed by the CSP industry for the CSP
 industry. In other words, developing a robust supply chain for the CSP industry means
 developing a robust supply chain for facets. The balance of heliostat components can come from
 other existing industries, with or without modification.

 In view of the above, the previous summary for the recommended pathway forward for the CSP
 community is updated as follows:

 • Provide direct collaboration for heliostat developers to manufacturing solution
 professionals, starting early in the design phase.

 • Align component design-for-manufacturing with specific industries to leverage existing
 high productivity manufacturing methods. For facets, adapt proven manufacturing
 methods from other industries to CSP industry needs.

 • Explore composite material combinations beyond conventional foam sandwich lamina
 yielding durable, stable, inexpensive facet assemblies (e.g., cement-polymer-glass-fiber
 monoliths) suitable for high productivity manufacturing.

 • Consider single-facet heliostats, eliminating the need for a facet alignment process in the
 manufacturing process.

 • Select a heliostat that is representative of the state of the art and characterize its cost and
 performance. Publish the resulting findings as an industry reference.

 9 Resources, Training, and Education
 Introduction
 The CSP sector, particularly its heliostat systems, faces several critical challenges that must
 be addressed for optimal technological advancement and widespread adoption. These
 challenges, identified as key gaps, include little public awareness of heliostat technologies
 and insufficient exposure to the industry among students, a lack of publicly accessible
 resources such as institutional knowledge, training materials, data, and reference materials,
 and a lack of communication and collaboration between universities, industries, and research
 institutions. Additionally, there has been limited CSP- or heliostat-focused research in
 universities. To address these challenges, the CSP community, through the HelioCon initiative
 led by NLR and SNL, has implemented comprehensive initiatives. These initiatives aim to
 develop a dedicated database of heliostat R&D resources, design fundamental training and
 education programs, and promote the expansion of heliostat-related curriculum into academia.

 9.1 Review of Tier 1 Gaps
 A deeper examination of the identified gaps reported in the last HelioCon roadmap report
 highlights their specific manifestations:
 • The Public Awareness and Student Exposure Gap is characterized by a limited
 general public understanding of heliostat technologies, insufficient exposure to CSP
 industry opportunities among students, restricted awareness of career pathways, and
 limited integration of CSP concepts into mainstream educational curricula.
 • The Resource Accessibility Gap involves restricted access to institutional knowledge,
 limited availability of comprehensive educational and training resources, fragmented
 distribution of industry and plant performance data, and inadequate centralized
 repositories for reference materials.
 • The Academic-Industry Collaboration Gap is marked by weak communication
 channels, limited collaborative research initiatives, insufficient knowledge transfer
 mechanisms, and restricted access to practical implementation experience for
 researchers.

 9.2 Related Projects and Progress Made Toward Closing Gaps
 9.2.1 Key Strategic Initiatives and Activities

 9.2.1.1 Resource Training and Education Development (RTE) Project
 The RTE project is a comprehensive approach aimed at addressing the identified gaps by
 updating and building CSP resource and educational databases and connecting them. The
 objective is to provide researchers, scholars, and students with easy access to relevant
 resources and structured educational content. All materials developed are intended to be
 accessible to the CSP industry and the public.
 • Database Development and Expansion: A central achievement is the establishment
 of a comprehensive heliostat database (compiled by Matthew Neber at NLR). This
 database currently holds 85 unique heliostat entries, encompassing concepts, designs,

 116

 prototypes, and commercial implementations gathered from publicly available
 information. This serves as a crucial milestone in creating standardized reference
 material. Simple database tools are being leveraged to find patterns and draw useful
 conclusions. Pattern recognition tools are being implemented to enable systematic
 analysis of heliostat designs. From the data, the consortium hopes to determine an
 applicable set of best practices to define baseline design requirements. Interesting
 patterns have begun to emerge. A novel classification system has been developed,
 categorizing heliostats across four distinct categories. This classification emerged as a
 taxonomy based on the type of subcomponent used in the design. The database is
 being expanded to include educational materials developed by universities. The
 project includes the development of structured access protocols for research materials
 and the implementation of knowledge management systems.
 • Educational Infrastructure Enhancement: The consortium has successfully
 integrated academic and industrial resources. This includes promoting and housing
 public short courses on CSP topics developed by Rice University and Northeastern
 University (hereinafter referred as Rice and Northeastern respectively) on the
 HelioCon webpage. The project involves implementing centralized resource
 management systems and creating standardized educational content frameworks.
 Specialized seminar series (HelioCon seminar series) featuring CSP experts from
 academia, national labs, and industry have been established. More than 30 seminars
 have been held with about 20 to 40 attendees for each. These seminars (currently led
 by Brooke Stanislawski) have received positive feedback on both content and
 coordination and continue to be hosted by the RTE team.
 • Training Programs and Industry Interface: The RTE project includes developing
 heliostat training programs for the education and training of new employees entering
 the research, academic, and industry workforces. Course materials have been
 developed at Northeastern and Rice universities. The development of these course is
 discussed is section 9.2.2 and 9.2.3. The HelioCon consortium is standardizing CSP
 training modules and will integrate industry-relevant content. Professional
 development pathways are also being established. Hosting the HelioCon seminar
 series, serves as part of establishing the industry-academia interface.
 • Intern and Communication Program: An intern and communication program has
 been established. This includes student outreach via mentorship. Mentors are found
 with the help of the Leadership Team. Researchers from both NLR and SNL have
 successfully served as mentors to students at Rice and Northeastern on CSP systems
 (parabolic through, heliostats, etc.). HelioCon will continue to identify mentors for
 students for upcoming projects. Structured mentorship frameworks are being
 developed for future expansion. Interns contribute to projects, including database
 development and the CSP student competition initiatives, working with on the
 HelioCon database and building a prototype for the competition.
 • Tool Development and Promotion: Practical tools are being developed and shared.
 The STEP 1 economic analysis tool, developed by Jeffrey Gifford et al., has been
 promoted at both Northeastern and Rice. This tool received a positive echo and will
 potentially be used as an educational tool in both universities.

 117

 9.2.1.2 Student Thermal Collegiate Competition (STCC)
 The STCC aims to increase public awareness and interest in solar thermal technologies
 through a high visibility dynamic event. It seeks to educate and engage more students at
 collegiate institutes in solar thermal technologies and connect them with solar thermal
 industries in the United States. The competition is designed to allow students to successfully
 complete all components, including prototype demonstration. It is intended to be accessible to
 students of diverse backgrounds while considering community impacts.
 • Competition Focus and Design: The competition uses a practical demonstration
 application to make the technology relatable. A coffee roaster has been chosen as the
 topic of interest for the competition. Solar parabolic dish technology has been
 identified as the most appropriate technology for the competition, following
 comprehensive literature review and SolTrace simulations performed by a spring
           Intern (FY25Q2).
 • Framework and Operations: Draft rules and guidelines for the competition have
 been issued. Comprehensive safety protocols have been drafted and developed. A
 structured competition framework is being created. Two Summer interns have been
 hired to work on the design and proof of concept for the competition (FY25Q3-4).
 Coordination is underway with the NLR Research Operations team regarding
 logistics, locations, and safety. The competition also includes integration with
 national laboratory resources and expertise.

 9.2.1.3 HelioCon Consortium Activities
 Beyond RTE and STCC, HelioCon engages in broader activities to strengthen the sector.
 These include research infrastructure development, such as establishing strategic testing and
 validation facilities and supporting industry technology advancement. Workforce
 development programs encompass student internship initiatives, formation of early career
 scientist networks, and promotion of specialized training. All these activities will be beneficial
 to both the RTE and STCC project.

 9.2.2 Training and Educational Modules Developed at Rice University

 9.2.2.1 Introduction
 Through HelioCon’s request for proposals (RFP), Rice University has led following project:

 “A Brighter Future: Education and Outreach for Small-Scale Concentrating Solar on Urban
   Brownfields” (DOE ID: 38488-009).

 The goals of this project include developing educational modules based on small-scale, real-life
 applications and research questions, creating collaborative student research teams to evaluate the
 technical potential of small-scale heliostats on urban brownfields adjacent to diverse communities,
 and assessing social dimensions, technology awareness, and acceptance for locating concentrating
 solar systems in urban brownfields. The project is structured around the following three major
 goals:

 • To develop educational modules (both in the classroom and through extracurricular
 activities) that enhance concentrating solar workforce diversity, based in small-scale and

 118

 community-centered real-life applications and research questions. This goal emphasizes
 creating learning opportunities grounded in practical, relevant problems to build a more
 diverse workforce for CSP technologies.
 • To create collaborative undergraduate and graduate student research teams with the goal
 of providing an initial evaluation of the technical potential of small-scale heliostats on
 urban brownfields. This goal focuses on hands-on research by student teams to assess the
 feasibility and performance of small-scale CSP in specific environments like urban
 brownfields.
 • To assess the social dimensions, technology awareness, and acceptance among local
 communities and groups for locating concentrating heliostat-based solar systems in urban
 brownfields. This goal addresses the human element, understanding community
 perspectives, knowledge levels, and willingness to accept CSP installations in areas like
 brownfields near where they live.
 Progress toward these goals is organized through several interconnected tasks, with key
 activities reported quarterly.

 The PI and Co-PI of this project at Rice University are Prof. Laura Schaefer and Prof. Jim
 Elliot respectively.

 9.2.2.2 Task 1.1 Design of a Survey To Assess CSP Awareness, Obstacles, Diversity,
 and Opportunities in Academia (Targeted Months 1-5)
 • Activities: This task was a foundational step to understand existing knowledge and
 perceptions of CSP among students and recent graduates. It involved a thorough
 review of existing literature on awareness studies in energy fields. The project team
 consulted with experts experienced in survey design and implementation in related
 areas, such as Allison Lenthall, who conducted survey work for SolarDynamics. A
 mechanism was created, like a Google form, to collect input from other professionals
 working in related energy disciplines to help shape the survey questions. Input was
 specifically received from a diverse group, including graduate students, postdoctoral
 researchers, and faculty from various institutions.
 • Outcome: Surveys were created for different target groups, including current two-year
 and four- year college students, recent graduates, and high school students. These
 surveys were designed to include a mix of close-ended and open-ended questions to
 gather varied types of data. The survey and its promotional materials underwent
 review by the Rice Institutional Review Board (IRB) and were approved after one
 iteration in formatting. Examples of the promotional material for the survey
 distribution are provided online at https://rice.box.com/v/HC-SurveyAdsQ2. Copies
 of the survey can be found at https://tinyurl.com/energygrads for recent graduates,
 and https://tinyurl.com/energyugs for current students. An online tool was selected for
 administering the survey, incorporating features like variation in question order to
 account for potential order effects.
 • Status and Analysis: Task 1.1 was completed ahead of its scheduled end date in
 Month 5. This early completion provided the necessary instrument to proceed with
 Task 1.3, survey distribution and collection. The comprehensive approach, including

 119

 literature review, expert consultation, and diverse input, indicates a robust design
 process for the awareness survey. The IRB approval is a critical step enabling data
 collection.

 9.2.2.3 Task 1.2 Creation of a Cross-Disciplinary Student Club To Promote CSP
 (Targeted Months 3- 11)
 • Activities: The primary activity involves identifying and recruiting students interested
 in forming a club focused on CSP. Efforts began by polling students across various
 disciplines, including STEM majors, Economics, Sociology, and students in relevant
 energy or writing courses. A core student leadership team was successfully formed,
 consisting initially of five students from STEM, one from Economics, and one from
 Sociology. Recruitment efforts expanded to include leveraging existing groups like
 the Rice University Wind Club and presenting planned activities to faculty groups.
 The team also engaged with participants from other solar initiatives, such as
 HelioCon, to help scope potential projects, like a kick-off computational challenge.
 Promotion continued through activities like running a booth at the Spring Activities
 Fair.
 • Outcome: The core student team is actively working to recruit additional members from
 majors including environmental studies, sociology, policy studies, and management. The
 leadership team is in the process of drafting a formal charter to file with Rice to officially
 establish the club. This formalization could potentially allow the club to receive student
 activity funding.

 9.2.2.4 Task 1.3 Distribution and Collection of the Survey Among Students (Targeted
            Months 6-12)
 • Activities: With the survey approved, efforts focused on getting it to the target
 audience. The survey has been distributed through multiple channels, including
 mailing lists, promotion at conferences (e.g., ASME IMECE 2024), targeted emails,
 and letters. Physical flyers were posted at Rice and local community colleges
 (Houston Community College, San Jacinto College, Lone Star College). Plans
 include additional rounds of advertisement via reminders and new emails/flyers to
 boost the response rate.
 • Outcome: Initial responses to the survey have been received and are undergoing
 qualitative and statistical analysis. The distribution efforts have reached a large
 number of potential participants (over 1000). However, the total number of responses
 received is still below 100. An interesting, and unexpected, trend noted is that the
 response rate from recent graduates is higher than from current college students.
 Initial analysis is underway, which will provide valuable insights into awareness and
 barriers

 9.2.2.5 Task 2.1 Development of Joint Undergraduate/Graduate Teams for
 Computational Modeling (Targeted Months 1-12, Continues in Year 2)
 • Activities: This task forms the core of the technical evaluation of CSP potential. Joint
 student teams, supervised by the PI, are developing computational models. Modeling
 efforts cover a range of topics: existing combined cycles, small-scale solar field

 120

 design (including component variations), combining CSP with other technologies
 (initially PV, later parabolic trough collectors), and investigating
 industrial/commercial integration applications such as data centers and gas turbines
 (the latter via a collaboration with the Commonwealth Scientific and Industrial
 Research Organisation [CSIRO]). The students are actively collaborating to
 understand how their individual projects contribute to the larger goal. Discussions
 about this work have occurred with the project's Technical Monitor and other
 participants (from SNL and NLR). Efforts have been made to connect students with
 external research mentors from organizations like NLR, SNL, and CSIRO to deepen
 their technical understanding. Students have been in touch with these mentors.
 • Outcome: Students have conducted literature searches and validated models against
 existing work. The computational work is progressing, with initial results available for
 cycle performance and combining heliostat fields with cycles and applications.
 Participation in this research has had a direct impact on at least one student,
 influencing their decision to pursue graduate study in a related field rather than
 enter industry immediately. Future plans include working with external mentors to
 deepen understanding of heliostat fields and incorporating this into simulations to
 develop Python modules for simulating solar field operation

 9.2.2.6 Task 2.2 Initiation of a Research Project on Use of Geographic Information
 System (GIS) Tools for Small-Scale, Urban CSP (Targeted Months 3-12)
 • Activities: This task involves identifying suitable physical locations for small-scale urban
 CSP. Work has focused on mapping brownfields in the greater Houston area, initially
 concentrating on the south side. Collaboration with groups like the Adaapta/Community
 Lattice Director of Community Relations (Treasa Anthony) and leveraging the Co-PI's
 work have provided information on brownfields, including those not typically listed in
 national surveys. Connections have been made to identify locations and needs for SHIP, a
 key potential application for heliostat fields. This includes utilizing data from the U.S.
 Environmental Protection Agency's Greenhouse Gas Reporting Program database from
 NLR’s STEP 1 software developed by Jeffrey Gifford and his team. The connection with
 Jeffrey was provided by the Technical Monitor. An undergraduate student joined the team
 to specifically work on this GIS aspect, and has completed necessary GIS training offered
 by the Rice Fondren Library Data Center (https://library.rice.edu/places/gisdata-center-
 gdc). Initial community meetings helped understand potential sites and local energy
 usage/needs, also relevant to this task.
 • Outcome: Initial GIS maps (Figure 70) have been created, combining brownfield
 locations with data points identifying industrial locations and their energy needs (via
 Adaapta, STEP 1, and North American Industry Classification System code of a range of
 industries requiring low- to mid- temperature process heat). The undergraduate student is
 reportedly excited about the next steps in this project. Future plans involve collating and
 quantifying insights and data from community organizations for inclusion in the models.
 Collaboration is planned with the students working on computational modeling (Task
 2.1) to integrate their results for potential implementation locations into the GIS model.
 Community connections have been expanded to aid in gathering GIS data.

 121

 Figure 70. Initial GIS Map

 Similar to Task 1.1, discussions were held with experts on survey strategies,

 Brombacher, Director of Economic Development for Harris County, to explore

 development contacts to explore potential installation locations and survey
 populations. Students attending a community meeting gathered initial qualitative data
 on community reactions, which is being used to refine the survey. Collaborations are
 being pursued with organizations like the Kinder Institute and Rice's Director of
 Diversity and Inclusion for Research Programs to further refine the community

 122

 survey questions.
 • Outcome: An initial survey draft exists. Relationships are being established and
 expanded with key community groups. These meetings provide valuable insights and
 links for future survey administration. Initial community input has been gathered and
 is informing the survey refinement. Relationships with community groups are
 ongoing and represent important precursors to accessing the needed data for this task
 and Milestone 1.4. of Economic Development for Harris County, to explore potential
 locations for solar installations and for survey populations (this also ties back into
 Task 2.2). These interactions have led to connections with local economic
 development contacts to explore potential installation locations and survey
 populations. Students attending a community meeting gathered initial qualitative data
 on community reactions, which is being used to refine the survey. Collaborations are
 being pursued with organizations like the Kinder Institute and Rice's Director of
 Diversity and Inclusion for Research Programs to further refine the community
 survey questions.

 9.2.2.8 Overall Analysis and Conclusion
 Overall, the project demonstrates appropriate progress across the board, with the variance in
 Task 3.1 being the primary deviation from the original internal schedule. The creation and initial
 distribution of student surveys, the expansion of the Solar Club participant group and leadership
 formation, the initiation and advancement of joint student research teams for modeling and GIS,
 and the establishment and expansion of community connections are all highlighted as significant
 technical or project accomplishments. The project is effectively linking its diverse tasks – for
 instance, community connections aid both GIS data gathering and the social dimensions survey,
 and the technical modeling results from Task 2.1 are intended to feed into the GIS analysis in
 Task 2.2. This integrated approach, along with a clear focus on workforce development and
 community engagement, suggests the project is well-positioned to continue addressing its
 objectives.

 One paper has been accepted for the American Society of Mechanical Engineers’ (ASME’s)
 Energy Sustainability 2025 conference: ES2025 156769: Feasibility Analysis of Implementing
 Small-Scale CSP System for Industrial Process Heat on Urban Brownfields, by Liaqat (grad
 student), Schulke (undergraduate), and Schaefer (PI). A second paper has been accepted for
 IEEE SusTech: 1571096863: Energy Efficiency in Data Centers: Solar Thermal-Boosted
 Organic Rankine Cycle for Waste Heat Utilization, by Liaqat (grad student) and Schaefer (PI).

 9.2.3 Training and Educational Modules Developed at Northeastern University

 9.2.3.1 Introduction
 Through HelioCon’s request for proposals (RFP), Northeastern University has led two projects:

 Project 1 (DOE ID: 38488-003): “An Educational Program on Concentrated Solar Power and
 Heliostats for Power Generation and Industrial Processes” with the following curricula:

 • Gordon Institute of Engineering Leadership Update
 • Capstone Projects (or Mechanical Engineering Design)

 123

 • Short Industry Courses
 Project 2 (DOE ID: 38488-0010): “Advancing University Level Education on Heliostat Design
 and Operation” with the following curricula:
 • Cornerstone of Engineering
 • Systems Analysis and Control
 • Financial Management for Engineers
 • Solar Simulator Update

 Through Projects 1 and 2, Northeastern University has successfully integrated heliostat
 technology and CSP principles into a range of university courses, encompassing both
 engineering and finance disciplines. Northeastern’s overarching objective for this project is to
 continue developing educational, research, and student outreach opportunities in the space of
 heliostats and CSP technology. This includes introducing solar energy principles and heliostat
 technology, control, design, and financial considerations in undergraduate courses, involving
 students in co-op assignments with NLR and SNL, developing capstone engineering design
 projects, conducting MS Projects/Theses in CSP, modifying existing courses, and presenting
 work at conferences and journals. All developed materials are intended to be publicly accessible
 online through the HelioCon Educational database website.

 Throughout Year 1, Quarters 1, 2, and 3, the project successfully completed its objectives in a
 thorough and timely manner, with no need for alterations to tasks, budget, or timeline outlined in
 the Performance Work Statement. The project has maintained a steady trajectory. Below is a
 breakdown by specific activities and courses.

 The PI of the projects at Northeastern University is Prof. Hameed Metghalchi.

 9.2.3.2 Overall Student Outreach and Recruitment Activities (Y1Q1 Task 1.1, Y1Q2
 Task 1.4)
 • Activities: Conduct student outreach and recruitment activities. Conduct event at
 freshman showcase. Post available NLR/Sandia co-op positions.
 • Outcome: Northeastern University has successfully accomplished all goals laid out for
 student outreach and recruitment. A successful presentation of CSP activities at
 Northeastern was conducted at the Showcase of Undergraduate Research and Endeavors
 (SOURCE). Interested undergraduate students engaged with a graduate student to learn
 about CSP classwork, research, and co-op opportunities.

 9.2.3.3 Cornerstone of Engineering (Y1Q1 Task 1.2, Y1Q2 Task 1.5, Y1Q3 Task 1.7) -
 Taught by Prof. Bala Maheswaran
 • Activities: Introduce CSP topics, projects, and problems to provide various levels of
 exposure to CSP control systems and design methodologies. Conduct a hands-on activity
 for new engineering undergraduate students to introduce CSP and provide an
 understanding of solar concentration principles and heliostat potential for energy
 generation. Design and build a miniature heliostat model using mirrors, 3D-printed and
 laser-cut materials, motors, sensors, and a pivot mechanism, controlled by an Arduino
 microcontroller. Teams conducted research on heliostat systems and solar concentration

 124

 principles, planned designs, selected components, fabricated parts using 3D printing and
 laser cutting, assembled components (mirrors, motors, sensors, Arduino), programmed
 the Arduino to track the sun, and tested/calibrated the models in various lighting
 conditions. Students engaged with and made presentations regarding their approach to the
 design of basic heliostat models.
 • Outcome: The heliostat model assembly activity was successfully completed. All student
 groups successfully demonstrated fundamental heliostat technology while learning
 valuable insights into the engineering design process. The model demonstrated the ability
 to track the sun and reflect light onto a target, achieving basic functionality. Student work
 was evaluated through oral presentations, model demonstrations, and written reports. A
 survey was sent and results analyzed to gather feedback. The project highlights its
 significant impact on participants' perceptions of energy technologies and future career
 aspirations, sparking or deepening interest in energy, providing insights into solar-
 thermal system complexities and innovation opportunities, and increasing awareness of
 environmental importance. The project inspired a commitment to exploring and
 contributing to energy solutions.

 9.2.3.4 Mechanical Engineering Design (Capstone Project, ME4550, Y1Q1 Task 1.2,
 Y1Q2 Task 1.5, Y1Q3 Task 1.7) - Taught by Prof. Yiannis Levendis
 • Activities: Introduce CSP topics, projects, and problems. Students develop capstone
 engineering design projects. Integrate CSP/Heliostat design and analysis questions.
 Expose students to the development of heliostat design for design projects to satisfy
 learning objectives of exploring commercial designs and designing a product/developing
 a portfolio. Teams developed two functional heliostat prototypes. Capstone teams
 submitted posters to the America Association for the Advancement of Science (AAA)
 competition. Students designed a deformable heliostat and a system to convert biomass
 into biochar using solar through biomass pyrolysis.
 • Outcome: Two heliostat prototypes were developed and underwent full structural
 analysis to ensure wind survivability and mechanical integrity, aligning with real
 operating conditions. The first design used a spur gear assembly with a custom anti-
 backlash system for precise tracking, while the second used a leadscrew-driven
 mechanism to eliminate backlash and improve load distribution. These prototypes were
 successfully completed and analyzed for structural performance. Heliostat design
 presented an interesting challenge to mechanical engineers. The specific design
 specifications for the first heliostat included 9 m² film, aluminum backing, specific drive
 train capabilities (360° azimuth, 90° elevation, spur gear assembly with <3 mrad
 backlash, anti-backlash system, electromagnetic brake), A500 steel pedestal frame with
 stake, capable of surviving 60 mph winds for 3 sec, robust, capable of functioning in
 sandy environments indefinitely with maintenance, and a mechanical factor of safety of
 1.5 for critical components. Critical components analyzed included the azimuth shaft (0.6
 m tall, 60 mm diameter), mirror frame shaft (3 m long, 80 mm diameter), and mirror
 backing (3 m by 3 m, 12 mm thick panel). The second design used three lead screws to
 remove gears entirely, allowing flexibility, even load distribution, and high precision. It
 included a 1 m² mirror weighing about 550N, a metal frame/rail welded to the mirror, ball

 125

 joints, a female threaded motor powered by a 1 horsepower motor, a motor mount, and a
 tripod base bolted to a cement platform.

 9.2.3.5 System Analysis and Control and Robot Dynamics and Control (ME3460, and
            ME4555 respectively – Y1Q1 Task 1.2, Y1Q2 Task 1.5, Y1Q3 Task 1.7) -
 Taught by Prof. Rifat Sipahi
 • Activities: Introduce CSP topics, projects, and problems. Year 2, Quarter 1 (likely meant
 Year 1, Quarter 1 based on context) saw the introduction of CSP-based teaching in
 control and robotics courses. Integrate heliostats to expose students to the technology and
 structure while reinforcing core concepts in controls and kinematics. Students analyzed
 nonlinear dynamics, modeled electro-mechanical behavior, and implemented
 proportional-integral-derivative (PID) feedback control strategies using MATLAB and
 Simulink simulations. Further assignments explored forward and inverse kinematics.
 • Outcome: Students explored PID controller design for heliostat systems, utilizing
 MATLAB/Simulink to optimize response speed, noise sensitivity, and energy efficiency.
 Forward and inverse kinematics were introduced to explore heliostat motion control in
 3D space. Homework questions regarding heliostat PID control were anticipated.
 Students consistently showed very strong performance, with average scores above 85%
 across assignments. Curriculum materials for ME4555 and ME3460 were developed. In
 ME4555, 9 homework problems and 3 YouTube videos were designed covering single
 degree of freedom dynamics, transfer functions, closed loop systems, pole placement,
 Simulink/MATLAB PID Tuner, and the transfer function between sensor noise and
 tracking error. In ME3460, a Matlab/Simscape simulation diagram of a two-degree-of-
 freedom heliostat with DC motors and gearboxes was designed.

 9.2.3.6 Financial Management for Engineers (EMGT6305 ,Y1Q1 Task 1.2, Y1Q2 Task
 1.5, Y1Q3 Task 1.7) - Taught by Prof. Hossein Noorian
 • Activities: Incorporate an exploration into capital budgeting and investment decision
 making in the context of CSP. Introduce the economic complications of CSP projects.
 Students were guided through complexities using CSP projects as case studies. Students
 evaluated mutually exclusive projects, applying methods such as payback period, net
 present value (NPV), internal rate of return (IRR), and modified IRR. Students analyzed
 how political events and different financial mechanisms would impact the evaluation of
 each project. A homework assignment explored four key financial evaluation methods to
 compare two sample CSP investment projects. The assignment provided appropriate
 variables and asked students to perform calculations and discuss the impact of external
 factors. The course included class lectures and discussions about the impact of
 macroenvironment, political environment, perception, weather, technology, government
 policies, and economics. Assignments were developed based on real-life conditions and
 popular budgeting methods.
 • Outcome: Students performed the required calculations needed to evaluate the
 comparison between two investments. The exercises added a strategic dimension,
 connecting the technical engineering basis with real-world financial scenarios. A
 curriculum has been developed providing students with a solid background on
 macroenvironment, political environment, and perception. 12 assignments based on real-

 126

 life conditions and 4 budgeting methods were developed and given, with 85-90 students
 participating and 95% participation rate in Y1Q2.

 9.2.3.7 Short Industry Courses - Taught by Prof. Gregory Kowalski
 • Activities: Develop existing short courses and graduate-level concentrated solar energy
 courses. Work on short course development is ongoing. Reorganization of course
 material from an online course offered in Spring 2024. Outcome: Work is ongoing but
 taking longer than expected. Challenges include balancing background info with practical
 levels required for short courses, deciding depth of radiation theory vs. practitioner needs,
 and balancing software use vs. theoretical understanding. Balancing module duration and
 content within time constraints is also a challenge.
 • Status: Ongoing. Work on development is ongoing but delayed.
 • Analysis: The delay is significantly influenced by reorganizing material from a previous
 online course. There are inherent challenges in structuring the content for industry short
 courses to meet diverse audience needs.

 9.2.3.8 Solar Simulator Setup - Andrew Szerdy and Maxim Ndjiki-nya (Students)
 • Activities: Two students led a project designed to emulate the sun's position in a spherical
 coordinate system.
 • Outcome: All parts for the simulator have been obtained, and assembly is completed. The
 simulator uses a light source at a fixed radius with adjustable azimuthal and zenith
 angles.
