 9.2.3.11 Conclusion and Impact on Curriculum Development for CSP
 In conclusion, the project has been highly effective in integrating CSP and heliostat design and
 operation into the Northeastern University curriculum at various levels, from introductory
 undergraduate courses to advanced control systems, mechanical design, and graduate-level
 financial management. The approach combines hands-on activities, theoretical analysis,
 computational simulation, and real-world financial considerations, providing students with a
 comprehensive understanding of CSP technology and its applications. This integration not only
 reinforces core engineering and financial concepts but also sparks student interest in energy
 technology careers. The project is on track to continue expanding these educational offerings and
 contribute publicly available resources to the field.

 9.2.4 Progress and Status Analysis
 The initiatives demonstrate promising progress across all identified gaps

 • Public Awareness Gap: STCC program development is advancing steadily. By selecting
 practical applications like the coffee roaster, the consortium is successfully integrating
 relatable technology demonstrations. The competition framework is nearing
 implementation readiness, which is expected to significantly increase public awareness
 and student engagement. Public outreach programs are being developed
 • Resource Accessibility Gap: The RTE initiative has successfully created a foundation for
 knowledge sharing. Database expansion continues with structured growth, now
 containing 60 entries and revealing patterns. Integration of educational materials is
 progressing, moving toward democratizing access to specialized knowledge. Pattern
 recognition and taxonomy development are yielding valuable insights. The development
 of centralized resource management systems, accessible database tools, and standardized
 documentation protocols directly addresses the lack of publicly available resources.
 Centralized knowledge repositories have been created.
 • Academic-Industry Collaboration Gap: Effective collaboration mechanisms have been
 established. Mentorship programs are operational, actively connecting industry experts
 like researchers from NLR and SNL with students at participating universities. Tool
 development and sharing, exemplified by the promotion and potential use of the STEP-1
 tool, is active. Intern participation is contributing meaningfully to projects like database
 development and competition initiatives. Structured pathways for industry-academic
 collaboration are being created. Active partnerships have been established between
 universities, research institutions, industry stakeholders, national laboratories, private
 sector entities, and international research organizations.

 9.3 Gap Updates and Priorities Supporting Intermediate Goal
 9.3.1 Introduction: The Imperative for CSP Education Development
 The development of expertise in CSP technology is a critical challenge in modern energy
 education, essential for preparing the next generation of professionals for a rapidly evolving

 128

 energy landscape. This roadmap framework is based on DOE priorities, industry gaps, and
 pedagogical innovation. It synthesizes insights from stakeholders. A comprehensive analysis
 based on stakeholders’ feedback reveals critical gaps in existing CSP curriculum development
 within higher education. These include gaps in expertise development among faculty, curriculum
 enhancement, employment perception among students, and industry connection. Addressing
 these interconnected gaps requires a comprehensive, multi-faceted approach. Drawing on the
 strategic focus of the Solar Energy Technologies Office (SETO), analyses of curriculum
 development gaps, technical standards frameworks, economic considerations, interdisciplinary
 integration needs, industry and national laboratory collaboration models, pedagogical
 innovations, and specific technology areas like thermal energy storage (TES) and CSP modular
 systems, this framework proposes a phased approach to curriculum development,
 implementation, and continuous improvement. It addresses key challenges including faculty
 expertise limitations, curriculum content and delivery gaps, student employment perceptions, and
 critical disconnects with industry needs. The goal is to establish a balanced educational
 environment that equips students with deep theoretical understanding, practical competencies,
 and the ability to navigate complex systems and innovate in the CSP area.

 The development of CSP curricula is a critical challenge in preparing energy professionals. CSP
 technology is complex, integrating mechanical, thermal, electrical, and control systems. It offers
 unique advantages, particularly the ability to integrate thermal storage for dispatchable power
 generation, unlike traditional photovoltaic systems. This complexity, coupled with the rapidly
 evolving nature of the technology, necessitates a structured approach to education development.
 This roadmap framework provides a structured approach aiming to synthesize a plan for
 curriculum development that aligns with SETO's priorities, addresses identified gaps, integrates
 technical standards and emerging technologies, leverages industry and national lab resources,
 and incorporates innovative pedagogical methods.

 9.3.2 A 2-Year RTE Roadmap Prioritizing Expertise and Industry Integration
 Based on Stakeholders’ feedback, below is a 2-year roadmap outlining prioritized challenges and
 proposed solutions for addressing key gaps in the RTE curriculum development and advancing
 HelioCon Resource Development and Educational Initiatives for the final two years of the
 HelioCon Consortium initiatives.

 As stated in 9.2.3.2, several critical gaps in RTE curriculum development that require immediate
 attention have been identified. These include expertise development among faculty, curriculum
 content and implementation enhancement, student employment perception, and strengthening
 industry connections. These gaps are described as interconnected and requiring a comprehensive,
 multi-faceted approach. The CSP Consortium has already made significant progress in resource
 development and educational initiatives, and future directions include expanding analytical
 capabilities, international collaboration, and advanced training modules incorporating emerging
 technologies.

 Prioritizing the gaps related to faculty expertise and industry connection seems critical, as
 knowledgeable faculty and strong industry ties are foundational for updating curriculum content
 and improving student preparedness for real-world applications. The curriculum enhancement
 gap is also highly important, as it directly relates to the quality and relevance of educational
 content. The employment perception gap, while significant, can be addressed more effectively

 129

 once the curriculum is enhanced and industry connections provide clearer career pathways and
 resources. A 2-year RTE roadmap can be structured into Short-Term (0-12 months) and
 Medium-Term (1-2 years) phases.

 9.3.2.1 Year 1 (Short-Term: 0-12 Months): Assessment, Foundation Building, and Initial
 Implementation
 This phase focuses on systematically assessing the current state of CSP education and laying the
 groundwork for future enhancements. The prioritized gaps addressed are primarily expertise
 development, industry connection, and initial steps in curriculum enhancement.

 • Faculty Enhancement Foundation: Initiate structured professional development
 workshops focusing on core CSP technologies and the effective integration of CSP
 concepts into existing curricula. Establish a preliminary series of industry expert guest
 lectures and knowledge transfer sessions. Utilize existing SETO resources and
 documentation as foundational material for faculty professional development.
 • Industry Connection Foundation: Initiate connections and partnerships with industry
 stakeholders and national laboratories. Establish basic protocols for industry
 collaboration. Develop a preliminary mentorship framework and guidelines, recruiting
 experienced mentors from industry and national labs. Begin regular engagement with
 industry experts.
 • Curriculum Enhancement Foundation: Establish basic curriculum modules and
 frameworks that integrate theoretical foundations with practical applications. Focus on
 developing a core technical foundation, including fundamental principles of
 concentrating solar power, thermodynamics, heat transfer, optical systems, and materials
 science applications. Begin incorporating fundamental electrical safety standards and
 basic material specifications (ASTM E903) into relevant modules. Start the process of
 transforming traditional engineering problems into CSP-relevant examples to overcome
 familiarity bias. Develop scalable teaching materials and methodologies. Secure initial
 funding commitments and identify necessary resources.
 • Consortium Integration: Leverage the existing heliostat database and its tools as
 standardized reference material for faculty and students. Promote the use of existing
 public short courses on CSP topics developed by partner universities. Actively engage
 Consortium mentors in university mentoring programs. Utilize established
 communication networks for current or future interns. Continue the development of
 detailed competition guidelines and safety protocols for the collegiate competition
 initiative. Conduct initial impact assessments and evaluation of program effectiveness.
 • Gap Assessment: Conduct a comprehensive review of existing RTE curricula with
 stakeholders at the end of year 1, evaluate current faculty competencies, and assess
 industry partnership potential and immediate resource requirements. Systematically
 analyze stakeholder feedback. Examine current program outcomes and industry
 requirements, including technical standards like IEC and ASTM.

 9.3.2.2 Year 2 (Medium-Term: 1-2 years): Expansion, Consolidation, and Program
 Launch
 This phase involves implementing enhanced curriculum components, expanding partnerships,
 launching comprehensive programs, and refining initial initiatives. The prioritized gaps

 130

 addressed are all four primary gaps such as expertise development, industry connection,
 curriculum enhancement, and employment perception.

 • Faculty Enhancement Expansion: Implement enhanced faculty enhancement programs,
 focusing on advanced CSP technologies, specialized software, and pedagogical
 approaches for complex topics. Expand collaborative research opportunities with industry
 partners and national laboratories. Implement regular update mechanisms for curriculum
 content to ensure currency with rapidly evolving CSP technologies. Establish a best
 practices database and knowledge management system for faculty.
 • Industry Connection Expansion: Enhance existing industry partnerships and
 collaborations, developing structured collaboration protocols and national laboratory
 engagement strategies. Launch a comprehensive mentorship program connecting students
 with industry professionals and national lab experts. Increase practical application
 opportunities through structured project-based learning frameworks with industry input
 and real-world challenges. Develop and implement structured internship programs with
 industry partners. Conduct site visits to operational CSP facilities to provide firsthand
 experience. Implement knowledge transfer systems through regular expert engagements,
 workshops, and case study development protocols.
 • Curriculum Enhancement Expansion: Implement enhanced curriculum components,
 including advanced technical modules in performance modeling, control systems
 integration, safety protocols, environmental impact assessment, and economic feasibility
 studies. Introduce emerging CSP technologies, materials science advances, and
 innovations, such as advanced thermal storage materials, AI in system optimization.
 Develop interdisciplinary modules incorporating related fields like AI, machine learning,
 computer science, and chemical engineering for industrial process integration. Introduce
 complex system integration standards and specialized testing protocols (IEC and ASTM).
 Incorporate technical considerations and educational value of modular CSP systems.
 Refine curriculum delivery methods, implementing blended learning approaches,
 simulation-based training, and project-based learning. Refine and enhance assessment
 mechanisms and evaluation tools to ensure comprehensive measurement of knowledge
 and practical application. Implement hands-on laboratory experiences with CSP systems.
 Most of the proposed items in this section can be implemented via seminars or
 workshops.
 • Employment Perception Enhancement: Develop comprehensive career development
 resources and support systems. Establish industry advisory boards for program guidance.
 Cultivate strategic industry partnerships for diversified funding strategies and program
 expansion. Implement career tracking systems and outcome measurement. Work with
 industry partners to address career visibility issues and provide clearer progression
 opportunities within the industry.
 • Consortium Integration: Expand the analytical capabilities of the heliostat database.
 Develop and integrate advanced training modules that incorporate emerging technologies
 in the CSP sector. Continue and strengthen the HelioCon seminar series and professional
 development pathways. Implement the collegiate competition program as a practical
 application opportunity for students. Refine all RTE initiatives based on ongoing
 evaluation and feedback mechanisms.

 131

 We believe that by prioritizing faculty development and industry connections in the initial phase,
 and then expanding curriculum content, practical opportunities, and career support in the second
 year, Northeastern and Rice can effectively bridge the identified gaps and align with the progress
 and future directions of the HelioCon Consortium initiatives, preparing undergraduates and
 graduates for the demands of the evolving CSP sector.

 9.3.3 Future Research and Development Directions
 The stakeholders have identified several areas warranting further investigation and development
 to support curriculum evolution:
 • Technical Development: Standardization of modular CSP systems, cost reduction
 strategies for smaller installations, integration of advanced materials and technologies,
 development of novel control systems
 • Educational Innovation: Virtual reality training environments, remote monitoring and
 control systems, collaborative learning platforms, industry-academic partnership model.
 Integration of technology like automated scheduling tools, progress tracking software,
 virtual collaboration platforms, performance analytics dashboard, etc.
 • Market Analysis: Emerging applications for modular CSP, economic viability studies,
 regulatory framework development, public acceptance factors.
 • Impact Assessment Studies: Long-term effectiveness evaluation of curriculum
 interventions, career outcome tracking, alumni success metrics, industry satisfaction
 measurement, return on investment analysis.
 • Emerging Technologies Integration: Investigation of new CSP innovations and their
 educational implications, advanced manufacturing techniques training, integration of
 energy storage systems education, incorporation of smart grid technologies.
 • Global Best Practices: International program comparison and benchmarking,
 development of cross-cultural adaptation strategies, analysis of regional industry
 requirements, investigation of language and cultural considerations in CSP education.
 • Support Infrastructure: Development of mentorship programs, peer support networks,
 resource sharing platforms, and knowledge repositories. Pursuing these research
 directions while maintaining alignment with SETO's commercial-scale focus is crucial.

 9.3.4 CSP Workforce and Industry Development Strategies
 Academic curricula and industry partnerships principles reported here can significantly extend
 their impact beyond students to upskill professionals in the Concentrating Solar Power (CSP)
 industry and even benefit professionals in the broader energy sector and other industries. These
 principles also offer insights into strategies for revitalization the CSP industry as well as
 educating the general population and debunking specific myths about CSP.

 9.3.4.1 Professional Development and Upskilling for Current CSP Professionals
 The foundational principles of CSP curriculum and workforce development, as outlined in the
 sources, are highly applicable to existing professionals within the CSP industry. These strategies
 can facilitate continuous learning, upskilling, and knowledge transfer, which are crucial for
 driving industry growth and innovation.
 • Structured Training Programs:

 132

 o Upskill in Advanced Technologies: Provide workshops and training on emerging
 CSP technologies, advanced materials, novel control systems, and integration with
 AI/Machine Learning.
 o Facilitate Knowledge Transfer: Establish industry expert lecture series and peer
 mentoring programs where experienced professionals can share insights and best
 practices.
 o Standardize Training: Develop standardized CSP modules with industry validation
 and shared resource repositories to ensure consistent knowledge across the workforce.
 • Practical Application and Experiential Learning:
 o Workshops and Mentorship Programs: Implement structured workshops where
 industry partners present real-world challenges (e.g., operational efficiency,
 maintenance, infrastructure limitations) and facilitate collaborative problem-solving.
 Mentorship programs, traditionally for students, can be extended to peer-to-peer or
 senior to midcareer professional relationships, offering guidance and insights from
 active involvement in CSP projects.
 o Industry-Standard Software Training: Provide hands-on practice with industry-
 standard design and analysis software such as SolarPILOT and SolTrace.
 o Case Studies and Project-Based Learning: Utilize case studies drawn from actual
 CSP installations, highlighting key decisions, successes, and failures, to provide
 contextual learning and problem-solving exercises. Encourage collaborative research
 projects addressing industry challenges and utilizing actual plant data.
 o Site Visits: Organize facilitated site visits to operational CSP facilities for firsthand
 observation and understanding of real-world implementation.
 • Integrating Technical Standards: Emphasize continuous education on industry
 standards from organizations like the International Electrotechnical Commission (IEC)
 for system safety, performance metrics, and grid integration, and the American Society
 for Testing and Materials (ASTM) for materials science and testing. This includes design
 documentation standards, test report specifications, and professional practice
 (certification, installation, maintenance, troubleshooting).
 • Digital Tools Integration: Train professionals on industry-provided digital tools such as
 advanced visualization systems (3D layouts, VR/AR simulations), digital twin platforms
 (real-time monitoring, performance simulation, maintenance tools), and collaborative
 development tools (project management, version control, documentation repositories).

 9.3.4.2 Revitalization the CSP Industry
 To revitalize the CSP industry, there are several interconnected strategies that involve leveraging
 SETO's mandate, promoting modular systems, and strengthening industry-academia links:
 • Strategic Alignment with SETO's Mandate: The Solar Energy Technologies Office
 (SETO) has a congressional mandate emphasizing commercial-scale power generation
 and has shown an increasing interest in SHIP in recent years. Academic institutions and
 industry should align their development efforts with these focus areas to access funding
 mechanisms and ensure relevance. SETO's framework includes collaborative partnerships
 with industry stakeholders and integration with national laboratories and research
 institutions.
 • Leveraging Modular CSP Systems: Modular CSP systems are an innovative approach
 addressing historical limitations and leveraging integrated thermal storage for

 133

 dispatchable solar power generation. Their standardized design and compact footprints
 offer deployment flexibility across diverse geographic contexts. Key advantages that can
 revitalize the industry include:
 o Reduced Project Risk and Cost: They offer reduced project development timelines,
 economies of scale through mass production, lower financial risk through phased
 deployment, and enhanced financing options.
 o New Market Opportunities: Their design makes them suitable for urban integration
 (compact footprints, height compliance, visual/noise minimization), transportation
 infrastructure integration (along highways for electric vehicle charging), and diverse
 industrial applications (data centers, process heat).
 o Technical Innovation: Continued research into advanced thermal storage materials,
 improved collector efficiency, and AI in system optimization can further drive their
 adoption
 • Strengthening Industry Connection and Workforce Development:
 o Closing the Industry Connection Gap: The “Industry Connection Gap” (limited
 access to practical projects, insufficient industry partnerships, gap between theoretical
 and practical knowledge, restricted access to cutting-edge technologies, etc.) needs
 systematic addressing. This requires structured industry collaboration protocols and
 knowledge transfer systems.
 o Industry Expert Engagement: Involving industry experts in curriculum development,
 review, and validation, and establishing industry advisory boards for program
 guidance are crucial.
 o Skilled Workforce Pipeline: Focus on creating a specialized knowledge base in CSP
 technologies, developing versatile engineering skills, preparing for emerging market
 opportunities, building innovation capacity, and establishing future leadership
 pipelines. This includes preparing students for immediate employment opportunities
 like plant operations, system design, R&D, and project engineering.
 • Economic Feasibility and Market Analysis: Conduct “economic feasibility studies and
 cost-benefit analysis,” including project finance, market analysis, and supply chain
 management. Future research should investigate emerging applications for modular CSP,
 economic viability studies, and regulatory framework development to support market
 growth.

 9.3.4.3 Benefits to Professionals in the Broader Energy Industry and Outside
 The educational framework for CSP is designed to instill transferable competencies that extend
 far beyond the specific domain of solar power, making it highly beneficial for professionals in
 the broader energy industry and other sectors.

 • Systems Thinking and Interdisciplinary Problem-Solving: CSP technology serves as
 an exemplary model for teaching systems thinking and interdisciplinary problem-solving
 methodologies due to its intricate integration of mechanical, thermal, electrical, and
 control systems. Professionals trained in CSP learn to:
 o Understand systemic relationships and make informed decisions at the intersection of
 technology and business.
 o Analyze physical system components, operational infrastructure, and business
 integration elements as interconnected parts of a larger system.

 134

 o This approach develops adaptable problem-solving approaches and enhances
 collaborative work capabilities applicable across diverse fields.
 • Cross-Disciplinary Integration: The CSP curriculum emphasizes integration with
 various disciplines, offering valuable skills for professionals across industries:
 o AI, Machine Learning, and Computer Science: Integration with these fields is a
 transformative opportunity for performance optimization and real-time data
 processing. Skills in robust computational infrastructure, sensor networks, cloud
 computing, and high-performance computing are broadly applicable.
 o Chemical Engineering: CSP offers distinct advantages in chemical synthesis
 processes, particularly syngas production. Chemical engineers can contribute
 expertise in process optimization, thermal process management (heat transfer, fluid
 dynamics, temperature control), and reaction engineering. These skills are valuable in
 chemical, manufacturing, and energy processing sectors.
 o Thermal Energy Storage (TES): Expertise in TES is crucial for CSP, but also has
 broad industrial applications in power generation, desalination, chemical processing,
 and "manufacturing. Professionals gain knowledge in heat transfer, thermodynamics,
 materials science, and system design applicable to any thermal system.
 o Modular Systems Concepts: The principles of standardized capacity, scalability, and
 deployment flexibility seen in modular CSP can inform project management and
 deployment strategies in other infrastructure and energy sectors. Their applicability to
 "urban integration," "transportation infrastructure," and "industrial applications"
 further demonstrates broad relevance.
 • General Professional Skills: The CSP curriculum fosters competencies such as
 advanced mathematical modeling, engineering design principles, scientific methodology,
 technology integration proficiency, interdisciplinary communication techniques, and
 complex problem-solving frameworks. These are highly valued in any technical or
 management role.

 9.3.4.4 Educating the Population at Large and Debunking Myths
 While the provided concepts here primarily focus on formal educational frameworks, curriculum
 development for students, and professional development within academic and industry settings,
 these could contribute to address strategies for public education or debunking specific
 misconceptions about CSP technology through the following:
 • Environmental Impact Assessment: The curriculum includes “environmental impact
 assessment methodologies”. Professionals trained with this knowledge would be
 equipped to provide data-driven, factual information to the public about CSP's
 environmental footprint, including potential impacts and mitigation strategies.
 • Public Acceptance Factors: “Public acceptance factors” are identified as a “Market
 Analysis” area for future research. Understanding these factors would be a prerequisite
 for developing effective public education campaigns, but the sources do not detail the
 campaigns themselves.
 • Community Acceptance Strategies: “Community acceptance strategies” are mentioned
 in the context of modular CSP implementation case studies. This suggests that
 engagement with local communities is considered, which could include addressing local
 concerns or misconceptions.

 135

 • Case Studies: “Case studies” from successful implementations and “success story
 compilation” are part of the curriculum and continuous improvement. These success
 stories could be adapted for public consumption to highlight positive aspects and benefits
 of CSP.
 • Addressing Concerns Through Design and Research:
 o Modular CSP systems are designed with visual impact minimization through
 reduced tower heights and noise pollution reduction through enclosed systems.
 Highlighting these design innovations in public discourse could address visual and
 noise concerns.
 o “Community acceptance strategies” are an area of investigation within
 implementation case studies for modular CSP, that should be considered for future
 research in market analysis and could help address public concerns during project
 deployment. Research into these factors could inform effective public communication
 strategies.

 9.4 Conclusion: Fostering a Transformed CSP Workforce
 The intersection between SETO's commercial-scale mandate and academic development presents
 unique opportunities for advancing CSP expertise. The successful development of CSP curricula
 requires a comprehensive approach addressing multiple interconnected gaps. This roadmap
 framework, synthesized from the stakeholders’ feedback, outlines a structured methodology that
 progresses from gap identification and foundational development to detailed framework design,
 implementation, assessment, and continuous improvement.

 Key components include understanding and leveraging SETO's priorities, systematically
 addressing expertise, curriculum, employment perception, and industry connection gaps,
 designing a multi-faceted curriculum incorporating core, advanced, and practical elements,
 integrating specific technologies like modular CSP, TES, technical standards, chemical
 engineering applications, and interdisciplinary areas like AI/ML/CS, implementing practical
 training, industry collaboration, and resource optimization strategies, evaluating effectiveness
 through multidimensional assessment, and ensuring long-term impact through continuous
 improvement and quality assurance.

 This framework represents more than just a technical training program; it embodies a
 transformative educational approach that prepares professionals with deep technical expertise,
 practical skills, and the versatile competencies needed to address complex challenges in the
 rapidly evolving energy sector. By systematically implementing the strategies outlined herein,
 educational institutions can effectively bridge the gap between traditional academic instruction
 and specialized CSP training requirements, preparing graduates for immediate contribution and
 future innovation in the global energy landscape.

 136

 10 Field Deployment
 Field deployment captures all activities required to establish a functioning solar field. Figure 71
 depicts the broad scope under the field deployment subtopic. Pre-deployment activities include
 site selection, field layout modeling, and activities supporting capitalization, and permitting
 including power production models, environmental impact studies, and reliability/performance
 evidence.

 Figure 71. Diagram of field deployment scope

 10.1 Review of Tier 1 Gaps
 In the previous Roadmap report it was acknowledged that much of field deployment is site
 specific and heliostat specific which can limit the degree to which technological improvements
 can be broadly relevant and impactful. In the first phases of HelioCon, the field deployment
 working group focused on activities that reduce investment risk such as improving power
 production models, reliability, and reducing permitting barriers. HelioCon also focused on
 deployment methods that would help the heliostat market penetrate into new thermal energy
 applications with the intent that more consistent deployments would stimulate learning rates
 which would drive down costs. Design innovations that directly reduce deployment costs such as
 wireless communications or mounted PV power that eliminate trenching and wiring costs were
 investigated in the components and controls subtask as well as external partners funded through
 the HelioCon RFP.

 137

 In the previous Roadmap it was stated that the predominant hurdle to cost reduction was the
 paucity of opportunities to deploy heliostats, relative to other energy technologies, which has
 hindered learning and innovation in these processes. To break this overarching problem into
 manageable scope, the recommended pathways endeavored to identify the main cost and risk
 factors that make heliostat field deployments less likely. The following is quoted from the
 previous report:
 • CSP projects have been delayed and cancelled in the permitting phase. Technical means
 of mitigating the environmental impacts and reasons for community opposition should
 focus on the issues raised in past challenges including visual impact, violations to the
 Endangered Species Act and Migratory Bird Treaty Act—specifically environmental
 impacts on birds, terrestrial animals, and plants.
 • Projects have also failed to garner the capital needed from investors because less risky
 options may exist. Recommended pathways should strive to quantify the reasons for
 shortfalls in power prediction models and offer improved models as well as lead on the
 development of standards that can ensure bankable quality and reliability.
 • Diversifying into industrial heat applications could lead to more frequent opportunities,
 but unfamiliar technologies may be perceived as too risky. Technical work should
 leverage existing field layout applications, and add features that simplify the ability of a
 potential plant owner to explore the techno-economics of CSP as well as visualize the
 integration of fields with existing property lines and the impacts of glare on traffic and
 nearby establishments that are more likely in populated settings where retrofitted plants
 are likely to be.

 Field deployment cost reductions can come from innovations that reduce the costs of facet
 washing (labor and water usage) and innovations that reduce the financial impact due to wind
 induced tracking error which are being investigated in other subtasks. A major gap in field
 deployment is the lack of statistics on the costs of various deployment and O&M processes since
 true field deployments have been owned by construction firms and not researchers interested in
 informing the broader community.

 Reducing investor risk is approached by leveraging work in the other subtopic areas including
 techno-economic analysis, metrology, wind, and soiling to improve the power prediction
 modeling. Model improvements will also come from combining efforts in the TEA and existing
 plant support to perform a “post-mortem” analysis on previous or existing projects to understand
 the difference between model assumptions and actual plant operations and reconcile predicted
 versus actual production. Investor/utility risk is also reduced by leveraging the standards and
 advanced manufacturing topic areas to develop a set of reliability standards that touch on the
 entire development cycle: design, testing, production, quality control, and life cycle monitoring.

 Table 16 shows the tier ranking criteria for the Tier 1 gaps identified in the 2022 roadmap study.
 The items that were not undertaken have been crossed out while the remaining items have made
 progress by the HelioCon team or related funded projects.

 10.2 Related Projects and Progress Made Toward Closing Gaps
 10.2.1 High-Fidelity Solar Field Performance Prediction
 Heliostat fields compose a significant proportion of the project costs of a solar tower system, and
 the performance of the heliostats determines a plant’s productivity at a given location. While

 140

 system characterization tools such as SolarPILOT and System Advisor Model (SAM) include a
 large collection of inputs that influence the performance of a solar tower system, many are
 uncertain prior to the development of the project and may have a significant impact on the
 overall energy delivery and profitability of a project; moreover, the fidelity of these models
 under default conditions may be insufficient to determine the value of component improvements
 such as those under development elsewhere in HelioCon. This work introduces a Monte Carlo
 simulation framework that incorporates uncertainty in key performance parameters to generate
 confidence intervals and percentile estimates for a heliostat field’s energy delivery. The key
 contributions of this work are intended to (i) provide guidance on developing interval estimates
 to supplement a point estimate on solar field production, and (ii) build on prior work (Kesseli et
 al. 2019) that either adds fidelity to the forecast to increase accuracy or provide measures of
 conservativism so that underperformance relative to projections is less likely in future projects.

 Methodology

 The objective of this subtask is to develop a high-fidelity, heliostat-based system performance
 prediction model. Our approach uses Monte Carlo simulation to obtain point and interval
 estimates of plant production and will incorporate the multiple sources of uncertainty, for which
 we detail modeling progress and assumptions below by source.

 Direct normal irradiance (DNI) inputs. The framework we developed incorporates multiple
 years of historical weather data, using the National Solar Resource Database to obtain inputs at
 5-minute fidelity for input to our performance characterization models (Sengupta et al. 2018).
 The migration from hourly fidelity to 5-minute fidelity weather inputs increases periods in which
 the receiver “trips” or shuts down for a small amount of time during periods of low DNI, which
 is less frequent with hourly data in which the measure is averaged over the hour. In the Monte-
 Carlo simulation framework, we select a year at random from the available history.

 Wind inputs. Wind stow events occur in SAM when wind is sustained over a user-specified
 threshold, reducing thermal production to zero in that time period until the solar field and
 receiver can start up again. Additionally, wind can cause heat loss at the receiver, and we will
 employ existing models that estimate wind profiles at high elevation using measurements at
 lower elevations as a guide and extrapolation of profiles in the literature (Mills et al. 2019).

 Solar field failures. To simulate partial field availability, we have adopted a model from prior
 research that assumes exponentially distributed failures for individual heliostat components, in
 which the heliostat is unavailable after a component failure. In the short term we currently use
 historical data from Nevada Solar Two to calibrate the failure rates; however, we intend to refine
 our model as more understanding regarding wind load on the heliostats is obtained.

 Power block failures. While HelioCon’s focus is on heliostats, an electricity-producing plant
 includes a power block which will limit or preclude operation of the solar field in the event of an
 outage. We use a simulation framework developed in prior work to simulate these failures
 (Wales et al. 2023). Using distribution fitting models in Python’s scipy library, we have obtained
 monthly distributions of outage frequency and duration using the U.S. Energy Information
 Administration and Nuclear Regulatory Commission data as guidelines for both datasets; power
 block failures are assumed to shut down receiver operations if storage is not available or is full

 141

 and industry data serves as input to these models, with the exception of recently characterized
 salt-to-steam heat exchanger reliability modeling (Hood et al. 2024).

 Soiling. Both continuous soiling accumulation over time, which is mitigated with mirror-
 washing operations at regular intervals, and rare events that cause heavy soiling, such as dust
 storms, will be considered for this; these factors will rely on analysis and modeling demonstrated
 in previous work on heliostat O&M (Zolan and Mehos 2022).

 Optical performance. Optical error is difficult to characterize in the field and limited field data is
 available, so our forecasting work is likely to perform sensitivity analysis on this parameter in the
 place of a distribution or include a prior distribution with high variance.

 Modeling error. This uncertainty is due to lack of fidelity or in situ validation of the models that
 we employ in our forecasting framework, including ray tracing and the physical models in SAM.

 Due to a relative lack of data available, many measures, including the optical performance of the
 heliostats, is assumed to be highly uncertain a priori and may be updated if a metrology
 campaign is performed. The generic modelling error factor allows for epistemic uncertainty in
 our framework.

 Figure 72. Flowchart describing the performance forecasting methodology.
 Figure 72 depicts a flowchart of the inputs and outputs of each submodel that we use to obtain a
 performance estimate. The software library under development is housed in a GitHub repository.
 While we have obtained results by backcasting to multiple years of production for Ivanpah (one
 year of which was shown in prior reporting), we have identified three key modeling parameters
 in which we can bring in additional fidelity to provide less variable uncertainty bounds, the
 implementation of which are currently in progress. The migration from hourly fidelity to 5-
 minute fidelity weather inputs increases periods in which the receiver “trips” or shuts down for a
 small amount of time during periods of low DNI, which is less frequent with hourly data in
 which DNI is averaged over the hour. In the place of the default assumption that field and power
 cycle availability is constant, our simulation framework updates solar field availability for each
 period in response to heliostat failures, and updates plant capacity and efficiency in response to

 142

 component failures and power cycle outages. Finally, we intend to assume a specific number of
 focal lengths in the solar field in the place of the default SolarPILOT assumption of either flat or
 individually focused heliostats. This requires a level of fidelity not currently available in SAM or
 SolarPILOT and so we intend to publish the workflow in a GitHub repository upon completion.

 Preliminary Results
 Figure 73 displays an example of results for a contrived case. The results demonstrate how the
 forecast adapts in response to additional site characterization data obtained during preparation
 and initial operations. By assuming high levels of uncertainty a priori, the model can provide
 interval and percentile forecasts that are appropriate for the fidelity of information known at the
 time; the fidelity improvements in this study serve to avoid overestimating performance. A
 validation effort comparing modelled output to that of an operating plant is ongoing.

 estimates for a solar field as additional information on the site and system is obtained.

 10.2.2 Foundation Styles and Cost Analysis: Ground Screws
 A lack of knowledge was identified in gaps F1-F4 as well as gaps F7-F8 around the
 cultural/environmental impact and field preparation techniques for heliostat fields. The
 previously identified gaps specifically highlighted the need to reduce danger to endangered
 species, migratory birds, and reducing environmental impact for gaps F1-F4 and expand the
 understanding between ground conditions and the deployment of foundations for F7-F8. A series
 of site preparation techniques and foundation methods were investigated with the intent to
 address these identified gaps. A focus was maintained on the process and needs of a field
 deployment activity that was presumed to take place on federally managed land because of the
 increased difficulty involved. Site preparation techniques include what is referred to as “blade-
 and-grade” and “drive-and-crush” to name two. As the name implies a “blade-and-grade”
 strategy levels and grades the project area and drastically impacts the local environment. This
 technique comes with many pros and cons, but ultimately is disincentivized for several reasons.
 A few of these reasons include the delicate nature of a desert ecology and slow to non-existent
 recovery of desert flora. Additionally, a lack of vegetation increases the likelihood of dust
 generation during construction and operations which is detrimental during permitting and
 construction as well as operations and maintenance. A “drive-and-crush” strategy does not

 143

 remove the natural vegetation but does as the name implies with installation vehicles driving
 over natural vegetation to deploy foundations and pedestals at intended locations. This technique
 also impacts the local environment but has been identified as preferential to the blade-and-grade
 method if large vehicles are necessary. The images shown in Figure 74 demonstrate the stark
 difference in natural vegetation and a blade-and-grade style site preparation for a location in the
 Mojave Desert.

 Figure 74. Mojave Desert Reference Images. Left) A Scrape or Blade and Grade Area. Right)
 Pristine land at a similar location
 Beyond site preparation techniques, a literature review of multiple foundation types for heliostats
 informed a decision matrix to determine which foundation method was explored. This decision
 matrix evaluated the following styles: drilled pier, over-drilled precast, over-drilled cast-in-place,
 shallow footing, driven pile, and helical pile (ground screws). The weighting factors for the
 decision matrix consisted of load capacity, range of tolerated soil conditions, range of tolerated
 groundwater conditions, ease of access for instillation equipment, ease of construction, speed of
 instillation, and ease of site cleanup. This decision matrix informed the selection of ground
 screws as the candidate to continue to research to address previously identified gaps. A
 contributing factor to the selection of ground screws is the fact that current utility scale solar
 field deployments (PV systems) utilize the technology. An additional contributing factor was the
 reduced labor costs for deployment because of the elimination of the need to pre-drill foundation
 support holes.

 Figure 75. Costing estimate of a heliostat foundation vs. reflective area for ground screw style
 foundations leveraging available cost data from Solar Dynamics' SunRing and sbp's Stellio. Left)
 Full Graph. Right) Subset image of Full Graph.

 144

 During an additional literature review focused on ground screws, a lack of load capacity data, for
 areas of solar resources conducive to SHIP or CSP, was available for ground screws across soil
 types. A few solar development projects and supporting locations were identified spanning both
 SHIP or CSP and PV installations and can be found in Figure 76. As such, a simple test scenario
 was devised to test ground screw axial and transverse load capacity in different soil types above
 ground in controlled soil environments at small scale coupled to limited in-ground testing. This
 testing activity to explore the relationship between a potential foundation method and various
 soil types is ongoing.

 Figure 76. Left) A typical geological soil texture pyramid with soil types identified for a few major
 solar development locations. Right) Preparations for the above-ground ground screw testing for
 various soil types at the National Solar Thermal Test Facility.
 The results of this study will provide two main things. First, an analysis of performance of
 differing sizes of ground screw in various soil types and their resistance to loading. This loading
 analysis will inform the maximum size of heliostat reflective area that a ground screw foundation
 will be able to support subsequently projecting cost per foundation. Second, ground screw
 foundations are common for utility scale PV deployments and offer advantages for adopting
 similar field deployment methods including reduced burden to prove scope of environmental
 impact, and the versatility to deploy heliostats that comply with the natural terrain.

 10.2.3 Heliostats Environmental Impact Studies
 As previously stated above, the cultural and environmental impact of field deployment as well as
 the need to reduce danger to endangered and protected species (F1-F4 and F7-F8) are critical to
 addressing field deployment. Anecdotally, previous CSP deployments in the United States have
 encountered high costs when deploying in the habitat of the desert tortoise (Gopherus agassizii),
 a listed threatened species. Some of these costs are associated with the physical relocation of
 tortoises while others are ongoing with the need to contract skilled biology professionals to
 monitor and care for tortoises near the site to name a few. To begin to address the identified gaps
 associated with protected species the developer of tortoise habitat at the Utility Scale Solar PV
 Bonanza Solar Project, (Bonanza) in conjunction with a contracted external biologist from
 Sundance Consultants, LLC (Sundance) and advisement from subject matter experts from the
 Bureau of Land Management, U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service, and the U.S. Geological Survey,
 this site was explored further. A critical expense in assessing the state of species and local

 145

 ecology on a project site is the time associated with skilled biology professionals surveying.
 While this cost cannot be eliminated, wide area surveys using drone photography methods can
 drastically speed up preliminary site surveys and continuous monitoring. Resi Solutions (Resi)
 did just that for the Bonanza solar site with the express focus on identifying tortoises in the area.
 This occurred in conjunction with Sundance’s scoping study of the project area to determine if
 this utility scale project area could be suitable for a future longer-term study focused on the
 impacts of site preparation and their relation to the desert tortoise. The land area covered can be
 found in Figure 77.

 Figure 77. Mojave Desert Bonanza Solar Site. Left) Drone Survey Locations. Right) Biologist
 Scoping Study Location(s).
 The drone survey covered nearly 21 square kilometers in a single week only flying during times
 the desert tortoise was known to be active. In addition to the image acquisition a trained
 convolutional neural network for tortoise detection in aerial photos was used in conjunction with
 distance sampling methods to estimate density and abundance of the desert tortoise for the
 project area. The algorithm detected tortoises and burrows each were manually verified in the
 processed images and the results can be found in Figure 78.

 146

 Figure 78. Mojave Desert Bonanza Solar Site Drone Survey Results. Left) Tortoise Detections.
 Right) Burrow Detections
 In conjunction with results obtained via the drone survey and image detection methods prior
 environmental surveys in the area and similar locations were compared with historic context for
 weather conditions. To summarize, the calculated abundance and density of tortoises in the
 survey area compared with recent similar surveys indicated that the survey area density and
 population was consistent with similar portions of the Mojave Desert. In addition, the local
 tortoise population was hypothesized to be expanding following heightened mortality events
 associated with prior drought conditions.

 The environmental impact of utility scale solar field deployments for heliostats has been
 challenging to quantify. One of the main reasons is the lack of active field deployments in the
 United States. As such, the above work was conducted on land close to ideal conditions for
 heliostats with environmental variables of interest. Some of the major takeaways from the
 surveys on a project site slated for PV were increased awareness for survey techniques, and the
 understanding that previous mitigation and management strategies for accommodating the desert
 tortoise are costly and impactful on tortoises simply by interrupting their natural tendencies. The
 team became aware of the challenge, and lack of data, associated with proposing a utility scale
 solar development where tortoises could be reintroduced to project land after development. To
 address this challenge, a specific question about the ability of desert tortoises to survive within a
 solar field was posed. Tortoises, Gopherus agassizii, exhibit temperature dependent sex
 determination during critical periods of incubation. This means that the temperature of the eggs
 during critical periods is the primary driver of the gender of the offspring. If reintroduced
 tortoises were to burrow and lay a clutch of eggs within a solar field, would the shading offered
 by heliostats influence the temperature range of the burrow outside of normal ranges during
 hatching season? To that end, artificial tortoise burrows have been constructed and deployment
 of those burrows in realistic settings is an ongoing effort. Additional artificial burrows will be
 deployed at the NSTTF with controlled shading environments to bolster this dataset.

 147

 10.2.4 Toward Reducing Barriers to Programmatic Environmental Impact
 Statement Compliant Design
 Previously identified gaps included improving the accuracy of power production models and
 creating standards for modeling reliability, deployment and operations and maintenance
 strategies (F1-F4 and F7-F8). Toward those goals, additional experiments have been established
 to quantify mirror soiling and reflective performance losses. In conjunction with HelioCon
 partner ASTRI, an array of mirrors has been constructed at the National Solar Thermal Test
 Facility that mimics an array established in Australia to capture the impacts of soiling events on
 reflective performance. A side-by-side comparison can be found in Figure 79. The generation of
 the dataset compiling reflectivity of the mirrors and airborne particulate matter is ongoing. When
 complete, the more robust understanding of soiling deposition on mirrors at a particular
 orientation and the associated reflective losses can be leveraged to increase the robustness of
 power production modeling. In addition, predictions of soiling rate based on in situ weather can
 inform O&M strategies from a conceptual deployment stage such as estimating required washing
 frequency.

 Figure 79. Mirror Soiling Arrays. Left) Array and Ballasts at the NSTTF. Right) ASTRI Array
 Constructed in Australia.
 The mirror arrays shown in Figure 79 are aligned with the cardinal directions and set at a series
 of angles with reference mirrors completely face up as well as face down.
 Feedback received by the Field Deployment technical advisory committee was that field
 deployment challenges continue to be incredibly specific to location choice. In addition, visual
 hazard analysis and the stewardship of community visual resources has been and continues to be
 a challenge for developers. Within the many sections of guidelines and constraints that could be
 imposed on a field deployment project as per the 2024 Final Utility-Scale Solar Energy
 Programmatic Environmental Impact Statement, visual resource analysis was identified as a
 critical item to address and take steps to produce standard analysis techniques. Other identified
 gaps including the safety and logistics of glint/blare/beam (F5-F6), identifying reasons for
 community opposition (F1-F4), and standardizing the modeling methods to address permitting
 barriers (F1-F4) further bolstered this choice. To specifically address many of these methods a
 series of glare analysis scenarios have started development utilizing a hybrid approach between
 analytical and computational glare estimates. This work leverages the rich history of glare
 analysis produced by SNL. Current scenarios in development include receiver reflectance
 glint/glare, standby position glint/glare, and stow position assuming reflective surfaces are face

 148

 up. These methods have produced the preliminary results that can be found in Figure 80. In
 addition to addressing previously identified gaps, this glint and glare analysis produces inputs
 that can directly be used to calculate ocular hazards for observers and takes many strides to
 address the “Visual Resource” and hazard analysis sections of the 2024 Final Utility-Scale Solar
 Energy Programmatic Environmental Impact Statement.

 Figure 80. Example preliminary output of glare analysis. Left) Irradiance contours of receiver
 reflectance glare for an observer at ground level for a receiver at 200 feet above ground. Center)
 Calculated glare profile associated with a single aim point for the NSTTF heliostat field 1000
 meters above the standby aim point. Right) Sample of glare analysis for upward facing stowed
 heliostats for an observer located 60 degrees from normal for the field and the associated
 irradiance an observer would experience as a function of distance.

 10.3 Gap Updates and Priorities Supporting Intermediate Goal
 A technical advisory committee was convened to discuss the path forward. It has been shown
 that the largest plants will have the lowest LCOE. Small plants may not produce sufficient power
 to properly amortize the investments in power blocks, towers, and storage silos. However, the
 complexity of deploying a large-scale plant (i.e., 50-150 MWe) may be insurmountable without a
 directive from authorities as is the case in China.

 The technical advisory committee delivered the following suggestions:

 • Pristine land will likely be harder to permit than brownfield retrofits.
 • Optimize systems < 1 MWe to retain classification as a “small-scale generator” eligible
 for expedited application procedures, fewer technical requirements, and reduced fees.
 • Beam down systems may be better suited to couple with existing industrial plants
 because equipment is already integrated and positioned low to the ground the visual
 impact of the bright spot can alarm detractors and stimulate opposition from neighboring
 communities.
 • Dust and environmental impact should be eliminated for expedited deployment in open
 spaces.
 • Translocation of wildlife is costly and almost always harmful to both the translocated
 species and the ecological co-dependent species. How a field is deployed and maintained
 determines whether wildlife can cohabitate within a field or not. Specifically, grading
 with heavy machinery can result in elimination of plant species that take decades to
 return ensuring ecological dependents will also not be able to return. Using boom lifts to
 drill many holes from a central position has been demonstrated by BrightSource as a
 means to mitigate field deployment impact who also adopted facet cleaning and
 vegetation pruning methods using manual teams on foot. The costs of manual cleaning

 149

 may be offset because it reduces water consumption and allows vegetation to grow within
 the field also reduces dust and facet soiling. Manual washing also allows for greater
 power density on the land since additional spacing between rows of heliostats for
 washing truck roads is not necessary nor is it necessary to arrange heliostats in rows or
 level land.
 • Investor/lender risk is very high due to initial capital required for large plants. It often
 requires several entities to be materially invested which can create disagreements and
 delays if stakeholder priorities conflict.
 • CSP makes a great companion to PV. HelioCon can develop methods to facilitate low-
 cost deployment by leveraging methods and components used to rack fixed tilt and single
 access tracking PV.

 Proposed path forward: model a heliostat field deployment that avoids the major obstacles
 currently facing the industry.

 • Environmental Impacts – microclimate and dust are a concern for desert habitats and
 opposition from the environmental community that would otherwise support solar
 energy.
 o Land topology is left pristine with no leveling or vegetation removal. Field
 deployment utilizes boom equipment to insert many pedestals from a single
 machine location, as was done by BrightSource in Ashalim.
 o Washing is manual to minimize water use and trim vegetation as necessary.
 Avoids the need to level egress routes for washing equipment.
 o Temperature studies are conducted to determine the microclimate impacts of
 heliostats on ground temperatures, air temperatures, and subterranean burrow
 temperatures (ongoing from previous work).
 • Land Use Permitting – develop a field on private land held by industry. Size according to
 the lesser of available space on lot or 10 MWth (<1 MWe baseload) and contribute only a
 portion of heat to storage media.
 o Model a CSP concept in New Mexico with enough power to roast chili peppers
 (“chile” in New Mexico) or sanitize glass.
 o HelioCon could provide technoeconomic models that assume existing land is
 already owned by the customer. Models could factor scenarios where excess
 energy is sold at market price or negative.
 • Visual Impact – maximum tower height inclusive of all receiver equipment will be less
 than 200 ft tall. Perform design study on secondary collectors to minimize visibility of
 bright spot and glare to reduce community opposition. Develop set of recommendations
 for industry standards for visual impact metrics, methodology, and requirements with the
 goal to streamline the burden of evidence needed to obtain permitting of a solar tower
 system.
 o To make the safety case for frozen heliostat impacts, HelioCon can determine the
 cost and feasibility of developing complex glare models capable of showing the
 flash impact of frozen heliostats at every position of the day over an array of
 position such as a road or flight path. (Forge Solar is interested in developing such
 a model.)

 150

 o Secondary collectors can be studied by HelioCon as a means to aid in urban
 deployment.
 • Capitalization – define the field layout and deployment methodology and costs for a
 $1,000,000 field (~10 MWt) using BrightSource heliostat capital costs (~$70/m2) using
 adjacent lands at American Gypsum, Bernalillo, NM.
 o Determine the impacts of increased optical error (as a proxy for lower quality and
 cost) as a function of distance from the receiver to infer the potential cost/m2 for
 short range heliostats on an industrial lot.

 In summary, the metrics that have steered CSP design have heavily weighted LCOE, efficiency,
 and concentration ratio. However, investments in large plants that would excel by these measures
 are risky to an extent that CSP is not currently being considered in the energy plan of California.
 While there are no other state plans, CSP has not been awarded a baseload energy project since
 the first two concurrent plants, Ivanpah and Tonopah. Therefore, it is necessary for industry to
 prove performance and reliability in lower risk situations. The technical advisory committee and
 HelioCon field deployment sub-team proposes to assess small capacity (≤10MWth), standard
 heliostat design, deployment on existing industrial or agricultural sites as a means to potentially
 reduce risk by reducing start-up capital costs to the extent that few or even one investor can
 develop the project, avoiding new design technology risk, avoiding transmission buildouts and
 lengthy permitting processes needed to develop pristine habitats by hosting the heliostat field on
 private land where a single entity can decide what can be developed. While this set of
 opportunities bypasses the historic barriers to deployment for CSP, it exposes the technology to
 new risks that can be assessed more critically by the HelioCon team. Likely barriers to
 deployment are primarily focused on safety and visual impacts to humans working or residing
 near small heliostat plants.
