 11 Wind Load
 The wind load subtask is concerned with the development of measurement, modelling and
 characterization techniques for the wind load design of heliostats and mitigation strategies for
 existing and planned CSP plants.

 11.1 Review of Tier 1 Gaps
 Tier 1 gaps related to wind load in the HelioCon roadmap report are as follows:

 WL1 Insufficient wind measurement and characterization at heliostat field sites

 WL2 Lack of understanding on the impact of atmospheric turbulence on dynamic loading and
 tracking error

 WL3 Lack of understanding on wind load on heliostats in array configurations

 WL4 Missing design standards for determining heliostat wind load coefficients and safety
 factors

 Field measurement campaigns (WL1) are crucial to validate and better understand limitations of
 scale model wind tunnel experiments. Comprehensive and high-resolution datasets in operational
 CSP fields and in outdoor real-world conditions are useful to validate analytical models and train
 computational models of wind effects in heliostat field ray tracing simulations.

 Investigations of wind-induced optical errors (WL2) need to be generalized and coupled with in
 situ metrology methods and TEA to understand and quantify the performance and financial
 impacts. Wind load mitigation strategies such as mesh grids, perimeter fencing, aerodynamic
 shaping, and dynamic stowing have been demonstrated in experimental or numerical studies;
 however, their techno-economic feasibility has not been evaluated.

 Measurements of wind and load variations in heliostat arrays (WL3) need to quantify field zones
 or regions where reductions and amplifications of wind loads are expected and should work
 toward determining plant operation strategies to reduce overly cautious downtime due to wind.

 Heliostat wind load guidelines and standards (WL4) to be developed can leverage American
 Society of Civil Engineers (ASCE) wind design guidelines and propose a revised version to
 include heliostat requirements. Wind load characterization outdoors is not feasible for most
 developers, hence indoor characterization plus a safety factor may be a suitable approach in the
 absence of outdoor measurements.

 11.2 Review of Tier 2 Gaps Related to Computational Modeling
 Tier 2 gaps related to computational modeling of heliostat wind loads in the HelioCon roadmap
 report are as follows:

 WL5 Insufficient computational modeling accuracy of dynamic wind loads on heliostats

 WL8 Insufficient computational modeling accuracy of atmospheric turbulence

 152

 Current computational models adopt idealized assumptions for atmospheric turbulence. Studies
 considering realistic field conditions such as complex terrains, gusts, and thermal stratification
 are limited. CSP deployment in tropical areas is constrained by cloudiness and cyclones. Site-
 specific wind characterization in such climates is missing and currently relies on wind maps at
 standard heights of 10 meters and greater.

 11.3 Related Projects and Progress Made Toward Closing Gaps
 The community has made great progress in understanding the wind effects applying to heliostats,
 for example through wind tunnel studies and in collecting in situ wind load data in solar fields.
 Several field measurement campaigns have been conducted in the last few years in operational
 CSP fields and in outdoor real-world conditions. Related projects with key publications cited and
 their progress toward each of the wind load Tier 1 gaps are summarized in the following:

 11.3.1 Outdoor Field Measurement Campaigns on Wind Loads (WL1)
 The University of Adelaide characterized in real-world settings how wind conditions translate to
 static and dynamic wind loads on a single heliostat at the Atmospheric Boundary Layer Research
 Facility (ABLRF) (Emes et al. 2024). Comparison of load coefficients at the ABLRF with a 1:6
 scale model heliostat in the University of Adelaide wind tunnel indicated good agreement for the
 prevailing wind direction impacting the front of the heliostat. Drag force coefficients were
 overestimated, and lift force coefficients were underestimated in wind tunnel experiments
 compared to field measurements at the ABLRF.

 DLR collected field measurement data of atmospheric wind, pressure distributions, loads and
 tracking data on individual real-scale heliostats at the heliostat testing platform in Juelich. The
 DLR-CIEMAT project AdaptedHelio collected in-field wind measurements using two scanning
 LIDARs and 3D ultrasonic anemometers within the CESA-1 heliostat field at the Plataforma
 Solar de Almeria (PSA).

 NLR collected first-of-a-kind wind, tracking and loads data in parabolic trough collector and
 heliostat fields at operational power plants in the United States. Comprehensive datasets (Egerer
 et al. 2021) that are publicly available have been analyzed to characterize the impact of turbulent
 wind on bending and torsional loads on parabolic trough collectors (Egerer et al. 2024;
 Stanislawski et al. 2025).

 11.3.2 Atmospheric Turbulence Effects on Dynamic Loads (WL2)
 Field measurements by NLR in multiple rows of an operational parabolic trough field showed
 wind-induced torsional errors of up to 18 mrad (1 degree) in row 1 at positive tilt angles for wind
 approaching the back of the troughs (Stanislawski et al. 2025). Comparison of drag and moment
 coefficients showed that scale-model wind tunnel experiments underpredict peak load
 coefficients in rows 1, 2 and 4 of a collector field (Egerer et al. 2024).

 DLR field measurements on a Stellio heliostat (Blume et al. 2023a) and University of Adelaide
 wind tunnel tests (Jafari et al. 2019) investigated how turbulent wind impacts dynamic loads
 through the development of aerodynamic admittance functions for drag forces, lift forces and
 hinge moments. Aerodynamic admittance functions showed that reduced (non-dimensional)
 frequencies between 0.1 and 1 were most effective in generating aerodynamic loads. Time-

 153

 averaged wind pressure distributions on the Stellio heliostat were generally consistent with scale
 model wind tunnel testing for one elevation angle and a range of wind directions (Blume et al.
 2023a). Large eddy simulation (LES) of synthetic atmospheric boundary layer (ABL) turbulence
 inflow profiles (Li et al. 2024) and resulting wind pressures and loads on a 1:10.5 scale heliostat
 validated with wind tunnel tests have demonstrated progress in computational modeling of near-
 surface ABL turbulence for heliostat wind load applications.

 DLR conducted field measurements of dynamic photogrammetry on a Stellio heliostat and found
 that the wind-induced tracking deviation of 0.44 mrad RMS comprised one third of the total
 tracking deviation (Blume et al. 2020). DLR field measurements of facet displacement amplitude
 spectra and tracking deviation spectra on a medium-sized (Stellio) heliostat and a small-sized (T-
 type) heliostat revealed spectral peaks in the range of 2-3 Hz (Blume et al. 2023b). Analysis of
 high-frequency ABL wind and loads data by DLR and University of Adelaide have identified
 dominant frequencies and load distributions on heliostats for consideration of atmospheric
 turbulence on dynamic wind loads (Jafari et al. 2024), extreme value analysis of peak loads
 (Emes et al. 2022), and analytical models of fatigue loads (Pfahl 2024).

 11.3.3 Wind Load Variations in Heliostat Array Configurations (WL3)
 The University of Adelaide characterized atmospheric turbulent wind flow and wind-driven load
 variations over the first several rows of radially staggered (Marano et al. 2023) and linearly
 staggered (Marano et al. 2024) heliostat arrays in wind tunnel tests. Drag and lift force
 coefficients on heliostats in downstream rows within the linearly staggered array decreased for
 elevation angles of 45° and greater, whereas drag and lift forces increased for elevation angles of
   30° and smaller (Emes 2024).

 The DLR-CIEMAT project AdaptedHelio characterized wind flow within the CESA-1 heliostat
 field at PSA (Paulus et al. 2024). Wind speed and turbulence intensity variations were largest at
 the heliostat elevation axis height and similar for operating and stow positions, whereas wind
 speed and turbulence intensity varied less at the upper edge of the heliostat in vertical position
 and showed a stronger dependence on the orientation of upstream rows.

 NLR conducted comprehensive field measurements of the atmospheric turbulent wind conditions
 and resulting structural wind loads at four rows in a parabolic trough field at the Nevada Solar
 One (NSO) plant and three individual heliostats (two exterior and one interior) in the Crescent
 Dunes power tower plant using meteorological masts, a scanning LIDAR and structural load
 sensors (Egerer et al. 2021; 2024; Stanislawski et al. 2025; Yellapantula et al. 2024). Significant
 variations in localized wind speed, ABL turbulence characteristics and loads were observed
 between different rows of the collector fields.

 11.3.4 Wind Load Design Guidelines and Standards Development (WL4)
 DLR conducted analysis of field measurements of wind-driven loads on two full-scale heliostats
 (Stellio and T-type Helfer) and developed a simplified analytical model for wind loads and wind-
 induced tracking deviations (Blume et al. 2023b). The model incorporated inputs of mean wind
 speed, wind spectra, mean moment coefficients and aerodynamic admittance functions at
 different angles of attack, geometric parameters of the heliostat and mechanical parameters of the
 heliostat. Experimental procedures for a pulling test with concentrator deviation measurements to

 154

 derive concentrator stiffness, eigenfrequencies and damping ratios were also developed and will
 be published in the future.

 A simplified technique was developed by DLR to couple wind pressure distributions from large
 eddy simulations (LES) with finite element analysis simulations to generate dynamic wind
 loading on smaller heliostats (Blum et al. 2024; Pfahl 2025). There have been several additional
 computational modeling studies involving wind driven loading of solar collectors (Li et al. 2024;
 Merarda et al. 2024; Fadlallah et al. 2021; Sosa-Flores et al. 2022; Xiao et al. 2022) with
 promising approaches for validation with wind tunnel tests and field measurements.

 11.4 Gap Updates and Priorities Supporting Intermediate Goal
 Several field measurement campaigns have been conducted in the last few years in operational
 CSP fields and in outdoor real-world conditions. These campaigns have generated high-fidelity
 datasets on wind and load measurements and scientific publications with unique insights not
 previously available in public literature. Progress has been made in measuring and characterizing
 wind conditions at heights relevant to heliostats (WL1). There has been some preliminary
 analysis relating localized wind measurements in field measurements to dynamic loads (WL2).
 Initial wind tunnel studies and field measurements have investigated the variations of flow and
 loads within an array of heliostats in a sector of a field (WL3). This has led to improved
 understanding of localized wind profiles and atmospheric turbulence impacts on wind loads in
 different rows at the exterior region of a field. There is a need to distill scientific understanding
 into practical tools and guidelines (WL4) in a form that can be applied by industry to develop
 new plants. There is a high priority to develop a streamlined path to provide heliostat wind load
 guidelines with industry input, however the complexity of wind loads in relation to different
 heliostat designs makes this a challenging task. More time and effort are needed for researchers
 to analyze the datasets and publish insights and findings to the research community that are
 concurrently disseminated as practical outcomes with impact and lessons learned to industry. For
 example, dynamic wind effects and impact of fatigue loads on actuators (WL2), and flow
 variations in the interior of the field and load variations in arrays (WL3) are still not well
 understood. There is a high priority to make progress toward WL4 in the form of a guideline for
 wind measurements and procedures relevant to the design and establishment of new CSP plants.
 In addition, a second guideline for measurement, characterization and analysis procedures to
 inform the testing requirements of a heliostat design for ultimate and operating load
 considerations. Guidelines developed for WL4 should initially be developed with higher
 uncertainty and safety factors based on existing wind load estimation tools and currently used
 methods from wind tunnel experiments and fluid-structure interaction simulations. It is
 anticipated that wind load estimation procedures are progressively improved with quality
 understanding from scientific developments in field measurements, wind tunnel experiments and
 numerical simulations to reduce the uncertainty level in wind load predictions.

 Considerable research has been conducted on the measurement and analysis of static and
 dynamic wind loads and their impact on the structural design of heliostats, however further
 analysis of their effect on pointing accuracy is limited. Coupling on-site wind measurements with
 field operation, such as through real-time closed loop control, is a promising approach for wind

 155

 compensation at plant level. Field measurements and analysis of wind ramps and gust detection
 using scanning LIDARs for wind forecasting have been investigated to improve heliostat field
 operation strategies, such as accounting for short-term gust events including thunderstorms.
 Wind load mitigation strategies, such as mesh grids, perimeter fencing, aerodynamic retrofits,
 and dynamic stowing, have been demonstrated in experimental and numerical studies; however,
 their techno-economic feasibility has not been evaluated. Research on pointing accuracy of
 heliostats under wind loads is restricted to field measurements and ray tracing simulations, which
 are expensive and limited to specific concentrating systems and individual heliostats.

 Current computational models adopt idealized assumptions on atmospheric turbulence. Modeling
 approaches generating synthetic turbulence at the inlet have been developed but are limited in
 accurately reproducing near-surface wind profiles and atmospheric turbulence characteristics at
 heights below 10 meters that are relevant to heliostats. Recently there have been several studies
 investigating wind driven loads on solar collectors, especially heliostats. Increased availability of
 high-performance computing infrastructure has enabled higher fidelity large eddy simulation
 (LES) based studies of wind driven loading on heliostats with better resolution of dynamic
 effects. Additionally, LES based studies allow for application of more realistic boundary
 conditions representative of atmospheric boundary layers observed in the field. However, deep
 array simulations representing complex arrangement of several rows of heliostats are
 prohibitively expensive and therefore such studies are rare. Deep array simulations are essential
 to fully understand the impact of wind flow patterns induced by the upstream rows and their
 impact of dynamic loading of downstream heliostats in the interior of the field. Current
 simulations typically model rigid and stationary heliostats at various static azimuth and elevation
 angles. These simulations fail to fully capture the dynamic wind effects generated by the
 dynamic deflection of mirror structures. More research is needed to fully enable fluid structure
 simulations with several heliostats under the influence of realistic atmospheric boundary layer
 profiles. Coupling these simulations with ray tracing simulations will additionally inform the
 impact of dynamic deflection of mirror structures on overall optical efficiency. Enabling these
 high-fidelity but predictive and computationally inexpensive simulations will help the CSP
 industry establish plant efficiency metrics and significantly reduce risks associated with
 commissioning of solar field with newer designs of heliostats.

 156

 12 Soiling
 The soiling subtask is concerned with the development of soiling measurement, modelling, and
 techniques to characterize soiling and plan mitigation measures for existing and planned CSP
 plants. This includes:

 • Measurements of dust characteristics and soiling losses of heliostats fields

 • Modelling and characterization of soiling processes including development and
 validation of models describing the key mechanisms

 • Mitigation measures, including optimal mirror washing resources and deployment and
 passive mitigation techniques (e.g., coatings).

 12.1 Review of Tier 1 Gaps
 Despite its importance, soiling remains a complex, only partially resolved challenge. This
 roadmap report reviewed the state of the art on heliostat soiling and mitigation and summarized
 key gaps that were identified by the soiling subtask group in consultation with the broader CSP
 community in the HelioCon Roadmap Workshop in 2021.

 Table 18 summarizes the required functionality of the proposed solutions, justification, benefits
 from addressing the given gap, and the proposed addressing strategies for the four Tier 1 gaps on
 soiling. Among these gaps, So1 and So4 are paramount for enhancing the performance of CSP
 plants and reducing the risk for investors, whereas So2 and So3 offer high-potential opportunities
 for reducing cleaning and soiling costs.

 Gap So1 was a high priority due to the variability of soiling rates observed in the field. A
 procedure to assess these rates and (together with solutions from So4) different cleaning
 strategies was viewed as a critical need. Gap So2 was motivated by a number of high-profile
 automated cleaning systems and the possibility of both reducing manual labor and increasing the
 effectiveness of cleaning. Gap So3 was motivated by the success of coatings in photovoltaics and
 the possibility of reducing cleaning needs by applying coatings that reduce soiling rates and/or
 improve the effectiveness of (natural) cleaning events. Gap So4 represents a broad range of
 questions from the CSP community regarding how the impact of soiling can be mitigated by
 design and O&M choices.

 12.2 Related Projects and Progress Made Toward Closing Gaps
 This section summarizes the projects that were undertaken to address these gaps. Two of the four
 gaps have had significant progress (So1 and So4), while little progress has been made on So2
 and So3.

 12.2.1 CSP-Relevant Site Soiling Campaigns and Model Field Testing (So1)
 Queensland University of Technology (QUT) has conducted site soiling studies in Australia —
 two in Queensland, two in Victoria, and three in South Australia. Each site has multiple
 campaigns and analysis is discussed in publications (Picotti et al. 2023; Anderson et al. 2023).
 The data from four of the sites has been made publicly available at
 https://github.com/cholette/mirror_soiling_data. The data for one site (Mount Isa) can be seen in
 Figure 81. Four soiling campaigns (two locations) were conducted with RayGen as a part of a
 commercial research project, and it is expected that the campaigns from at least one of these sites
 will be made available through the soiling database. Additional campaigns at the National Solar
 Thermal Testing Facility (NSTTF) have been planned and equipment has been purchased, but

 159

 shipping and equipment failures have delayed these tests. Nonetheless, once these issues have
 been resolved the NSTTF data will be added to the database.

 The data from all campaigns have been utilized to address important aspects of So1 to:

 • “Stress test” soiling models. In (Picotti et al. 2023) QUT — in collaboration with
 Politecnico di Milano (PoliMi) — examined the predictive capability of an extended
 version of a previously developed physical model (Picotti et al. 2018) against a newly
 developed simplified physical model. The simplified model performed well in the
 experiments available at the time. A more comprehensive benchmarking of a broader set
 of models is ongoing, again in collaboration with PoliMi.
 • Develop and test prediction uncertainty characterization. Methods were developed to
 characterize the statistical uncertainty of soiling model predictions for the first time in
 (Picotti et al. 2023). A new method was also developed to characterize the statistical
 distribution of daily soiling losses (see Figure 82) and presented at SolarPACES (Picotti
           et al. 2024).

 The data from three campaigns has yet to be made publicly available due to confidentiality
 with the industry partners. This data was collected at one active and two potential sites for
 heliostat fields with industry partners. It is expected that this data will be made available in
 the soiling database at a later date.

 160

 Figure 81. Data from Mount Isa soiling study. The error bars indicate the standard deviation of the
 mean measurements while the color indicates the orientation of the mirror face. The black line
 represents the predictions of a soiling model while the grey area indicates the prediction
 confidence interval (starting from day zero) Source: (Picotti et al. 2023).

 161

 Figure 82. Statistical distributions of daily soiling losses for three different Australian locations.
 Adapted from (Picotti et al. 2024).

 12.2.2 Cleaning Optimization Studies (So4)
 Work related to So4 has largely focused on optimizing cleaning resourcing (trucks, staff). While
 a significant body of work was available from soiling subtask researchers in this area prior to
 HelioCon, two studies were conducted under the Consortium:

 • Cleaning optimization for a candidate CSP site in Queensland, Australia (Anderson et al.
 2023). This study was a collaboration between QUT, PoliMi, and Vast to optimize
 cleaning for a potential CSP site. The wide-ranging study optimized the cleaning
 resources considering, for the first time: (1) the oversizing of the field relative to the
 receiver, (2) uncertainty in the airborne dust loadings, (3) different stow positions, and (4)
 the effect of different dispatching strategies. This study was also the first to study a site
 from experiments through to optimization.
 • QUT and PoliMi collaborated on a study on the design of solar fields considering O&M
 activities (which included soiling). The study developed an approach to design solar field
 considering O&M costs (e.g., cleaning, repair). The solar field size was optimized
 considering soiling (among other effects) which was shown to affect the optimal field
 size under certain conditions. The study had been accepted and will appear in Solar
 Energy soon (Lupi et al., n.d.).
 • Soiling analysis and mirror washing study for a U.S. industry partner. NLR and QUT
 conducted a study of the soiling and mirror washing practices of a U.S. CSP operator
 using measurement data provided by the partner. NLR undertook a measurement
 campaign to assess spatial distribution of soiling losses and compare the loss assessments
 from two reflectance measurement device. The study included the use of two separate
 scheduling models, one with a threshold policy and one with a fixed schedule that varies

 162

 by month. Both models suggested a washing policy that was similar to the existing
 schedule at the policy, with the exception of some minor seasonal variation.

 12.3 Other Related Activities (So1, So4, Tier 2 Gaps)
 A number of other activities have addressed the So1 and So4 gaps as well as some Tier 2 gaps
 (Zhu et al. 2022), Table 27 that have relevance to So1.

 • Soiling aspects of the UW-Madison-Led PROMETHEUS project (So4). University of
 Wisconsin Madison, PoliMi, and QUT are collaborating on the PROMETHEUS project,
 which secured funding from the Round 2 Request for Proposals in 2024. The project aims
 at developing an innovative tool to improve real-time heliostat control to optimize the
 aiming strategy to account for flux intensity impact on receiver lifetime, the actual status
 of the heliostats (soiling distribution, broken mirrors and/or drivers), and required driver
 power consumption during transient operation. Adapting the aiming strategy to account
 for soiling losses is expected to provide another lever to mitigate soiling impacts on plant
 performance and O&M.
 • A collaborative study on the effects of moisture on soiled mirrors was conducted by QUT
 in collaboration with PoliMi and Fraunhofer Institute for Solar Energy Systems (ISE) as a
 part of the SFERA III project (Anderson et al. 2024). This study addresses a Tier 3 gap
 that is a key understudied source of uncertainty in soiling.
 • QUT participated in an international Round Robin on particle counting using image
 analysis. The inter-lab variability of image analysis of particles (e.g., counts) was
 analyzed for consideration in future studies.
 • QUT was part of a successful bid led by the University of Adelaide to establish a state-of-
 the-art facility for atmospheric wind, dust, and plume measurements (“LE240100116 —
 Facilities for Atmospheric Boundary Layer Evaluation and Testing,” n.d.). As a part of
 this bid, the team obtained a Tracking Cleanliness Sensor (TraCS) (Wolfertstetter et al.
 2012) for automated reflectance measurements and dust samplers that are being placed on
 a mobile platform for future soiling campaigns.
 • Utilizing the developed soiling database, QUT and PoliMi researchers are comparing
 airborne concentration from satellite reanalysis products (MERRA-21) with measured
 suspended particulates. The results will provide insight on the accuracy of satellite-
 derived soiling data for estimation of local soiling rates.

 12.4 Gap Updates and Priorities Supporting Intermediate Goal
 A number of stakeholders from industry, national labs, and academia were identified and
 separate interviews were conducted. The interviews were conducted initially by QUT. In each
 discussion, the Tier 1 gaps were presented along with the progress toward their closure. A
 discussion followed regarding the future priorities for the consortium in the area of soiling.
 Following these interviews, a meeting of the Soiling Subtask group was convened to interpret the
 feedback and develop priorities for the remainder of HelioCon.

 1
 https://gmao.gsfc.nasa.gov/reanalysis/MERRA-2/

 163

 Progress to date has been strongest on So1 and So4, with many campaigns and studies focused
 on developing the methodology for site soiling characterization and planning mitigation
 measures. This progress is due mostly to industry interest — So1 has attracted significant interest
 from those designing new plants, while So4 has attracted interest from operators. This was
 reinforced by the stakeholder discussions, where there was general agreement that guidance on
 site characterization was valuable for de-risking designs.

 On the other hand, progress on So2 (automation) and So3 (coatings) has been slow. The
 comparatively low interest of stakeholders in these areas seems to be driven by perceived
 lackluster performance of existing automated cleaning systems and questions surrounding the
 durability of coatings. For automation, it seems that the challenges inhibiting adoption are plant
 specific and best understood by operators. The research needed to address these issues remains
 unclear. For coatings, the ongoing research in coatings in photovoltaics may be sufficient to
 assess its feasibility for CSP applications in the future.

 The stakeholder discussions also revealed a number of suggestions, but the two most consistent
 themes were:

 • So1 is the most valuable activity among the Tier 1 gaps and rests upon a number of key
 technical developments (e.g., uncertainty assessment, going beyond single-incident angle
 measurements, understanding rain/dew effects). The main value of this activity is de-
 risking new plants.
 • Provide more advice to existing operators. Stakeholders asked for standard measurement
 protocols and more work on understanding the value of different measurements (e.g.,
 airborne dust, sampling reflectance around the field, comparison of different
 reflectometers).

 Taken together, the HelioCon Soiling Subtask suggests the following path forward:

 • So1 and So4 are valuable activities that should continue.
 • While So2 and So3 are important gaps, addressing them should be lower priority. This
 evaluation is based on the stakeholder feedback noted above, the time/resource
 availability within HelioCon, and the possibility of leveraging ongoing work on coatings
 in photovoltaics to assess feasibility for CSP. Yet, the Soiling Subtask should seek to
 leverage insights gained from its activities to assess possible future automation pathways
 (e.g., heliostat-mounted devices vs. automated trucks).
 • Prioritize providing operators with advice on measurements and mitigation measures.
 This suggests prioritizing activities to address the following Tier 2 gaps:
 o So5: No standard method for solar field reflectance sampling has been adopted.
 o So6: Typical processes for assessing field reflectance are labor-intensive, and the
 value of automated ones is not yet well understood.
 • Develop a report/standard on the state-of-the-art site characterization methodology for
 use by industry to assess site soiling. This work should be conducted in close
 collaboration with (and by contribution to) relevant efforts in SolarPACES.
 • Modeling improvements and understanding prediction uncertainty for site
 characterization. The work to date has pointed to a number of technical challenges that
 must be addressed for site characterization:

 164

 o Understanding the stability of airborne size distribution. The overall airborne
 particulate mass does not provide information on the distribution of sizes in air,
 which can strongly affect the reflectance loss. Understanding the stability of this
 distribution and the influential factors will help in characterizing the uncertainty
 and the
 o Develop methods to characterize additional sources of uncertainty, including
 those due to unknown (and largely unobservable) changes in atmospheric
 stability, changes in dust size distribution, the existence of horizontal deposition
 modes (not included in most modes outside of very small particles).
 o Refinement of angle of incidence (AOI) models. It is well known that AOI
 dependance of reflectance is exacerbated by soiling, yet there is little work on
 developing and validating quantitative models for predicting this dependence for
 soiled heliostats. Such models would be quite valuable for assisting operators in
 understanding this aspect of soiling and how it affects their plant (e.g., changes to
 the flux map due to inhomogeneity of soiling across the field).

 165

 13 Heliostat Reference Design Development
 Over the span of at least four decades, a vast number of heliostats have been conceived,
 prototyped and offered commercially. Of the many heliostat concepts, designs and mass
 production runs that have been achieved, it remains unclear which design, if any, is best suited as
 a long-term solution given the tradeoffs, cost-benefits, and strengths inherent to each of the
 various designs. Recognizing how challenging it is to simultaneously meet the cost,
 performance, and reliability targets necessary for commercial viability, we believe industry
 would benefit from an effort that gathers best practices and lessons learned into a reference
 heliostat design effort.

 Substantial variation exists between designs, even those of similar general architecture. The
 many different designs are all aimed at solving the same fundamental problem: a heliostat of
 high beam quality, high tracking accuracy, high resilience, long lifetime, and low cost. However,
 only a few designs have been installed in utility scale solar power plants. No new recent
 developments show the overall cohort to be converging toward a globally accepted universal
 design. Barriers are high for innovative constructions because

 1. It is difficult to prove the new heliostat’s fitness for large scale implementation, due to
 missing standards and standardized performance evaluation capabilities (see section 6,
 Metrology and Standards).
 2. There is little information publicly available about the performance of existing
 heliostats. Therefore, it is difficult to quantify the benefits of a new design or to find
 sound arguments for cost-performance tradeoffs.
 3. Small companies don’t have the resources to start from scratch and repeat errors and
 lessons learned that others have already surpassed.
 We propose a new task under the HelioCon umbrella to develop industry-supported engineering
 design requirements for a reference heliostat for both heat and electricity driven cases. Following
 a study of available information on most of existing heliostat designs, important patterns
 emerged that will inform this reference design together with the body of knowledge that is
 available within the HelioCon team.

 The purpose of the heliostat reference design documentation is twofold:

 1. Collaboratively bring together industry knowledge, HelioCon advances, and past and
 present research in one publicly available set of documents that serve as a baseline for
 future heliostat developments and improvements. Bringing this knowledge concisely
 together serves to reduce development costs, avoids repeating mistakes of the past, and
 reduces risks and uncertainty in future projects.
 2. Remove barriers to new entrants to the heliostat design space and improve the
 bankability future heliostat fields.
 Publishing heliostat design requirements or any conceptual designs that may result will not be in
 competition with industry but rather provide a base starting point for all of industry to continue
 to innovate and compete to reduce costs and improve performance. An example of a similar

 166

 approach is the 5 MW reference wind turbine developed by NLR in 2009 (Jonkman et al. 2009).
 While this turbine was never commercialized exactly as designed, it did serve as a baseline for
 research, simulation, and prototype development. This template became invaluable not only for
 academia, but for industry players seeking to validate new blades, drivetrains, and control
 systems against a recognized standard.

 13.1 Related Gap Analysis
 A review of the gaps reported in the first HelioCon roadmap (Table 19) reveals that the lack of
 accessible knowledge along with benchmark designs, requirement, and procedures was and
 continues to be an issue.

 Our extensive study of the heliostat state of the art revealed additional key insights:

 • Changing load conditions for heliostat fields in recent years is primarily due to better
 understanding of environmental behaviors. Wind loading, for example, is much better
 understood but tools with this knowledge need to be readily available to design engineers.
 • The range of motion requirements has not changed, but if it were more granularly
 understood, it could be adjusted. This may result in cost savings.
 • Accuracy requirements are a known tradeoff between field performance and cost, but
 tools for quantifying this tradeoff are still limited. Future CSP projects favor field
 performance to target higher temperature applications.
 • Beam quality and facet performance continues to be the area of greatest interest from
 industry stakeholders, as it is believed to be the area with high potential for overall cost
 reduction for CSP energy. Unfortunately, these plant-level cost reductions are not
 generally captured in a simplistic $/m2 cost metric for heliostats.
 • Design modularity, allowing individual components to be replaced or updated as
 necessary to meet project-specific requirements without affecting the rest of the heliostat,
 would improve design re-usability and limit engineering expenses.
 • Overall plant performance is related to reliability and serviceability of heliostats, however
 there is currently a shortage of publicly available requirements for heliostat serviceability
 or operations and maintenance.

 productivity manufacturing lines

 13.1.1 Stakeholder Feedback

   13.1.1.1       Approach
 In early 2025, we approached CSP experts from research and industry to get feedback about the
 proposed idea of a baseline reference heliostat design. These discussions were conducted as part
 of the stakeholder interviews discussed in Section 6, Metrology and Standards. We had nine
 conversations with experts from the United States, Europe, China, and Australia covering the
 following fields of expertise (some overlap is present):

 • Five developers and/or operators of utility scale solar power projects.
 • Six developers of heliostat designs and solar field designs.
 • Four developers or experts on optical metrology tools (in-house or as third-party service).

 We talked mostly with the people from engineering departments and the conclusions from these
 discussions are therefore mostly taken from an engineering point of view.

 After presenting the proposed idea, we started the discussion with three main questions:

 • Do you see value/importance of this endeavor to industry?
 • What information would you want to find when doing research about heliostats?
 • What features, components, technologies etc. would you want to see studied and
 published?
 • What design features are most troublesome?

 168

 13.1.1.2 Main Takeaways
 Figure 83 summarizes the main themes that came up in multiple discussions. We can see that the
 idea of a baseline heliostat design is of interest and was positively received in 75% of our
 conversations. Especially the open-source aspect of it was welcomed.

 Figure 83. Ranked topics of interest from stakeholder interviews
 In 87.5% of our conversations, it was agreed that the mirror facet has the most potential for
 improvement and poses the biggest challenges. It was mentioned in 37.5% of comments that a
 single facet is the preferred option. Other things to consider here are thermal performance, low
 cost and robust mounting design, as well as easily tunable/serviceable optics.

 For the design itself, the benefits of modularity were pointed out in 75% of conversations.
 Modularity makes the design adjustable to varying environmental requirements without a total
 redesign. At the same time, the same components can be reused across designs. Different aspects
 of the design can be of use to different entities. Participants supported publication of CAD files,
 a list of Do’s and Don’ts as well as a list of recommended parts.

 Additionally, the importance of long-term durability and survivability testing of components like
 drives and other moving parts, adhesives, mirror backings, insulation, connections etc. was
 stressed in 62.5% of conversations, as this testing helps to reduce the risk of deploying a new
 product. Not only do we need a better understanding of these phenomena, but also a body of data
 that is publicly available. With an open-source approach, testing components for a baseline
 design can help mitigate risks

 62.5% of the participants also pointed out the importance of designing the heliostat for
 manufacturability, scalability, supply chains and environmental conditions. The manufacturing
 process greatly influences the optical quality of the final product. Therefore, quality control was
 indicated as equally important.

 37.5% of participants discussed design loads. There is a need for a better understanding to
 address questions like:

 169

 “How can we avoid overdesigning?”; “What are actual loads?”; “What are dynamic
 responses?”; “How can we be prepared for severe wind events?”; “Can we improve
 field downtime due to conservative safety measures with a better understanding of
 loads?”

 13.2 Proposed Development Plan
 Our recommendation is that the primary focus in terms of reference heliostat be a published set
 of design requirements established in collaboration with HelioCon and U.S. industry. The
 purpose of the documents is to derisk and enable future U.S. heliostat fields. Heliostat
 requirements will be established for two example applications: 10 MW thermal process heat and
 100 MW electrical generation. U.S. industry will include both those focused on CSP and other
 manufacturing industries that have the facilities and expertise for scale production of generalized
 heliostat components. Establishing thorough heliostat design requirement documentation that is
 supported by both HelioCon research and industry experience will collectively improve heliostat
 bankability and support cost reduction. Key documents should include Concept of Operations,
 Stakeholder Requirements Definition, System Requirements Specification, and Functional
 Analysis and Allocation.

 The Concept of Operations sets the foundation in that it defines the overall vision and purpose of
 the heliostat. It outlines how the heliostat will operate within its intended environment (the
 broader electricity or industrial process heat systems). Key aspects include:

 1. System objectives: what are the primary goals for the heliostat (e.g., maximize optical
 efficiency, minimize cost, high reliability, predictable lifetime performance, etc.)?
 2. Use cases: how will the heliostat be used in different scenarios (e.g., power generation,
 direct steam generation, process heating, where - city/desert, greenfield/brownfield,
 what size plant/tower height, etc.)?
 3. Operational environment: temperature ranges, wind loads, dust, humidity, seismic
 activity, city regulations, near airports, migration paths for birds, etc.
 4. Maintenance strategy: frequency, types of maintenance, access requirements, cleaning,
 etc.
 5. Installation strategy: grading or natural terrain, robotic, initial calibration and
 commissioning test, etc.
 6. Security and safety: measures to protect personnel and equipment, bad actors, etc. and
 7. Interactions with other systems: how will it interface with the receiver, control system,
 and power block, factory floor, etc.

 The Stakeholder Requirements Definition involves identifying all individuals, groups, and
 organizations that have an interest in or are affected by the heliostat design. Their needs and
 expectations are gathered and documented. This includes:

 1. Customer requirements: performance, cost, reliability, usable life, dispatchability,
 control, etc.,
 2. Regulatory requirements: environmental, safety, electrical and building codes,

 170

 3. Manufacturing and installation requirements: manufacturability, shipping, assembly,
 supply chain, skills required to install,
 4. Operations and maintenance requirements, and
 5. Disposal / end-of-life requirements.

 In the System Requirements Specification, the information from the Concept of Operations and
 Stakeholder Requirements is translated into a set of precise, measurable, and achievable
 technical requirements captured in the System Requirements Specification. These define what
 the heliostat must do. Examples include:

 1. Optical performance: specular reflectivity, beam quality, tracking accuracy, etc.
 2. Structural integrity: wind load survival, seismic resistance, etc.
 3. Control system: pointing accuracy, response time, communication protocols, etc.
 4. Durability and lifetime: corrosion resistance, expected operational life, etc.
 5. Cost targets: capital expenditure, operational & maintenance expenditure, etc.
 6. Environmental compatibility: material choices, energy consumption for operation,
 interactions with wildlife (birds, desert animals) and undergrowth.

 The Functional Analysis and Allocation document breaks down the overall heliostat system into
 its constituent functions and then allocates these functions to specific hardware or software
 components. One starts by identifying high-level functions such as solar energy collection, flux
 pointing, active tracking, structural support, scram, etc. These functions are then broken into sub-
 functions (e.g., flux pointing mapped to azimuth and elevation control). One then assigns
 functions to physical components (e.g., azimuth control to azimuth drive motor and gearbox,
 elevation control to elevation drive motor and gearbox, etc.). This process leads to efficient
 selection and testing of individual components that will support the overall heliostat
 requirements.

 The engineering steps above are common to many industries (naming varies slightly), where
 each is seeking to minimize the design and prototyping phases needed to achieve the targeted
 product. Much of the work behind these documents is already being conducted through
 HelioCon, IEC standards development, and heliostat developers in their individual designs.
 While the work is being done, however, it is not being accumulated in a single streamlined set of
 documents that all participants can use. For example, research projects by HelioCon and others
 have examined wind loads on heliostat fields but there is not a resulting wind loading
 requirements document. The IEC heliostat design qualification standard provides methods to
 measure optical performance, but it does not specify what optical performance should be for a
 particular heliostat application. By bringing a set of reference heliostat design requirements into
 an open-source publication, the industry will be able to accelerate new design cycles, reduce
 costs, and produce more bankable heliostats.

 A secondary goal, after completing the above reference documents, should be to facilitate the
 transition into a conceptual design phase. Based on the Functional Analysis and Allocation,
 several conceptual designs for the heliostat can be generated and evaluated against the

 171

 requirements. At this phase it is also recommended to evaluate conceptual design of the most
 successfully deployed commercial heliostats, studying what characteristics they had that were
 most beneficial, and what challenges each design faced. This evaluation can help to focus the
 conceptual design effort, incorporating lessons learned to accelerate the selection of a promising
 design. Armed with the Functional Analysis and Allocation and a comprehensive review of
 previous heliostats, a new conceptual design can be created. This involves considering different
 configurations, sizes, materials, and technologies. For example: evaluate the various options for
 mirror facets, structural design, drive mechanisms, control algorithms, and calibration, etc. A
 design matrix can be then used to conduct a cost and risk assessment. All CAD drawings,
 calculations and tools used in this process should be published so that industry can work from the
 reference design to further innovate and reduce cost.

 Throughout this development process, engagement with diverse stakeholders in the CSP industry
 will be critical to success. Building on the stakeholder interviews described above, the reference
 heliostat design team will solicit input and feedback from engineers, operators, manufacturers,
 financiers, and other stakeholders, to ensure a comprehensive set of requirements is developed
 and that the resultant design meets all the needs of a commercially successful heliostat.

 172

 14 System-Level Considerations
 While HelioCon is chartered to develop and advance heliostat technologies, the context within
 which heliostats may be deployed is important and deserves some attention. Indeed, there are
 few applications for which a heliostat field alone could meet an energy need; heliostats are an
 important component of a SHIP or CSP system, working in concert with receivers, thermal
 energy storage systems, heat exchangers, power blocks, and the like. In this section we will
 discuss some very high-level considerations in other areas of SHIP and CSP plants, for context
 along with the heliostat-specific discussions above. In addition, we will highlight some ways in
 which certain system designs can influence, or place requirements on, the heliostat field.

 14.1 Power Generation
 Power generation has historically been the most prevalent application of concentrating solar
 thermal technologies, from the early parabolic trough plants built forty years ago to the modern
 power towers under construction around the world today. When considering modern CSP
 systems, it can be worthwhile to consider both Gen 2 systems using molten salt heat transfer and
 storage fluid coupled with steam Rankine cycles for power generation, as well as Gen 3 systems
 using solid particles for heat transfer and storage coupled with supercritical carbon dioxide
 Brayton cycles.

 14.1.1 Gen 2 CSP Systems
 Gen 2 CSP systems, built upon molten salt technology, represent the most common form of CSP
 being deployed today. This prevalence can be attributed to several factors, including a relatively
 long history of technology development and commercialization, cost effectiveness, and ability to
 be deployed at scales relevant for utility power generation applications. Outside of the heliostat
 field, the noteworthy subsystems of a Gen 2 CSP plant include:

 • Molten salt receiver
 • Thermal energy storage system, in the form of hot and cold salt tanks with associated
 pumps, valves, and piping
 • Molten salt heated steam generator
 • Steam Rankine cycle.

 Early Gen 2 CSP plants have experienced failures and/or performance shortfalls in each of these
 key subsystems, providing valuable cycles of learning to improve during subsequent project
 deployments. While this report will not go into depth in these various failures, some brief
 examples include hot salt tank floor failures leading to extensive repairs and extended
 shutdowns, and slower than anticipated daily power block startup leading to reduced energy
 delivery. For future Gen 2 CSP plant deployments, it is imperative that the project developers
 work with experienced partners who have deployed these technologies before and have
 incorporated the lessons learned into their design standards going forward. It’s also noteworthy
 that for the Gen 2 CSP plants which have experienced operational anomalies, the heliostat field
 is generally not cited as being a significant source of their downtime or performance shortfall.

 173

 14.1.2 Gen 3 CSP Systems
 Gen 3 systems represent the next step forward in CSP technology, using solid particles for heat
 transfer and storage, and supercritical CO2 Brayton cycles for power generation. This
 combination promises CSP plants operating at higher temperatures, which should lead to
 improved energy density, efficiency, and economics. However, this technology is not yet
 commercially mature. Numerous R&D projects around the world have contributed to the
 development of individual component technologies, and very recently projects have begun
 exploring integrated systems at small scale. Similar to the Gen 2 system described above, the
 noteworthy subsystems of a Gen 3 CSP plant include:

 • Particle receiver
 • Thermal energy storage, consisting of hot and cold particle storage silos with associated
 particle transport and handling equipment
 • Particle-to-sCO2 heat exchanger
 • sCO2 Brayton cycle.

 Each of these subsystems is currently less mature than a heliostat field and carries more technical
 and cost risk. Therefore before these systems can be commercially deployed, a scale-up R&D
 effort is necessary for each subsystem, in addition to an integrated system test demonstrating
 how these components can all work together in an operational facility.

 14.1.3 Heliostat Field Interactions With Gen 2 and Gen 3
 When planning development of heliostats and heliostat fields, it’s also important to consider how
 the choice of CSP plant architecture can influence the requirements placed on the heliostat field.
 Table 20 compares heliostat field characteristics for Gen 2 versus Gen 3 plant designs, noting
 that these are generalizations, and that individual plant designs can vary from these descriptions
 for a variety of reasons.

 Table 20. Comparison of heliostat field characteristics for Gen 2 and Gen 3 CSP plant designs

 Factor Gen 2 / Molten Salt Gen 3 / Solid Particles
 Heliostat field arrangement Surround Polar
 Heliostat field size Very large – several hundred Medium – tens of megawatts
 megawatts thermal thermal
 Desired concentration ratio ~750x >1,000x
 Required optical accuracy High Very high
    Slant range to most distant         ~1,000-1,500 m                      ~400-600 m
 heliostat
 Heliostat size <200 m2 <40 m2
 It would be advantageous for upcoming heliostat development efforts to lead to heliostats, fields,
 and control systems capable of meeting the needs of both Gen 2 and Gen 3 CSP plants.

 14.2 Solar Heat for Industrial Processes
 In recent years, there has been significant interest in concentrating solar thermal applications for
 SHIP, not just for power generation. Heliostat fields may provide an attractive solution for

 174

 process heat applications, depending on factors such as the thermal demand, demand profile, the
 temperature, and the form of heat delivery required. These important system-level considerations
 are discussed below.

 14.2.1 Thermal Demand
 Heliostat fields can deliver peak optical powers ranging from tens of megawatts to hundreds of
 megawatts, so it’s important to match a heliostat-based solution with applications within this
 range of power requirements. For example, a beverage bottling facility requiring 500 kW of
 process steam is unlikely to be cost-effectively served with a heliostat field, due to the small size
 of the project, while a mineral processing facility requiring 25 MW of high-temperature heat is
 potentially a better candidate.

 14.2.2 Demand Profile
 When considering a heliostat field as a potential solution for an industrial process heat
 application, it’s also important to scrutinize how the thermal energy consumption of the process
 varies over time – hour to hour, day to day, and seasonally. One key advantage of heliostat fields
 for process heat applications is the cost-effective inclusion of thermal energy storage, however
 the heliostat field, storage, and process heat delivery must all be carefully designed together to
 ensure the most advantageous configuration is selected. In many cases, even with substantial
 thermal energy storage and a relatively large heliostat field, there may still be certain times
 throughout the year when the SHIP system is unable to provide the energy necessary for the
 industrial process. In these cases, a backup energy source is typically required – either
 maintaining the incumbent solution in the case of a plant retrofit or designing in a hybrid heat
 source in the case of a greenfield project.

 14.2.3 Process Temperature
 The applicability and technical risk level of a heliostat field used for industrial process heat are
 strongly related to the process temperature required. One main advantage of heliostat fields over
 other solar concentrators is their ability to produce very high temperatures at meaningfully large
 power levels, so applications requiring these two characteristics will generally be a better fit for
 heliostat fields. As an example, many industrial processes utilize saturated steam at
 approximately 10 bar pressure, implying a process temperature of approximately 185 deg C. At
 this modest temperature, parabolic troughs or linear Fresnel collectors may be a more cost-
 effective solution versus a heliostat field. However, for processes requiring temperatures in
 excess of ~400°C, heliostat fields are generally expected to be a more attractive solution.

 In the longer term, as continued advances are made in Gen 3 CSP technology, heliostat fields
 will likely be capable of addressing process heat applications up to 1,000 deg C and beyond.

 14.2.4 Form of Heat Delivery
 In addition to the considerations above, the feasibility of using a heliostat field for a process heat
 application will be strongly dependent on the form of heat delivery required by the process.
 While steam is a very common method of delivering thermal energy, it is by no means the only
 one. Many processes use hot air or directly heat other process materials (including solids). For
 each case, it’s important to evaluate the technical readiness of a solar receiver, thermal energy
 storage system, and any heat exchangers that might be necessary to deliver the thermal energy, in

 175

 the form required, at the necessary temperature. In many cases this might be accomplished with a
 molten salt receiver and TES, which have been matured through Gen 2 power generation plants,
 but in other cases this might not suffice and alternative technologies will be required.

 176

 15 Conclusion
 Since the HelioCon Roadmap Report was published in 2022, significant progress has been made
 toward closing the technology gaps identified within it. During that same time period, however,
 the U.S. market for CSP has changed substantially, with a decrease in the rate of project
 development and a reduction in the number of domestic technology developers actively
 marketing heliostat-based solutions. To ensure that the work done by HelioCon remains as
 impactful as possible during the remainder of its tenure, this update to the roadmap report has
 evaluated and reprioritized the remaining gaps with the goal of supporting near-term CSP project
 deployment in the United States.

 This update intends to define the ongoing priorities for HelioCon and should serve as a basis for
 research plans for future fiscal years. In addition, the authors hope that the report will provide a
 valuable reference to those developing CSP technologies outside and beyond HelioCon, helping
 create alignment on the most important topics necessary to support the commercialization of
 CSP.

 15.1 Summary of Priorities
 As discussed throughout this document, HelioCon aims to focus its future efforts on eliminating
 commercial barriers that prevent heliostat field deployments and reducing uncertainty around the
 cost, performance, and reliability of heliostat fields. Input from a broad spectrum of CSP experts
 and HelioCon stakeholders has indicated that this emphasis will be most beneficial to the CSP
 industry in the United States.

 Upon close inspection of the updated gaps and priorities identified in each topic area throughout
 this report, three broad priorities stand out:

 1. Improve standardization across the entire project life cycle and among all stakeholders.
 2. Resolve historical gaps between projected and realized plant performance.
 3. Focus publication efforts not only on scientific journals, but also on practical guidance
 for industry.

 Improved standardization is expected to expedite project development and financing—for
 example, through the use of common, validated performance models for heliostat fields and CSP
 plants. Standardization is also expected to reduce the risk (both real and perceived) associated
 with heliostat performance and reliability, through development of standard testing,
 measurement, and reporting techniques.

 Resolving historical gaps between projected and realized plant performance is a multifaceted
 effort that includes development and validation of improved models, assessment of historical
 performance shortfalls, and rationalization of the two. To the extent the industry can assess both
 the historical performance of CSP plants and the realistically achievable performance using
 today’s state-of-the-art technology, the risk of underperformance will be reduced, and CSP
 projects should become more economically financeable.

 177

 To maximize the impact of HelioCon’s work within the CSP industry, not solely within the
 research community, increased priority will be placed on publishing practical guidelines for
 implementing the technologies developed under HelioCon, rather than exclusively publishing
 scientific journal articles. There have historically been many examples where a large body of
 high-quality research work has been done, but with the emphasis being on siloed scientific
 journal publications. Thus, it has not been straightforward for a commercial CSP technology or
 project developer to put that work into practice. HelioCon aims to resolve this challenge by
 publishing guidelines and “how-to” documentation aimed at industrial companies actively
 working to commercialize CSP technologies, in addition to the scientific articles with a rigorous
 treatment of the research work.
